# koto security assessment — 2026-09-11 (Aikido)

| | |
|---|---|
| **Source** | Aikido automated security assessment, exported as CSV. The export is not committed, since it carries full reproduction steps. |
| **Findings** | 339: 2 high, 166 medium, 171 low. |
| **Export status column** | Meaningless. All 339 rows say `open` with no `closed_at`: the export predates the triage and was never synced back. This document is the status of record. |
| **Triage** | `docs/history/SECURITY_11_SEP.md`, the per-finding ledger this file is compiled from. Work ran from `2bb704d` to `ea6e113`, 144 commits, all on 2026-09-11. |
| **Tests** | `daemon/audit_fixes_test.go` pins the fixes, plus the tests named in individual entries. |
| **Compiled** | 2026-09-13. Each CSV row was matched to its ledger entry by title; one row had no entry (U1 below). |

## Numbering

IDs are the ledger's, so commit messages and code comments (`audit 2026-09-11 L<n>`) resolve here. **H1–H2, M1–M14 and L1–L12 collide with the 2026-09-04 audit** (`audit/2026_09_04_Fable_51.md`); this file's date disambiguates them.

- `H1`–`H2` and `L1`–`L171` are the nth high and low rows of the export, in file order.
- Medium IDs carry an off-by-one from triage. `M1` is unused, `M2`–`M130` are medium rows 1–129 and `M131`–`M166` are medium rows 131–166. **Medium row 130 was never assigned an ID or a verdict**, so it appears here as **U1**.

## Outcome

| outcome | high | medium | low | total |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| fixed | 1 | 129 | 148 | 278 |
| fixed, part declined | 0 | 8 | 2 | 10 |
| partly fixed | 0 | 4 | 3 | 7 |
| already fixed (duplicate) | 0 | 5 | 15 | 20 |
| accepted | 1 | 7 | 2 | 10 |
| not a finding | 0 | 12 | 1 | 13 |
| open | 0 | 1 | 0 | 1 |
| **total** | 2 | 166 | 171 | 339 |

- **fixed**: code changed, and in nearly every case pinned by a test.
- **fixed, part declined**: the reachable defect is fixed; part of the suggested remediation is declined with reasons, or a residual is named.
- **partly fixed**: a real part stays open by decision or is bounded by another mechanism.
- **already fixed (duplicate)**: closed by the finding named in its verdict.
- **accepted**: real, but the risk is the trust model's stated position.
- **not a finding**: the precondition does not exist, or the claim is wrong.
- **open**: not triaged.

## Headline

**308 of 339 findings are closed in code** (278 fixed outright, 10 fixed with part of the remediation declined, 20 closed by another finding). Both highs were triaged first. H1 (a `workspace.img` symlink redirecting VM disk operations) is fixed; H2 (a jailed VMM impersonating its guest on vsock) is accepted as unclosable and not an escalation.

Most of the volume is one shape repeated across the daemon, guest agent and TUI: guest-controlled input with no bound on memory, disk, goroutines or render work; lifecycle races across stop, clear, destroy and same-name recreation; and guest bytes reaching an operator terminal. Those are fixed.

What stays open is a set of decisions, not a backlog. Four themes account for almost all of it:

1. **The group is the isolation boundary, not the session** (M4, M28, M45, M53, M106, M107). Findings that asked for per-session authorization inside one VM were answered the same way each time.
2. **Tier 1 is trusted by definition** (M5, M8, M43, M52, M77, M128, M132). An attacker who can write the operator's PATH or state dir already runs as the operator.
3. **Unattended agents are the product** (M9, M12, M129). Skipping permission prompts, delegation reports and inference spend are bounded rather than gated.
4. **Named residuals left with the operator:** the claude system prompt on argv (M58/M107, postponed), a guest under-reporting its own telemetry (L79), stuck gRPC handler goroutines bounded only by stream admission (M154/L31), and RPM pinning of the rootfs (M46).

One finding was never triaged. **U1** (stale shell pane accepts input after a group switch, `tui/mouse.go`) has no ledger entry, and the click-to-focus path still lacks the `shellOnTarget()` check (verified 2026-09-13). It is the only open item.

## Not fixed, or not fully fixed

| ID | sev | finding | outcome | decision |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| U1 | medium | Stale shell pane accepts input after group selection | **open** | No ledger entry. `handleLeftClick` focuses the shell pane on geometry alone, without `shellOnTarget()`, so during the retarget delay after a group switch, clicks, keys and pastes go to the previous group's shell. |
| H2 | high | Jailed Firecracker can impersonate its guest on the privileged host control plane | accepted | The VMM is the vsock peer by construction, so no check can tell it from its guest; the channel grants only what that guest already holds, and the jail still keeps a VMM escape away from credentials, network and other groups. |
| M4 | medium | AttachShell permits cross-session tmux attachment within an authorized group | fixed (hygiene), claim partly rejected | The tmux selector is pinned to koto's namespace. Per-session isolation is rejected: the group is the boundary, and `attach_shell` already yields a shell that reaches every session in the VM. |
| M5 | medium | Security-sensitive helpers are resolved from an untrusted PATH | not a finding | Anyone who can write the operator's PATH is already tier 1 and runs as the operator on their next shell command. |
| M8 | medium | `koto tui` executes an attacker-selected executable with administrator credentials | not a finding | Same premise as M5. The one tier-2→tier-1 variant (a daemon-writable `koto-tui` fallback) was already removed by the 2026-09-04 audit's L10. |
| M9 | medium | Peer reports are promoted into MAIN's executable conversation | accepted | Reports are solicited, one-shot, sanitized and framed as data, not instructions. The daemon can't know which of main's later actions derive from a report, and gating them on the operator would end unattended orchestration. |
| M12 | medium | Claude worker permanently bypasses all permission checks | accepted | `--dangerously-skip-permissions` is the product. The boundary is KVM plus the `network`/`root` profiles, which default closed and are admin-only (M7). |
| M20 | medium | Main-to-peer goals can bypass the mandatory human-approval gate | fixed, partly rejected | Main→peer goals over ctl are forced plan-first. `plan=false` over gRPC is kept, because that caller is the operator the gate exists to involve. |
| M24 | medium | Recursive shared root mount allows worker mount events to propagate into fc-agent | accepted | Rootless podman in the guest needs a shared `/`. Every consumer of the hole was closed by M23/M13, and narrowing the mount without a booted test would risk breaking guest containers. |
| M28 | medium | Group-scoped Send authorization permits cross-session conversation hijacking | not a finding | Sessions are conversations, not tenants. The ACL boundary is the group. |
| M41 | medium | IPv4-mapped IPv6 loopback destinations bypass the host control-plane filter | not a finding (pinned by a test) | Premise wrong: `net.IP.IsLoopback` already classifies mapped IPv4. Pinned by `TestMappedIPv4DestinationsClassifyAsIPv4`. |
| M43 | medium | Installer executes a PATH-resolved `git` after privileged operations | not a finding | PATH-resolved `git`: the same tier-1 premise as M5/M8. |
| M45 | medium | Named Claude session state is readable and writable by every worker | not a finding | Every worker in a group shares a uid, a VM and `/workspace` by design. Workloads that need isolating go in separate groups. |
| M46 | medium | Mutable package resolution can poison the shared golden rootfs during a root build | partly fixed | `@anthropic-ai/claude-code` is pinned (2.1.268). Pinning RPM versions is declined (Fedora has no snapshot repo, and dnf already checks signatures), as is `--ignore-scripts` (claude-code needs its lifecycle scripts). |
| M52 | medium | Symlinked group state escapes the trusted state root during VM staging | not a finding (with one part already fixed) | Every sink needs write access to the operator-owned state tree (tier 1). The one non-tier-1 part is H1, fixed. |
| M53 | medium | Unbound guest turn streams let a compromised guest spoof turn lifecycle | accepted | A guest can only forge its own group's transcript, which it authors anyway. Slot binding was already closed (2026-09-04 L4); a per-turn capability would be a proto change with no second principal to protect. |
| M57 | medium | Non-admin ACL grants can stop the protected main orchestrator | not a finding | `stop: ["main"]` in acl.json is the operator's explicit grant. Stop is reversible, and `destroy` still refuses `main`. |
| M58 | medium | Prompt data is inherited by Venice workspace commands | fixed (with a named residual) | The prompt variables are removed from the Venice tool environment. **Residual, postponed by the operator:** the claude path still passes the system prompt on argv (`/proc/<pid>/cmdline`) until the CLI accepts it from a file or stdin. |
| M65 | medium | Group-only report capability accepts stale or canceled delegation results | partly fixed, partly rejected | `stop` now disarms the report window, and the destroy race was already closed by M63. The per-delegation nonce is rejected: a token that an LLM must echo back is not a capability. |
| M72 | medium | Credential and private-key files retain or inherit non-private permissions | fixed (the migration path); the rest declined with reasons | Migrated credentials are forced to 0600 and `creds/` to 0700. Declined: per-read owner/mode validation (a check before a use, against a tier-1 attacker) and `fchmod` on files freshly created at 0600. |
| M77 | medium | Installer accepts attacker-controlled descendant symlinks in the state directory | not a finding | M52 by another route. |
| M80 | medium | Authorized job-observability RPCs permit unbounded guest-agent execution | fixed (concurrency); context propagation declined | Same-group queries are deduplicated and there is a global cap of 16. Threading the RPC context into guest calls is declined; the calls are already time-bounded. |
| M83 | medium | Detached jobs can outlive turns and exhaust persistent workspace resources | partly fixed, partly by design | `cs-job rm` kills the job's process group before forgetting it. A daemon-owned job supervisor is declined: the microVM is the supervision boundary. |
| M97 | medium | ctl ask can substitute another session's response | fixed for the cross-session case; the same-session case named | `ctl ask` now filters by session. Named residual: two identical back-to-back prompts in the same session can't be told apart without a proto-level turn id. |
| M103 | medium | Unbounded guest-to-egress proxy tunnels enable shared resource exhaustion | fixed (admission); idle timeouts declined | Egress tunnels are admission-capped at 64 per group and 512 globally. Idle deadlines and a maximum lifetime are declined because they would kill legitimate ssh, websocket and long-poll connections. |
| M105 | medium | Tool transcript renders unredacted tool arguments | not a finding | The transcript is the audit log. Everyone who can read it can already send to the group, attach its shell and read its workspace. |
| M106 | medium | Group-scoped interrupt authorization permits cross-session turn cancellation | not a finding | Sessions are not tenants (M4/M28/M45). The real bug here was a race, and that is M85, fixed. |
| M107 | medium | Sensitive prompts are persisted in job command metadata | accepted | Group-scoped readers already hold more access. Argv persistence is tracked with M58's postponed residual. |
| M111 | medium | Untrusted TUI content can inject terminal control sequences | fixed for the reachable class; the blanket render-boundary sanitizer declined | Error lines are scrubbed at `addLine`, and every reachable producer was fixed at the source. A whole-frame sanitizer is declined because it would silently mangle glamour/lipgloss output when those libraries are upgraded. |
| M128 | medium | State directory validation is not bound to subsequent writes | not a finding (M52/M77/M112 again) | M52/M77 again. The one case with a real consequence (`--purge`) is fixed by M112. |
| M129 | medium | Guest-controlled Venice proxy lacks per-group spend or request-rate enforcement | accepted | Spending quota on inference is the feature, bounded by concurrency, the body budget and the endpoint allowlist. A per-group spend budget is recorded as a product decision for the operator. |
| M132 | medium | Daemon can read and replace the TUI's administrator credentials | accepted, no change | The TUI credential only authenticates to the daemon, which is already the authority, so stealing it gains nothing. Narrowing it (TUI's own uid and role, minimal creds) remains the recorded cs_tui open item. |
| M154 | medium | SubscribeGroup can retain streams indefinitely after subscriber shutdown | partly fixed, with the limit stated | Shut subscribers are now deregistered and drained. A goroutine stuck in `Send` can't be reclaimed within grpc-go; `streamAdmit` (M95) bounds how many there can be. `MaxConnectionAge` was rejected. |
| L7 | low | Quadratic work when rendering newline-dense guest text frames | not A finding | Premise wrong: the scan is already a linear reslice. Pinned by `TestNewlineScanShapeIsLinear`. |
| L19 | low | Unredacted tool arguments are persisted in group logs and replayed to transcript readers | accepted | The transcript is the record of what the agent did, and no rule for which tool arguments are secret is tractable. The audience is bounded by file modes and the ACL. |
| L31 | low | AttachShell retains handlers and vsock resources after guest termination | partly fixed, with the limit stated | The guest connection is now closed when forwarding ends. A stuck handler goroutine is bounded by `streamAdmit` (M95). |
| L49 | low | Goal handoff capture replays and sorts every active log stream | accepted | Reading one session's events without replaying every stream needs session-indexed transcript storage, which is a design change. Each read is already bounded by the per-stream caps. |
| L66 | low | Unbounded schedule listing can exhaust daemon resources | already fixed (M87), residual accepted | The store is bounded by `schedMaxTotal`=2000 (M87). An unfiltered listing of at most 2000 schedules is accepted, since silently truncating it would hide schedules that will fire. |
| L79 | low | Guest-controlled resource probe can forge per-group disk and memory telemetry | fixed (with a stated residual) | Out-of-envelope guest telemetry is dropped. **Residual:** a guest can under-report its own usage and suppress its own disk alert; host-side subjects are unaffected. |
| L138 | low | Reserved goal-session LLM calls contaminate group activity state | mostly fixed by L115, residual accepted | The visible bug is closed by L115. The residual (group-wide `llm` phase attribution) is accepted because the proxy receives no session id. |
| L151 | low | Unbounded subagent buffers and detached-job output allow tenant-scoped resource exhaustion | fixed (persisted job output accepted) | In-memory buffers are bounded. A detached job's output file is bounded only by the workspace size preset and the disk alert. |

---

## Findings and decisions

Each entry pairs Aikido's finding (summary and preconditions, from the export) with the triage decision from the ledger, reproduced unabridged. Aikido's full descriptions, root-cause analyses, reproduction steps and suggested remediations are in the export only.

## HIGH

### H1. Legacy workspace.img symlink enables cross-boundary VM disk redirection (HIGH, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/fc.go`
- **Finding:** The Firecracker migration path accepts a workload-controlled workspace.img symlink because it validates the target with os.Stat. Subsequent truncation, filesystem checks, ownership changes, and bind mounting reuse the unresolved path, allowing the workload to redirect VM disk operations to another daemon-visible file.
- **Preconditions:** A workload user must be able to write a legacy podman-era group's workspace and cause that group to enter the Firecracker ensure or migration path. Creating workspace.img as a symlink is sufficient; no path traversal or additional host privilege is required. The target must be visible and accessible to the daemon for the redirected operation to succeed.
- **Commit:** `2bb704d`

**Decision.**

`fcEnsureWorkspaceImg` accepted the image with `os.Stat`, which follows
symlinks, and every consumer downstream works on the PATH: `os.Truncate` +
`e2fsck` + `resize2fs` in the grow path, `os.Chown` in `fcJailFixupPerms`, and
the jail shim's bind mount, which hands the resolved inode to the guest as a
read-write block device — where the guest's mount fallback (`mkfs.ext4 -F` on
an unmountable `/dev/vdb`) would format it.

Planting the symlink needs write access to `groups/<g>/`, which under
Firecracker is tier 1 (there is no shared filesystem). The one exception is
the case the finding names: a **podman-era** workspace, which the migration
path deliberately treats as pre-existing untrusted input, and which a podman
sidecar had RW.

Fixed by `os.Lstat` + a regular-file check before anything touches the path.
Refusing the whole class beats reasoning about who wrote it.
`TestWorkspaceImgRefusesNonRegularFile`.

### H2. Jailed Firecracker can impersonate its guest on the privileged host control plane (HIGH, accepted)

- **Where:** `daemon/fc.go`
- **Finding:** The jailed Firecracker/VMM process can connect to the host's guest-to-host control listener and issue requests authorized solely by the listener's group, without authenticating the peer or request origin.
- **Preconditions:** Requires code execution in a running jailed Firecracker/VMM process, but once obtained the attacker can directly reach the bind-mounted Unix control listener and needs no additional host credential or secret. The request framing and protobuf encoding are straightforward, and the listener path is predictable inside the jail.
- **Commit:** `2bb704d`

**Decision.**

True as stated, unclosable, and not an escalation.

Firecracker's hybrid vsock makes every guest→host connection a `connect(2)`
**by the VMM process** to `<uds>_<port>` (see the channel map at the top of
`fc.go`). The VMM is definitionally the peer, so `SO_PEERCRED` cannot
distinguish "VMM relaying its guest" from "VMM acting alone", and a per-VM MAC
would have to be handed to the guest through that same VMM.

What the channel grants is exactly the authority the group's own guest already
holds — and a VMM compromise is reached *through* that guest, via a
virtio/vsock device-model bug, so the attacker held it already. The jailer's
actual claim is narrower and still holds: a VMM escape reaches no credentials,
no network, no other group's sockets, and no host filesystem outside its
chroot. Recorded in the comment at `fcJailFixupPerms`.

## MEDIUM

### M2. Unauthenticated local vsock peers can invoke the guest agent as root (MEDIUM, fixed (defense in depth))

- **Where:** `fcguest/main.go`
- **Finding:** When local AF_VSOCK transport is available, an unprivileged process in the guest can connect to the guest agent's wildcard-bound port without an application-level authentication check and invoke root-only operations, including shell command execution and VM shutdown.
- **Preconditions:** A local guest process needs local AF_VSOCK transport and access to CID_LOCAL:10000. Once connected, no credentials or application-level handshake are required; the attacker only needs to encode the documented protobuf frame. The host-vsock path may have external socket permissions or topology restrictions, but the guest agent itself does not authenticate the peer.
- **Commit:** `850e799`

**Decision.**

Already mitigated, and the mitigation is load-bearing enough to deserve a
second layer. `fc-agent` binds `VMADDR_CID_ANY` and its `Exec`/`ExecStream`/
`ShellAttach`/`Init`/`Shutdown` ops run as PID 1, i.e. guest root — so anything
in-guest that could reach vsock would defeat the `root=no` profile outright.
Nothing can: `build-kernel.sh` refuses to produce a kernel with
`CONFIG_VSOCKETS_LOOPBACK=y` or `CONFIG_VHOST_VSOCK=y` (audit I1).

But that is a property of an ASSET — `fcassets/vmlinux` — which an operator can
replace, and the agent itself took the peer on faith. `vsockAcceptLoop` now
keeps the `SockaddrVM` that `accept` returns and refuses any peer whose CID is
not `VMADDR_CID_HOST`. The kernel guard stays the primary defense; this makes
it a backstop rather than the only one.

A per-VM shared secret was the other half of the suggested remediation and is
deliberately not taken: the secret would have to reach the guest over the same
vsock channel it is meant to protect, so it buys nothing against a peer that
can already speak on that channel.

### M3. DNS rebinding bypasses the HTTP egress destination policy (MEDIUM, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/proxy.go`
- **Finding:** The proxy performs destination authorization using one DNS resolution, but the plain-HTTP forwarding path discards the authorized address and lets the default HTTP transport resolve the original hostname again. An attacker-controlled DNS name can therefore resolve to an allowed WAN address during validation and to a forbidden private, loopback, link-local, or host-reachable address when the daemon connects.
- **Preconditions:** Requires code execution in a guest with a network profile that permits egress (for example WAN) and control of a hostname's DNS responses. The attacker sends a plain HTTP absolute-form request; the DNS service returns an allowed address for authorization and a restricted address for the subsequent transport lookup. No host authentication bypass is required once the guest is running.
- **Commit:** `7d20d7e`

**Decision.**

`egressConnect` pinned its dial to the vetted IP; `egressHTTP` did not — it
handed the original URL to a shared `http.Client`, whose transport re-resolved
the name inside `Do()`. The comment there acknowledged the window and deferred
it on the grounds that plain-HTTP egress is rare. It is still a wide window,
because the daemon's own dial is not behind the guest's L3 frame filter: a
guest controlling DNS for a name it is allowed to reach could steer the
DAEMON's connection at a destination its profile forbids.

Fixed with a per-request `http.Transport` whose `DialContext` ignores the
address the transport resolved and dials the vetted `ip:port`. The Host header
still rides `r.URL`, so the origin sees the name it was asked for. The dial
guard `egressConnect` already had (never dial the control plane, whatever the
check said) is applied on this path too, and `serveEgress` now defaults a
missing port from the URL scheme so the vetted address is a complete `ip:port`.
`TestEgressHTTPDialsVettedIP`.

### M4. AttachShell permits cross-session tmux attachment within an authorized group (MEDIUM, fixed (hygiene), claim partly rejected)

- **Where:** `daemon/grpc_server.go`
- **Finding:** AttachShell authorizes only the group and forwards the client-controlled session selector directly to tmux create-or-attach. A caller with group-level attach permission can therefore join other conversation shells or reserved goal worker/judge shells, bypassing the session validation and reserved-session policy used by other ingress paths.
- **Preconditions:** An authenticated caller with attach_shell permission for the target group needs a known or inferable session name, which can be obtained from session/goal metadata or the documented naming convention. One opening frame selects the target, after which the caller receives PTY output and can send input; no additional administrative privilege or victim interaction is required. A newline-bearing selector can separately demonstrate the log-forgery consequence.
- **Commit:** `850e799`

**Decision.**

`open.Session` went to the guest unvalidated and became `tmux new-session -A -s
<value>`. Now pinned to `shellSessionRE` — `koto-shell` plus an optional
`-<chat session>`, which is exactly the namespace `shellSessionName` (TUI) and
`turn.go` (guest) mint. That closes the two real consequences: an arbitrary
selector created an unmanaged tmux session outside the group's namespace, and a
newline in it forged daemon log records.

The access-control framing does not hold. `attach_shell` on a group yields an
interactive shell as the guest worker uid, from which `tmux attach -t` reaches
every session in that VM — so restricting the selector cannot be the boundary
between conversations, and nothing here claims it is. Cross-conversation
isolation inside one group is a VM-level property koto does not offer; the
boundary is the group.

### M5 / M8. Triaged together (MEDIUM)

- **M5. Security-sensitive helpers are resolved from an untrusted PATH** (`daemon/install.go`), not a finding. Installation, setup preflight, and OAuth login execute helpers selected from the inherited PATH without verifying that the selected files are trusted. The affected sinks include bare `sudo`, PATH-selected `docker`/`podman`, and the unqualified `claude` OAuth command.
- **M8. `koto tui` executes an attacker-selected executable with administrator credentials** (`daemon/tui_cmd.go`), not a finding. The TUI launcher trusts the first `koto-tui` resolved through the caller-controlled `PATH`, and its fallback trusts a current-directory executable based only on attacker-creatable `go.work` and `daemon` marker files. The selected process receives the TUI bearer token and paths to the TUI client certificate and private key.
- **Commit:** `52b46d8`

**Decision.**

Both reduce to "an attacker can write to a directory on the operator's PATH".
That attacker already executes as the operator on their next shell command —
`ls`, `git`, `make` — with no koto involved. koto's trust model names the host
user as tier 1 by definition; a defense here would protect nothing, because the
thing it protects runs as the principal it is defending against.

The one part of M8 with a distinct shape — resolving `koto-tui` from somewhere
the DAEMON can write, which is a tier-2→tier-1 crossing rather than a tier-1
one — was found and closed by the previous audit (L10, 2026-09-06): the
`<state>/koto-tui` fallback is gone, and the surviving dev-clone convenience
requires a cwd carrying a koto checkout's markers, which `ProtectSystem=strict`
keeps the daemon out of. The rationale is in the comment at `tuiMain`.

### M6. IPv6 AWS metadata endpoint bypasses the guest egress filter (MEDIUM, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/fcnet.go`
- **Finding:** The frame-layer policy classifies all IPv6 ULA destinations, including AWS's IPv6 IMDS address `fd00:ec2::254`, as LAN traffic. `lan` and `full` guests can therefore send traffic to IPv6 IMDS, while `Ec2MetadataAccess: false` does not explicitly deny this host-routed destination.
- **Preconditions:** Requires code execution in a `lan` or `full` guest, with the root-enabled profile making IPv6 configuration or traffic generation practical. It also requires an AWS dual-stack host with IPv6 IMDS enabled. No additional authentication to IMDS is needed beyond the normal IMDSv2 token exchange once the guest-to-forwarder path is reachable.
- **Commit:** `7d20d7e`

**Decision.**

`169.254.169.254` — the metadata endpoint for AWS, GCP, Azure and most others
— is link-local, so `fcClassifyDst` already returned `fcDstCtl` for it. AWS's
IPv6 IMDS is `fd00:ec2::254`, a ULA, which `IsPrivate()` classes as LAN: a
`lan` or `full` guest on a dual-stack host could read the instance's role
credentials. `Ec2MetadataAccess: false` does not cover it — that governs the
netstack's own answer, not a host-routed dial.

Classified as control plane, so it is denied under every profile, on both the
frame filter and the L7 proxy. `TestIPv6MetadataIsControlPlane`.

### M7. Group-scoped Config permission bypasses field-level posture authorization (MEDIUM, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/grpc_server.go`
- **Finding:** The authenticated gRPC Config RPC checks only the coarse group-scoped `config` permission, then forwards every optional ConfigReq setter to the shared configuration writer. A delegated non-admin can therefore modify operator-only posture settings such as network access, guest root, published ports, VM size, and autostart.
- **Preconditions:** Requires an authenticated client certificate/token identity that an operator has granted the target group's coarse `config` permission. No admin role, race, or malformed payload is required; a normal setter-bearing Config request is sufficient. The supported ACL model permits delegated group-scoped grants, which is where the missing field-level authorization is exploitable.
- **Commit:** `850e799`

**Decision.**

Real, and the reasoning already existed on the other plane. `ConfigReq` carries
the delegable settings (`model`, `effort`, `provider`) and the posture keys
(`network`/`internet`, `root`, `ports`, `size`, `autostart`) in one message,
and the interceptor did one coarse `config` check against the group. So a role
granted "may set this group's model" could also give the group WAN egress,
passwordless guest sudo, a published host port, more of the host's RAM, or a
boot at daemon start.

`postureVerb` (auth.go) re-labels a posture-setting request as the synthetic
verb `config_posture`, which is in `adminOnlyVerbs` — so no acl.json grant
reaches it, `"*"` included. Reads and delegable-key writes stay `config`. This
is the gRPC twin of the ctl plane's posture refusal (audit H1, 2026-09-05);
the invariant is the same one, that `network=none` must not be voidable by
anything the default contains. `TestConfigPostureIsAdminOnly`.

### M9. Peer reports are promoted into MAIN's executable conversation (MEDIUM, accepted)

- **Where:** `daemon/report.go`
- **Finding:** A non-main peer can place arbitrary natural-language instructions into MAIN's normal action-capable model turn after a legitimate `reply:true` delegation. The quote prefix and warning are model-readable text, not an enforceable trust boundary, so an attacker-controlled or prompt-injected peer can attempt to make MAIN invoke privileged cross-group control-plane capabilities.
- **Preconditions:** A non-main peer needs a valid MAIN-originated `reply:true` delegation, which is expected during normal operation. It can then submit arbitrary report text through its own control FIFO. The report is delivered to MAIN's ordinary action-capable model context. A privileged action still depends on MAIN's model interpreting and following the injected instructions, but no daemon-side check prevents the report from influencing those instructions.
- **Commit:** `2f1cec8`

**Decision.**

Correctly described and already as bounded as the feature allows. A peer cannot
speak to main at all unless main first delegated with `reply:true`; the window
is one-shot, consumed on delivery and expiring in 24h, so a group can push at
most one turn into main per turn main pushed into it. The body is sanitized,
truncated, `> `-quoted, and prefixed and suffixed with an explicit frame:
peer-authored, findings not instructions, no spawn/stop/config/send/goal/sched
action because the report asked, and "the operator does not speak through
peers".

What the remediation asks for beyond that is not implementable here. "Enforce
authorization in the daemon for actions derived from peer data" requires the
daemon to know which of main's later actions derive from the report, which it
cannot. "Require operator confirmation before MAIN-derived actions affect other
groups" would end unattended orchestration, which is the product. And a
"distinct typed low-authority event" is what the framing above already is at
the only layer that exists — the model reads text either way.

A compliant model acting as a confused deputy for its delegate is a property of
delegation, not of this delivery path. The mitigations that DO apply are the
ones already in place: the solicited one-shot window, the rate bound it
implies, and the fact that a peer's own reach is `network=none` by default.

### M10. Independent DNS lookups bypass the boot-time egress profile (MEDIUM, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/proxy.go`
- **Finding:** The CONNECT authorization checks the live and boot network policies using separate DNS resolutions, but dials the address retained from only the live-policy check. A DNS-rebinding or answer-rotation response can make both checks approve while the connected address is disallowed by the boot-time profile.
- **Preconditions:** An attacker needs code execution in a running guest group and control of a DNS name resolved by the daemon, or a DNS service capable of returning different answers on successive lookups. The attack requires no host authentication beyond access to that guest's proxy path and can be triggered with one CONNECT request.
- **Commit:** `7d20d7e`

**Decision.**

`serveEgress` called `egressTargetAllowed` twice — once for the config profile,
once for the booted one — and kept the vetted IP from the first. Each call did
its own `egressLookupIP`, so a rebinding name answering LAN then WAN passed
`full` on lookup one and `wan` on lookup two while the dial went to the LAN
address the booted profile forbids.

Fixed by intersecting the two profiles first (`egressIntersect`) and checking
once, which also makes "the stricter of the two" a property of the value rather
than of the call sequence. `wan ∩ lan` is `none`, which is correct: a group
that booted `wan` and now reads `lan` reaches nothing until the `/restart`.
`TestEgressProfilesIntersectOnOneLookup`.

### M11. Unvalidated cs-job IDs enable path traversal outside the jobs directory (MEDIUM, fixed (hygiene))

- **Where:** `sidecar/cs-job`
- **Finding:** The guest-side `cs-job` utility concatenates caller-supplied IDs into filesystem paths without validating the generated-ID grammar or enforcing containment. For example, `cs-job rm ../..` resolves from `/workspace/.cs/jobs` to `/workspace` and recursively deletes it; the other ID-based commands likewise expose read, write, and file-removal traversal primitives.
- **Preconditions:** An attacker needs execution of a worker/sidecar command or influence over an agent that can invoke `cs-job`; no additional filesystem privilege beyond the worker's existing workspace access is required. A simple argument such as `..` or `../..` is sufficient, and shell quoting does not stop traversal.
- **Commit:** `52b46d8`

**Decision.**

Correct as a bug, not as a boundary: `cs-job` runs as the guest worker in the
guest's own workspace, so `cs-job rm ../..` destroys exactly what `rm -rf
/workspace` already would. Fixed anyway, because no verb here has business
resolving outside `$JOBS`, and the realistic trigger is a model repeating a
malformed id back at itself rather than an adversary.

`chkid` rejects anything but a bare alphanumeric before the path is built, on
`_notify`, `wait`, `peek`, `status`, `logs` and `rm`. It is called as a
STATEMENT, never inside `$(...)`: a command substitution runs in a subshell, so
an `exit` there ends only the subshell and the caller proceeds with an empty id
— which for `rm -rf "$JOBS/$id"` is worse than the traversal. (Written the
wrong way first; the smoke test caught it.)

### M12. Claude worker permanently bypasses all permission checks (MEDIUM, accepted)

- **Where:** `fcguest/turn.go`
- **Finding:** Every non-interactive Claude turn is launched with `--dangerously-skip-permissions`, removing the normal approval gate for model-directed tool actions.
- **Preconditions:** An attacker needs an authenticated identity permitted to send a turn to the target group, or an existing path to place model-visible content in that group's workspace. They can then provide a crafted turn or prompt-injection content; Claude may execute resulting high-impact tools without an approval interaction. Exploitation does not require host access, and the guest VM limits the direct blast radius to the group guest and its configured network.
- **Commit:** `52b46d8`

**Decision.**

`--dangerously-skip-permissions` is not an oversight, it is the product. koto
runs unattended agents; an approval prompt with nobody to answer it is a hang,
and the flag is why every group is a microVM with no NIC by default rather than
a process on the host. The boundary is KVM plus the `network` and `root`
profiles, not the model's own tool gate — which is exactly why those two
profiles default closed and why posture is admin-only (M7).

The workspace-destruction consequence is the group's own workspace, which the
agent is supposed to own. The remediation's "run workers in least-privilege
disposable workspaces, keep network access disabled by default" is a
description of what koto already does.

### M13. Root agent follows worker-controlled temporary-file symlinks (MEDIUM, fixed)

- **Where:** `fcguest/main.go`
- **Finding:** `writeWorkerFile` creates a predictable `<path>.tmp` beneath directories owned by uid 1000, then performs path-based `os.WriteFile` and `os.Chown`. A worker can pre-create a symlink at that name and redirect the root agent's write and ownership operation to another guest path.
- **Preconditions:** A worker with normal uid-1000 command execution can create the predictable symlink before the next message or session event. No timing race is required if the link is prepared in advance; the normal root-agent workflow triggers the privileged sink.
- **Commit:** `52b46d8`, `c93e829`

**Decision.**

Real, and it crosses the one boundary inside the guest that `root=no` claims to
hold. `writeWorkerFile` wrote `path + ".tmp"` with `os.WriteFile` (which follows
symlinks) and then `os.Chown`ed it (which also follows), under directories that
are chowned to uid 1000 — so the worker could pre-create the predictable
temporary name as a symlink, have PID 1 truncate an arbitrary root-owned guest
file, and then take ownership of it.

Now `O_CREAT|O_EXCL|O_NOFOLLOW` — which refuses an existing entry of any kind,
closing the race rather than sampling it — plus `fchown` on the descriptor
rather than on a name that can change underneath. The `os.Rename` needed no
change: it replaces `path`, it does not follow it.

### M14. Destroyed groups retain executable schedules and can resurrect a reused group (MEDIUM, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/groups.go`
- **Finding:** Destroying a group removes its registry entry and workspace but leaves enabled schedules persisted by reusable group name. The scheduler later fires the orphaned record, queues its message, and sendNow calls ensure(), recreating the deleted group. If the name is reused, the replacement group can inherit visibility and execution of stale schedules.
- **Preconditions:** An authenticated non-main group agent can create a recurring self-schedule through its in-guest control interface. The operator's later destroy action supplies the lifecycle transition; the cron loop fires automatically on a later due tick, requiring no host filesystem access or further action by the former tenant. Reuse of the group name exposes the additional cross-tenant path, while resurrection works even without name reuse if the scheduler retains the record long enough to fire after deletion.
- **Commit:** `52b46d8`

**Decision.**

Real, and not only as a security matter: `destroy()` cancelled goals and
disarmed the report window but left schedules alone, and a schedule fire is an
`enqueueSend` whose `sendNow` calls `ensure()` — so a destroyed group's cron
line rebuilt its VM and a blank workspace minutes after the operator deleted
it, and a later group reusing the name inherited the old group's schedules.

`delSchedsFor(g)` in `destroy()`, next to `disarmReport` and the event-ring
drop, which close the same name-reuse hazard for their own state. The generation
-id scheme the remediation proposes is not needed once the records are gone
with the group.

### M15. Proxy concurrency limits do not impose an aggregate request-body memory budget (MEDIUM, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/proxy.go`
- **Finding:** Each admitted POST or PUT can materialize up to 64 MiB in the daemon, while admission permits 32 requests per group and 128 globally. The body remains referenced while the upstream request and retries run, allowing a guest to synchronize near-limit requests and impose multi-gigabyte heap pressure that can terminate the daemon and disrupt unrelated groups.
- **Preconditions:** A user able to run code in a guest can open parallel connections to that guest's proxy endpoint without host credentials. Thirty-two concurrent near-limit POSTs can reach the per-group request allowance, and multiple groups can approach the global allowance. The request-count semaphore and its 60-second wait do not prevent already admitted bodies from accumulating in memory.
- **Commit:** `b8642d5`

**Decision.**

The previous audit bounded one body (`proxyMaxBody`, 64 MiB) and how many
(32 per group / 128 global). Their PRODUCT is the number that matters: ~8 GiB
of live heap in the process that also holds the OAuth token, every group's log
tailer and the whole control plane — and the bodies stay live across up to 7
retry attempts, with `injectThinkingDisplay` unmarshalling one on top. No
exploit is needed, only large valid-looking requests in parallel, which
`cs-subagent` already fans out.

`proxyBodyBudget` (512 MiB) is charged as the body is READ — not guessed from
`Content-Length`, which the guest sets and a chunked request omits — and
returned when the body stops being referenced. Over budget answers 503, the
same "come back" the slot wait already answers with. Both providers go through
one `readRequestBody`; the Venice path buffers and retries identically and had
the identical hole. Deliberately far above real traffic (a turn's body is
kilobytes; 512 MiB still admits sixteen simultaneous maximum-size ones): an OOM
backstop like the semaphores beside it, never a scheduler.
`TestProxyBodyBudgetBounded`.

### M16. Concurrent whole-file config updates can restore revoked group security posture (MEDIUM, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/config.go`
- **Finding:** Unsynchronized read-modify-write operations over the complete group configuration allow a stale lower-trust snapshot to overwrite newer operator changes. A main guest or authorized Spawn caller can race an operator or provider update and restore protected values such as network, root, ports, size, or autostart.
- **Preconditions:** Requires a main-guest compromise or an authenticated Spawn caller with permission for the target group, plus a read-before-write-after interleaving with an operator or provider configuration update. Repeated permitted updates can improve the race likelihood. No filesystem access or operator credential is required from the lower-trust actor; the separate Spawn and provider sinks remain reachable if only the primary site is fixed.
- **Commit:** `5389150`

**Decision.**

Three callers — `configCmd`, `seedSpawnConfig`, `ensureProviderConfig` — each
parsed the whole `config.json`, changed a key or two, and wrote the whole
document back, with no lock between read and write. The last writer's stale
copy of every OTHER key wins, so an operator lowering `network` to none could
have it undone seconds later by a spawn seeding `provider` from a snapshot
taken before the change. Posture is admin-only (M7) exactly so it cannot be
lowered by a lesser principal; restoring it through a stale rewrite is the same
outcome by another route.

`updateGroupConfig` is now the only writer: a per-group mutex held across read,
mutate and commit, and a commit by rename so a reader sees one document or the
other. (`os.WriteFile` truncates in place, so readers could catch half a file.
`groupNetwork`/`groupRoot` fail CLOSED on a parse error, so that window was
never an escalation — but a config read silently answering "default" because it
caught a write mid-flight is not something to leave in.)
`TestGroupConfigUpdatesAreSerialized`.

### M17. Send and schedule paths can provision arbitrary unregistered groups (MEDIUM, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/send.go`
- **Finding:** Caller-controlled send and schedule targets are validated only for syntax, then reach `ensure`, which creates persistent group state, allocates a port, and attempts to boot a microVM even when the group is not registered. This lets send- or schedule-capable identities bypass the intended lifecycle authorization and the explicit ctl spawn quota; stale schedules can also recreate destroyed groups.
- **Preconditions:** Moderate: the attacker needs an authenticated identity permitted to send or schedule for the target, but does not need spawn permission. A fresh syntactically valid group name is sufficient for send paths; a schedule can be persisted and allowed to fire later.
- **Commit:** `5389150`

**Decision.**

Real, and the root of M14's other half. `ensure()` created whatever
syntactically valid name it was handed, and it is reached from `send`, `clear`,
`restart`, a schedule fire and a shell attach — none of which carry spawn
authority. So a principal with `send` on `"*"` provisioned groups without
`spawn`, and the ctl plane's `ctlMaxSpawn` cap was reachable around rather than
through.

Split: `ensure()` boots a group that is registered in `groups.json` and errors
on an unknown name; `spawnEnsure()` is the creating path, called from exactly
three admission points — the ctl `spawn` verb, the `Spawn` RPC, and the
daemon's own boot of `main`. The cap moved with it: the `Spawn` RPC enforces
`ctlMaxSpawn` too, since `spawn` is an ordinary grantable verb and a non-admin
role holding it on `"*"` previously had no bound at all.
`TestEnsureDoesNotProvision`.

### M18. List and WatchState bypass group ACLs and disclose unauthorized job metadata (MEDIUM, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/grpc_server.go`
- **Finding:** Authenticated callers granted only the targetless `list` or `watch_state` verbs receive the daemon-wide group snapshot, including groups and cached `JobInfo` records outside their authorized scope.
- **Preconditions:** An authenticated client with a non-admin certificate and matching bearer token can invoke List or WatchState using only the monitoring grants. No group target, Jobs permission, or target-name guessing is required; unauthorized groups and job records are returned immediately, and WatchState continues disclosing refreshed snapshots.
- **Commit:** `b8642d5`

**Decision.**

`list` and `watch_state` are verb-only in the ACL, so the interceptor
authorized the CALL and the handler then serialized the whole fleet — every
group, and with it every group's job records: command text, session, rc,
timings, output size. A role confined to one group by `"send": ["main"]` still
enumerated and continuously watched everything.

Rather than invent a second grammar, these two verbs now PROJECT through their
own grant's target set — something `acl.json` could already express and which
every seeded role writes as `"*"`, so a broad grant behaves exactly as before
and `"list": ["main"]` finally means what it reads as. Jobs are narrowed a
second time by the `jobs` grant: seeing that a group exists and reading the
command lines running inside it are different asks.

Mechanically this needed the caller in the handler, so the interceptors now put
the identity in the context (`withIdentity`; `idStream` does the same for
streaming handlers, which grpc-go gives no hook for). `WatchState` keeps the
shared-frame fast path for unnarrowed watchers and gives a narrowed one its own
frame and its own hash — its view changes on a different schedule.
`TestAggregateViewsProjectByGrant`.

### M19. Stale background tailer writes into a reused slot and misattributes output to a new session (MEDIUM, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/send.go`
- **Finding:** A background tailer retains a reusable slot-log pathname after its originating turn releases the slot. Because the tailer is not canceled or bound to a slot generation, delayed output can be appended after another session reuses the file and writes its session marker, causing the old output to be attributed to the new session.
- **Preconditions:** A same-group authorized user starts a background command whose output is delayed, waits for the originating turn to finish, and allows or triggers another turn to reuse the released slot. During the tailer's lifetime, delayed output is appended and inherited by the later session. No append-time race is required beyond ordinary delayed-task execution.
- **Commit:** `b8642d5`

**Decision.**

Real. `tailBackgroundTask` runs for up to ten minutes and the turn that spawned
it usually ends first — that is the feature, the operator watches the output
accumulate — after which the slot is released and a LATER turn, possibly
another conversation, writes its own session marker into the same stream. The
`[[bg]]` record carried no session, and the parser's attribution is sticky, so
the late line landed in whichever conversation owned the stream by then.

Fixed by writing the session INTO the record: `[[bg]] <id>:<session> <text>`.
Explicit beats sticky here, and it needs no slot-generation bookkeeping —
the tailer is *supposed* to outlive its turn, so cancelling it at turn end (the
other obvious fix) would have removed the feature. Legacy transcripts have no
colon and keep the old behavior, so replayed history still parses.
`TestBackgroundRecordsCarryTheirSession`.

### M20. Main-to-peer goals can bypass the mandatory human-approval gate (MEDIUM, fixed, partly rejected)

- **Where:** `daemon/goals.go`
- **Finding:** The ctl and gRPC GoalSet handlers trust caller-controlled plan=false even when main targets a peer. The shared goal creation path converts false into a running goal, persists it, and starts execution immediately, bypassing the documented plan-first and human-approval workflow.
- **Preconditions:** The attacker must control or successfully prompt-inject the main guest agent, or possess an authenticated identity already authorized to set goals for the peer. A single ordinary goal-set request with a peer target and plan=false is sufficient; no additional goal-specific capability or human approval is required after acceptance.
- **Commit:** `5389150`

**Decision.**

The ctl half is real. A main caller's `goal_set` always targets a peer (its own
group is refused), and plan-first on that path IS the human gate:
`goal_approve` is self-only precisely so main cannot approve a plan it set on a
peer. An explicit `"plan": false` went straight to `goalStatusRunning` and
started an autonomous, self-judged, multi-iteration loop on another group with
nobody in the loop. Now forced to plan-first, with a log line saying so. A
group setting a goal on ITSELF keeps `plan=false`: approving its own plan is
allowed, so it is the same authority by a shorter route.

The gRPC half is rejected. `GoalSet` over gRPC is the OPERATOR — the human the
gate exists to involve — so `plan=false` there is that human approving up
front, not a bypass. A gate that the person it defers to cannot also skip is
not a gate, it is an obstacle.
`TestMainPeerGoalsAreAlwaysPlanFirst`.

### M21. Turn text marker escaping is bypassable across frame boundaries (MEDIUM, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/fcturn.go`
- **Finding:** The turn writer fails to preserve a partial marker prefix between streamed Text frames, allowing guest/model-controlled text to form genuine host control-marker lines in the slot log.
- **Preconditions:** A normal streamed response supplies independent text-delta frames, and a guest or model able to control their text can split a marker prefix across two frames. No authentication or parser-provenance check distinguishes the resulting line from the daemon's structural output. Exact frame chunking may depend on the upstream stream, but a guest-side sender can send the two chunks directly.
- **Commit:** `2f8533f`

**Decision.**

Real, and the one guest→host transcript forgery the marker design exists to
prevent. `turnWriter.text` checked `markerLike` only when the frame began at a
line boundary, so a guest sending `"["` in one Text frame and
`"[turn_end]]\n"` in the next assembled a marker on disk that no frame ever
contained. `logParser.feedLine` accepts it: the turn ends early, the slot is
released while the real stream is still running, and the same trick forges
`[ts:]`, `[[tool]]`, `[[notify]]` and the rest.

The check is now on the LOGICAL line. Rather than buffer to the next newline —
which would stall a whole paragraph of streamed prose — `text` withholds the
first bytes of a line only while they are still an ambiguous prefix of a marker
(`[`, `[t`, `>>`): at most three bytes, only at a line start, released by the
next byte, a newline, an interrupting marker, or the end of the stream.
`TestTurnMarkerEscapingSpansFrames` splits every marker at every byte boundary
and asserts no unescaped marker line appears and nothing is dropped.

### M22 / M27. Triaged together (MEDIUM)

- **M22. Guest-facing framed channels permit resource exhaustion with incomplete frames** (`daemon/fcframe.go`), fixed. A process running inside a guest can retain host-daemon connections, goroutines, file descriptors, and large frame buffers indefinitely by sending incomplete or maximum-sized length-prefixed frames.
- **M27. Guest-facing vsock listeners allow unbounded resource retention** (`daemon/fc.go`), fixed. A process running inside a guest can open arbitrary connections to the host's 9002, 9003, and 9004 vsock services. The daemon starts an unbounded handler goroutine for each accepted connection, and the affected framed reads have no idle deadlines. Connections that send only partial headers or payloads can therefore retain host file descriptors and goroutines indefinitely; stalled turn frames can also retain large payload allocations.
- **Commit:** `265d0e5`

**Decision.**

One finding twice. `fcAcceptLoop` spawned an untracked goroutine per accepted
connection with no admission limit, and the framed readers allocate whatever
length the peer declares (16 MiB on the turn channel) and then block in
`ReadFull` with no deadline. A guest could open connections in a loop,
declare a large frame on each, send nothing, and pin host goroutines,
descriptors and heap for the VM's lifetime.

Two bounds, chosen so each covers what the other cannot:

- **`fcMaxConnsPerGroup` (64), per group.** Every cost here is per connection,
  so capping connections caps all of them at once — which is why there is no
  separate frame-memory budget. Real traffic is one proxy connection per
  upstream request, one ctl connection, one gateway link and up to `groupSlots`
  (10) turn streams, so 64 is a wide margin. Per GROUP, so one guest cannot
  starve another; published-port connections get their own bucket because they
  arrive from the host side, and charging them here would let an outside caller
  starve the group's control channels.
- **`fcFrameBodyWait` (60s), from header to payload.** The header is waited on
  with NO deadline, deliberately: both channels are legitimately idle between
  frames — the ctl connection for the VM's lifetime, a turn stream for as long
  as the model thinks — so an idle timeout there would kill working
  connections. Once a header is read the payload is already allocated and no
  honest peer pauses mid-frame (the writer emits header and body in one write),
  so the deadline starts exactly where the peer's obligation does.
  `fcReadFrameBounded` peeks the header rather than reading it, which keeps the
  shared framing code byte-identical to the guest's.

`TestGuestVsockConnectionsBounded`.

### M23. Privileged job readers follow worker-controlled symlinks (MEDIUM, fixed, by removing the privilege)

- **Where:** `fcguest/main.go`
- **Finding:** Root-authority job inspection and background-task tailing dereference worker-controlled pathnames without containment, regular-file validation, or no-follow semantics, allowing a guest worker to disclose arbitrary root-readable files within the guest VM.
- **Preconditions:** A malicious worker can create or replace job entries using ordinary filesystem operations. Disclosure occurs when an authorized client requests Jobs, JobLogs, or JobTail for that group, or when guest-authored task output causes the automatic background tailer to start. No shell injection is required: the pathname is syntactically valid and the privileged reader follows it later.
- **Commit:** `2f8533f`

**Decision.**

The worker owns `/workspace/.cs/jobs/<id>/`, and the daemon asked the ROOT
guest agent to `cat`/`wc`/`head`/`tail` paths inside it. Ordinary shell tools
follow symlinks, so replacing `out` or `cmd` with one made root read a file the
worker cannot — a worker→root disclosure inside a guest whose `root=no` profile
says there is no path to root.

The remediation proposed hardening the readers (`openat2`,
`RESOLVE_NO_SYMLINKS`, a confined walk). That is the wrong shape of fix here,
because nothing in this path ever needed root. Every script the daemon sends
over `exec`/`exec_stream` reads or removes WORKER-OWNED data — the job dirs,
the session and venice-history files, a `tail -F` of a file claude code wrote
as the worker, `stat -f /workspace` plus `/proc/meminfo` — and the one that
signals (`interruptAgent`) targets uid-1000 processes, which uid 1000 may
signal. So `exec` and `exec_stream` now drop to the worker uid, exactly as
`run_script` and `shell_attach` already did. A planted symlink buys the worker
what it could read anyway, and the whole class goes with it. If a future exec
genuinely needs root it gets its own op: the privilege should be named at the
call site.

### M24. Recursive shared root mount allows worker mount events to propagate into fc-agent (MEDIUM, accepted)

- **Where:** `fcguest/main.go`
- **Finding:** fc-agent recursively marks the entire guest root mount tree as shared while exposing FUSE and provisioning rootless namespace tooling for the untrusted node worker. A worker-created mount namespace can retain that shared propagation and propagate mounts back into the agent's namespace, allowing attacker-controlled content to replace paths subsequently used by PID 1 or other root-side operations.
- **Preconditions:** An attacker needs code execution as the already-untrusted node worker, which is the normal workload execution identity. No guest-root capability or host access is required; the worker can create its own user and mount namespaces and use the existing shared propagation and FUSE exposure. The resulting impact is limited to the guest by Firecracker/KVM.
- **Commit:** `4ba2842`

**Decision.**

True, and largely moot after M23. `earlyInit` marks `/` `MS_REC|MS_SHARED`
because rootless podman in the guest requires it ("/ is not a shared mount"),
and the worker has the userns prerequisites to create a mount namespace — so a
mount it makes can propagate back to the peer group fc-agent lives in. There is
no mount-namespace separation between the two inside the guest; there never
was.

It mattered because root-side code read paths the worker controls. It no longer
does: `exec`/`exec_stream` run as the worker (M23), and `writeWorkerFile` uses
`O_CREAT|O_EXCL|O_NOFOLLOW` (M13), which refuses a path a mount has made point
at an existing inode just as it refuses a symlink. What remains is a guest-
internal `root=no` weakening with no identified consumer, contained by KVM.

Narrowing the shared subtree is the right long-term shape, but it cannot be
changed blind: the constraint comes from podman's storage layout, and verifying
it needs a rootfs build and a booted VM with containers actually running. Not
worth breaking the guest's container support on an untested guess for a hole
whose consumers have all been closed by other means.

### M25. Long-lived gRPC streams retain access after authorization revocation (MEDIUM, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/auth.go`
- **Finding:** Authenticated streaming RPCs authorize the caller when the stream is opened, but do not revalidate or cancel the stream when its ACL grant, token/role authorization, or certificate allowlist entry is revoked. Existing streams can therefore continue delivering replayed and live data after access has been removed.
- **Preconditions:** Medium. The attacker needs valid credentials and access at stream creation, then must keep the connection open while an administrator revokes access. No race or additional bypass is required; subsequent producer activity is delivered automatically. The issue is most relevant to compromised, decommissioned, or offboarded clients.
- **Commit:** `4ba2842`

**Decision.**

Real, and pointed at exactly the streams revocation is usually about. A
server-streaming RPC receives its one request and never calls `RecvMsg` again,
so every check the interceptor made happened at connect time: revoking a role,
deleting a token, or narrowing `acl.json` left every attached `SubscribeGroup`,
`WatchState`, `SubscribeLogs`, `JobTail` and `AttachShell` delivering
transcripts and pty output with the access it had at connect.

`SendMsg` is the one call every delivery path shares — AttachShell's separate
guest→client goroutine included — so the re-check rides there, on the existing
`aclStream` wrapper, and covers every streaming RPC at once without touching a
handler. It re-resolves the identity by NAME from `tokens.json` (a deleted
entry is a revoked device; an edited one may have narrowed its roles, and
trusting the connect-time roles would defeat the point) and re-runs
`rolesAllowed` against the live ACL.

Rate-limited to one re-check per stream per 5s: each reads `acl.json` and
`tokens.json`, the same per-call reads the unary path already does and the
reason role edits need no restart. Per frame that would be a file read per
transcript chunk; per five seconds it is nothing. The cost is a revocation
taking up to five seconds to reach an attached stream, which is the right trade
and is pinned by the test. `TestStreamAuthzRevalidates`.

### M26. Oversized cron step can crash the daemon via integer wraparound (MEDIUM, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/cron.go`
- **Finding:** The cron parser accepts any positive machine-sized step and then increments a field index without overflow protection. A schedule such as `1/9223372036854775807 * * * *` wraps the index negative after the first iteration, causing an out-of-range array access and an unrecovered process-fatal panic.
- **Preconditions:** Requires an authenticated schedule-creation capability. A guest-side control client authorized to issue self-targeted `sched_add` requests can submit the payload directly; authorized gRPC clients can reach the same sink. No host filesystem or administrative access is required once that capability is available.
- **Commit:** `2f8533f`

**Decision.**

Real and cheap. `parseField` checked only that a step was positive, and the
expansion is `for v := from; v <= to; v += step` — so a step near `MaxInt64`
wrapped `v` NEGATIVE on the second iteration, left the loop condition true, and
panicked on `mask[v]`. Reachable from every schedule-add path, the guest ctl
plane's `sched_add` included, none of which recovers: one malformed cron line
from a compromised guest takes the daemon down.

Steps are now bounded by the field's own span. Rejected rather than clamped: a
step wider than the range can only ever select `from`, so anything above the
span is a typo and accepting it silently would hide the typo.
`TestCronStepBounded`.

### M28. Group-scoped Send authorization permits cross-session conversation hijacking (MEDIUM, not a finding)

- **Where:** `daemon/grpc_server.go`
- **Finding:** A client granted `send` access to a group can select any valid named session in that group. The ACL authorizes only `SendReq.group`; no caller-to-session ownership or access check is performed before the message is queued and resumed in that session's persistent Claude conversation.
- **Preconditions:** Any authenticated client whose role grants `send` on the target group can exploit this directly. The attacker only needs a valid session name, which is exposed through group session metadata/events or can be learned from normal group activity; no path traversal or race is required.
- **Commit:** `2f1cec8`

**Decision.**

Named sessions are conversations, not tenants. They share one workspace, one
VM, one guest uid and one filesystem — `goals.go` says so outright about
concurrent goals ("goals that fight over the same files are the caller's
problem, same as two chat sessions editing one repo"). A caller who may `send`
to a group may already reach every file every session in it reads, so a
per-session authorization boundary would defend nothing while implying a
guarantee koto does not make. The boundary is the GROUP, and that is what the
ACL targets.

Same answer as M4, which asked for the same boundary on `attach_shell`.

### M29. Workflow requests can provision unregistered groups without spawn authorization (MEDIUM, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/grpc_server.go`
- **Finding:** SchedAdd and GoalSet validate only group-name syntax, not whether the target is an existing registered group. Their deferred execution reaches ensure, which creates persistent group state, allocates ports, registers listeners, and may spawn a microVM, allowing callers with workflow permissions to bypass the explicit group-creation/spawn control. These are the same underlying missing existence/creation-authorization check and should be fixed together.
- **Preconditions:** Requires network access and authenticated client credentials for a role with sched_add or goal_set on a broad or wildcard target. The attacker can generate valid unregistered names without knowing existing groups. Each request is inexpensive, while deferred execution can allocate persistent state and VM resources; fixing only one workflow leaves the other exploitable.
- **Commit:** `2f1cec8`

**Decision.**

Mostly closed by M17 already: `ensure()` no longer creates, so a fired schedule
or a goal's first turn can no longer bring a group into existence. That left
the record itself — `SchedAdd` and `GoalSet` happily persisted work against a
name that does not exist, which can now only ever fail at fire time.

`registeredGroup` is checked at creation on both, so the caller gets an error
it can read instead of a schedule that silently never works, and `spawn` stays
the one verb that brings a group into existence.

### M30. Group-scoped Metrics RPC discloses another group's latest metric (MEDIUM, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/grpc_server.go`
- **Finding:** The Metrics RPC authorizes a request against the supplied group but always includes the newest daemon-wide metrics record in GlobalMetric, allowing a client with access to only one group to receive metadata belonging to another group.
- **Preconditions:** Any authenticated client with a non-wildcard metrics grant for one group can trigger this with a normal Metrics RPC. The attacker only needs another group to have generated a recent metric record; repeated polling can observe changing cross-group activity.
- **Commit:** `2f1cec8`

**Decision.**

Real and narrow. The ACL did its job — `metrics` is checked against
`MetricsReq.Group` — but the handler then set `GlobalMetric` from
`latestMetricAny()` unconditionally, which is the newest record from ANY group:
path, status, request id, token counts, rate-limit headers, provider org. So
asking about a group you may see answered with another group's newest request.

`GlobalMetric` is a global read, so it now needs the `"*"` target the
untargeted form of this verb already needs. `TestScopedMetricsOmitGlobal`.

### M31. Goal dispatch proceeds after failed conversation reset (MEDIUM, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/goals.go`
- **Finding:** `clearGoalSession` ignores reset failures, allowing plan, worker, and judge turns to be queued against the prior conversation state instead of a verified fresh context.
- **Preconditions:** Moderate: the attacker needs influence over an automated goal or workspace plus a reset/guest fault. A transient reset failure can be followed by successful queued delivery; no direct unauthenticated endpoint or host-level primitive is shown.
- **Commit:** `1ca6b2e`

**Decision.**

Real, and it breaks the loop's central claim. `clearGoalSession` logged
`clearSessionContext`'s failure and returned normally, so the plan, worker and
judge dispatchers enqueued their turn regardless — and the enqueue can still
succeed, so a failed reset did not reliably stop delivery.

A fresh context per turn is the design, not a nicety: the worker iterates with
the filesystem as its memory, and the judge must review WITHOUT having watched
the work. A failed reset lets the plan phase inherit an execution context, an
iteration inherit the last one's, and — the one that matters — the judge
inherit the worker's own reasoning and rubber-stamp it, so the loop can end on
an unverified self-report. That is precisely what the judge exists to prevent.

The error now propagates and each of the three dispatchers pauses the goal with
`stalled` and a reason naming the reset, which also raises the operator
notification the pause path already carries. Pausing for a human beats running
without the isolation the loop advertises. `TestGoalTurnAbortsOnFailedReset`.

### M32. Install migration resurrects revoked client credentials from stale clone (MEDIUM, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/install.go`
- **Finding:** `koto install` and upgrades additively merge clone-side authentication registries into the installed state, so credentials removed from the live state can be restored from an older clone. The daemon then accepts the restored certificate fingerprint and token identity again.
- **Preconditions:** Medium: the attacker must retain the revoked client certificate and bearer token, and an operator must subsequently run `koto install` or an upgrade from a clone that still contains those records. No network access to the installer is needed, but the resulting authentication is accepted by the daemon and retains the stale identity's roles.
- **Commit:** `faee9c1`

**Decision.**

Real, and it undoes a security action. Revoking a device is deleting its line
from `clients.allow` and its entry from `tokens.json`. The additive merge ran on
every install, so an upgrade from a clone that predates the revocation put both
back — and the `client-*`/`token-*` files are copied too, so possession of the
old certificate and bearer token was enough to authenticate again with the old
roles. The previous audit (L10) had noticed the behavior and settled for
printing a line about it; printing is not a control.

The merge now runs only on a FIRST install, which is the migration it exists
for. Once the installed registries exist they are AUTHORITATIVE — and they will
exist, because the wizard mints into the state dir, so a clone-side identity is
legacy by construction. Clone identities the installed registry lacks are
NAMED, with the command that would add one back deliberately: silence would be
the worse failure of the two, since an operator who really did mint in the clone
needs to know why it does not work, and one who revoked a device needs to know
it stayed revoked. `TestInstallDoesNotResurrectRevokedIdentities`.

### M33. Provider-controlled session ID enables privileged path traversal during session clearing (MEDIUM, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/groups.go`
- **Finding:** The root-executed clear helper inserts the contents of a worker-writable session-ID file into a quoted pathname without validating it as a basename. Slash and `..` components therefore escape the intended Claude project directory and can make the helper unlink another `.jsonl` file.
- **Preconditions:** An attacker must be able to influence guest-side worker/provider state and have the affected session subsequently cleared. A malicious or compromised provider, or another guest process with access to the worker-owned marker, can supply slash/traversal components in the stored ID; shell metacharacter injection is not required.
- **Commit:** `2f1cec8`

**Decision.**

Real. The id is captured off the PROVIDER's stream, stored in a file the worker
can also rewrite, and then substituted into
`rm -f /workspace/.claude/projects/*/"$(cat "$I")".jsonl`. Quoting stops
metacharacters; it does not stop `..`, so `../other/transcript` walks out of
this session's project directory into another's.

Validated on both sides, because the two are different times and different
writers: `fc-agent` refuses a non-conforming id on the way in (`validSessionID`
— Claude's are UUIDs, so hex and dashes), and the clear script re-checks the
file's content before it becomes a path component, since the file is writable
by something other than the code that wrote it.

### M34. Host-address isolation fails open on stale or failed self-address refresh (MEDIUM, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/fcnet.go`
- **Finding:** The self-address denylist is refreshed every five seconds, but `net.InterfaceAddrs` errors are ignored and the newly collected result replaces the previous list even when enumeration fails or is incomplete. A host address acquired during the cache interval, or omitted after a failed refresh, is therefore classified as LAN/WAN rather than control-plane traffic. Guest L3 filtering and the forward proxy both consume this classification.
- **Preconditions:** Requires code execution in a guest attached to a LAN, WAN, or full network profile, knowledge of a host interface address, and a reachable host service. The stale interval is deterministic after an address change; refresh failure is environmental or transient rather than directly attacker-triggered. The attacker can repeatedly probe known or discoverable host addresses and can use either the raw L3 path or the forward proxy path.
- **Commit:** `2f1cec8`

**Decision.**

Real, and backwards in the dangerous direction. `fcSelfIPs` is a DENYLIST —
the host's own interface addresses, which no guest may reach — and the refresh
discarded `net.InterfaceAddrs`' error, then stored the (empty) result as fresh.
A failed netlink dump therefore unblocked every host address for a full TTL, on
the L3 filter and the L7 proxy alike, since they share `fcClassifyDst`.

The last known-good list is now kept on error and `at` is left alone so the
next call retries immediately rather than after another TTL.
`TestSelfIPRefreshFailsClosed`.

### M35. Empty or null ACL targets authorize global cross-group reads (MEDIUM, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/acl.go`
- **Finding:** The ACL loader turns both JSON `null` and the empty string into the empty target key, while global forms of targeted RPCs use an empty group as their target. The authorization check then treats that malformed key as sufficient authorization, allowing a non-admin role to read across all groups without an explicit `*` grant.
- **Preconditions:** A caller must already possess a valid allowlisted client certificate, matching bearer token, and a non-admin role whose persisted ACL contains `null` or `""` for a targeted verb. No ACL mutation privilege is needed; the malformed value can originate from deployment configuration, migration, legacy data, or manual editing. Once present, the caller sends a global targeted RPC with its group omitted and receives cross-group data.
- **Commit:** `076e013`

**Decision.**

Real, and a one-line hole with a wide answer. `json.Unmarshal` of `null` into a
string SUCCEEDS and leaves `""`, as does an explicit `""` — and the scalar
branch of `parseTargets` stored that as `names[""]`. The empty target is what
`targetOf` returns for a group-scoped request that OMITS the group: the
read-across-every-group form, documented to need `"*"`. So a malformed or
migrated grant read as a global one. (The list branch already skipped empties;
only the scalar branch did not, which is why it survived review.)

Empty and whitespace-only scalars now grant nothing. `TestEmptyACLTargetGrantsNothing`.

### M36. Group-wide attachment spool can deliver orphaned uploads to unrelated turns (MEDIUM, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/fc.go`
- **Finding:** Attachments are written to a group-global spool before queue admission and later selected only by a shared modification-time watermark. Rejected or discarded jobs leave unowned files that a later turn can package into its agent request, while concurrent session deliveries can select and consume the same file.
- **Preconditions:** An authenticated principal with ordinary send permission for a group can trigger queue rejection or arrange concurrent turns in separate sessions. No filesystem access or elevated privilege is required. Orphan consumption is deterministic once a failed or discarded request leaves a file; concurrent duplication is timing-dependent but enabled by the documented multi-session concurrency.
- **Commit:** `70a9150`

**Decision.**

Real, and three bugs in one mechanism. Uploads were selected by a group-wide
mtime WATERMARK: every file newer than the last delivery rode along with
whichever turn went next. With concurrent sessions in one group that means two
turns can both select a file before either advances the watermark (one
session's image reaches another session's agent), the turn that was supposed to
carry it can find it already deleted, and a file whose `Send` was refused after
staging — queue full, validation error — is orphaned yet still delivered later
to an unrelated turn.

The message already carried the answer, which is why this needed no claim
protocol and no plumbing through the queue: `processAttachments` embeds
`[image: .cs/uploads/<name>]` for exactly the files that turn is about, and
`fcSendMsg` already receives the message. `fcTurnUploads` reads ownership out
of the text — exact, per turn, and unaffected by concurrency. The watermark is
gone.

Two supporting pieces. The names become `tar -C uploads` arguments and the
message text is not always the operator's (a ctl `send` from main carries
arbitrary text), so a reference must resolve to a plain basename that already
exists as a regular file in that group's own uploads dir. And staging is rolled
back when the enqueue is refused, with a 24h sweep for the one orphan that
remains possible — a daemon restart between staging and delivery — so the
pending-upload quota cannot be held by a file no turn will ever claim.
`TestUploadsAreOwnedByTheirTurn`.

### M37. gRPC lifecycle paths bypass the daemon-wide group quota (MEDIUM, fixed (with M17/M29))

- **Where:** `daemon/groups.go`
- **Finding:** The existing 100-group limit is enforced only in the in-guest `ctl` spawn handler. gRPC `Spawn`, `Restart`, and lazy `Send` reach the shared provisioning path without an equivalent global admission check, allowing an authorized broad-scope client to register distinct groups and trigger persistent filesystem, registry, port, queue, and provisioning work.
- **Preconditions:** Moderate: exploitation requires a valid client certificate or bearer token and an ACL grant broad enough to cover attacker-selected group names. Once authorized, unique valid names and ordinary lifecycle RPCs are sufficient; no race, malformed input, or host access is required. The primitive is most plausibly a medium-impact denial of service against group-management and provisioning capacity rather than an unauthenticated service-wide compromise.
- **Commit:** `076e013`

**Decision.**

The same finding as M17 from the lifecycle side, and closed by the same split:
`ensure()` no longer provisions, so `Send` and `Restart` cannot bring a group
into existence, and the `ctlMaxSpawn` cap now applies to the `Spawn` RPC as well
as the ctl verb. `SchedAdd`/`GoalSet` refuse an unregistered target (M29).

The remainder of the remediation — per-identity quotas, rate limits on
