forged extra rendered rows and an escape reached the terminal.

**Fix:** a name policy (`libNameOK`), the same shape as the theme drop-in names
in M131 — a filename is a path component *and* a rendered string, so the
character class guards both. The refusal deliberately does not echo the rejected
name.

**Script OUTPUT, the finding's second half, is already covered**: the daemon
sanitizes `RunScript` output by default (`chunkSanitizer`) and the TUI's
`/runscript` never passes `raw`.

Test: `TestLibraryNamesAndGroupSwitchAreValidated` in `tui/scripts_test.go`.

### L17. Unchanged credential file can remain world-readable (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/install.go`
- **Finding:** `sudoWriteIfChanged` returns when `/etc/koto/koto.env` has identical content before enforcing the requested mode, allowing an existing permissive file containing credentials to remain readable by other local users.
- **Preconditions:** A local user needs no special privilege beyond ordinary read access to a permissive existing file. The permissive state must already exist due to an earlier install, administrator action, or other metadata change; rerunning `koto install` with identical content does not repair it. Any exposed API key may then be reused outside the host, subject to the provider's controls.
- **Commit:** `8196f73`

**Decision.**

`daemon/install.go`, `sudoWriteIfChanged`.

**Confirmed.** The `0600` is the confidentiality invariant for a file that can
hold an API key, and it was applied only on the **rewrite** path. A file whose
content already matched kept whatever permissions it had, and re-running install
never repaired them — which is exactly when an operator expects the promised
mode to be established.

**Fix:** same bytes is not the same mode. When the content matches but the
permissions do not, the helper issues `sudo chmod` and reports the change.

### L18. Stale cached transcripts remain renderable after group authorization is removed (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/model.go`
- **Finding:** A successful group-list refresh replaces the authorized group map but leaves transcript lines for removed groups in `m.lines`. Because `/sw` accepts any group name and `allBlocks` filters cached lines only by matching `m.cur`, a known former group name still renders retained transcript data; if the name is later reused in the same long-lived TUI process, the new group can inherit the old transcript.
- **Preconditions:** A previously authorized user, or a later user sharing the same long-lived TUI process or terminal, must know the former group name and have local access to that client state. No unauthenticated remote request can invoke the stale rendering path, and the transcript was previously delivered to the client, so the realistic impact is limited to post-revocation retention and cross-lifecycle disclosure in shared or reused sessions.
- **Commit:** `8196f73`

**Decision.**

`tui/model.go`, `/sw`.

**Confirmed, in the half M155 did not cover.** `m.groups` is the authorized,
per-identity projection, so a group whose authorization is removed leaves the
snapshot and `forgetGroup` (M155) now drops its transcript, caches and cursor.
But `/sw` set `m.cur` to **any** name at all, so the name stayed reachable —
and within the window before the next `listMsg`, its cached lines rendered.

**Fix:** `/sw` accepts only a group the daemon currently shows this client, and
says so otherwise. The spawn path is unaffected (it switches directly, before
the list refresh, and does not go through `/sw`), and so is tree selection,
whose rows are built from `m.groups`. It also catches a typo, which used to
switch to a blank screen with no explanation.

### L19. Unredacted tool arguments are persisted in group logs and replayed to transcript readers (LOW, accepted)

- **Where:** `daemon/fcturn.go`
- **Finding:** Complete serialized tool inputs, including potentially credential-bearing commands, headers, URLs, and file contents, are written to persistent group transcripts without secret redaction and are later returned through history and live-event APIs.
- **Preconditions:** Requires local filesystem access to the daemon volume or a valid authenticated client with ACL permission to read or subscribe to the affected group. There is no identified unauthenticated network path, but every credential-bearing tool call is automatically persisted and remains replayable to those principals.
- **Commit:** `8196f73`

**Decision.**

`daemon/fcturn.go`, `daemon/logparse.go`.

The facts are right: the complete serialized tool input is written into
`.cs/log.<slot>`, parsed back as `Event.Input`, and `sanitizeEvent` removes
terminal controls rather than secrets.

**Accepted, because the transcript IS the record.** The same reasoning declined
M105: koto's audit story is that what the agent did is reconstructible, and a
tool call's arguments are the substance of what it did — a `Bash` command line,
a file path, a URL. Redacting them would leave an audit log that cannot answer
the question it exists for. There is also no tractable rule for *which*
arguments are sensitive: they are arbitrary JSON authored by a model, with no
schema koto controls and no marker for secrecy.

What actually bounds the audience is already in place and is the right layer:
the transcript files are 0600 inside a 0700 directory under a 0750 state root
(M136), and reading them over the wire needs an authenticated identity whose
role grants `history`/`subscribe_group` on that group.

If a deployment needs guest secrets kept out of the transcript, the lever is the
guest's own behaviour — do not pass secrets as tool arguments — not a filter the
daemon cannot write correctly.

### L20. Invalid or unreadable provider configuration can silently route prompts to Venice while displaying Claude (LOW, already fixed (M148))

- **Where:** `daemon/proxy.go`
- **Finding:** The UI-side and proxy-side provider fallbacks disagree. Missing, malformed, unreadable, or unrecognized group configuration is displayed as `claudesdk` but routed to Venice, allowing prompt data to cross the intended external-provider boundary without the operator's awareness.
- **Preconditions:** Low. A remote unauthenticated attacker cannot directly choose the invalid state. Reproduction requires local configuration tampering, a failed configuration repair, a read/write race, or equivalent local corruption. Once the state exists, the next permitted prompt is routed to Venice without an additional confirmation that the displayed provider is stale or invalid.
- **Commit:** `8196f73`

**Decision.**

`groupProvider` is now a thin wrapper over `groupProviderName`, which returns
`defaultProvider` (claudesdk) for an absent, unparseable or unrecognised value —
so the proxy, the TUI and the guest turn path give the same answer for the same
state. The non-atomic write the finding also names was closed earlier too:
`updateGroupConfig` holds the group's lock across read-mutate-commit and commits
by rename. Verified rather than assumed.

### L21. Shell command injection in peer-facing report recipe (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/report.go`
- **Finding:** The daemon instructs the peer to substitute report content directly into a single-quoted shell argument. A report containing a single quote can terminate that argument and execute additional commands when the peer follows the recipe.
- **Preconditions:** Requires attacker-controlled content to reach a delegated peer and for the peer/model to follow the report recipe while substituting that content. After substitution, exploitation requires no additional shell or daemon permission beyond the guest worker's existing privileges; the Firecracker boundary limits the resulting impact to guest/worker capabilities unless a separate escape exists.
- **Commit:** `8196f73`

**Decision.**

`daemon/report.go`, `reportRequestNote`.

**Confirmed.** The note is an executable recipe delivered to a peer agent, and
it asked that agent to substitute its own report text inside
`printf '%s' '...'`. Shell single quotes cannot contain a single quote, so a
report whose content included one — a contraction, a quoted identifier, anything
an attacker-influenced summary might carry — closed the string and the remainder
ran as commands with the worker's authority. The daemon never performs the
substitution itself, so the hazard is in what koto **taught** the agent to do,
which is still koto's to fix.

**Fix:** the report text travels through a **file** and the command is
**constant** — it takes no substitutions, so nothing attacker-influenced is ever
spliced into a shell word. The note says so explicitly, since an agent
improvising around a template is the failure mode being removed.

### L22. Partial-line truncation enables embedded log markers to become semantic events (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/logtail.go`
- **Finding:** When an unterminated log line exceeds the partial-line limit, `tailFile` retains only its suffix and later sends that suffix to the line-oriented parser as if it began at a real line boundary. An embedded marker that lands at the retained-buffer boundary can therefore be interpreted as a forged prompt, tool, or error event, or as a block terminator.
- **Preconditions:** The attacker must influence guest/model output, produce a newline-free line larger than 8 MiB, and align the embedded marker with the suffix boundary (which depends on the effective read size). This is materially harder than ordinary marker injection, but the output channel is attacker-influenced and the resulting effect is forged event/transcript data and parser-state manipulation rather than host execution or privilege escalation.
- **Commit:** `8196f73`

**Decision.**

`daemon/logtail.go`, `tailFile`.

**Confirmed.** An over-long newline-free partial is cut to its tail, and the cut
lands on an arbitrary byte — so whatever follows becomes the **start** of the
line handed to `feedLine`. `turnWriter` escapes marker-like text only at genuine
output line starts, and this start is synthetic, so a guest emitting one long
line with `[[err]] `, `>>> `, `[[tool]] ` or a block-close marker positioned at
the boundary had it read as framing.

**Fix:** the surviving tail is prefixed with `…[line truncated] `. Nothing after
a non-empty prefix is at position 0 any more, so no marker can match — and the
mark is *content*, telling the operator the line was cut, which is true and was
previously silent.

Test: `TestTruncatedPartialLineCannotBecomeFraming` in
`daemon/audit_fixes_test.go` puts all seven markers at a synthetic start,
asserting none becomes an event or opens a parser block, that the text still
arrives as text, and that a real marker at a real line start still frames.

### L23. Guest-controlled newlines inject forged daemon log records (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/events.go`
- **Finding:** Guest-controlled fields reach daemon output without a single-line framing invariant. The sanitizer preserves LF, and the shared log delivery path emits the resulting text to stderr and publishes it to the log ring, subscribers, and TUI as operator-visible content. The separate job-completion notification sink also embeds the same guest-controlled metadata and requires independent normalization.
- **Preconditions:** Low-to-moderate: exploitation requires code execution in a guest or an authenticated client with access to the relevant control path, but no additional daemon vulnerability is needed. A single valid JSON request containing an escaped newline is sufficient; the impact is limited to log and notification integrity rather than terminal control or host compromise.
- **Commit:** `f170899`

**Decision.**

`daemon/events.go`, `logDeliver`.

**Confirmed in the general case; the specific one was already closed.** The
`job_done` `id`/`rc` half was fixed by M56 (`ctlJobField` bounds and strips
them). What remained is the rule itself: `sanitize` deliberately **keeps**
newlines, because most of what it guards is prose that legitimately has them —
but a log **record** is not prose. It goes to stderr with one terminating
newline, is held as one ring entry and rendered as one row, so an embedded LF
forged a whole extra record: a convincing continuation line for any
line-oriented collector reading the daemon's stderr, and an extra row in the TUI
attributed to the daemon itself.

**Fix:** `flattenInline` at the funnel every producer already passes, so the
invariant is stated once instead of being re-fixed per field. Checked first that
no call site emits a deliberately multi-line message — there are none.

Test: `TestDaemonLogRecordIsOneLine` in `daemon/audit_fixes_test.go`.

### L24. Workspace images are created with insecure permissions (LOW, already fixed (M136))

- **Where:** `daemon/fc.go`
- **Finding:** Persistent per-group ext4 workspace images are created with mode 0644 and later only chowned, leaving unnecessary group/other read access to private workspace data wherever an attacker can traverse the daemon state tree.
- **Preconditions:** Low to moderate: exploitation requires a local account or process with filesystem traversal access to the configured state directory. It does not require daemon authentication or IPC access, but it is not remotely exploitable and the standard installed state root reduces the set of principals able to reach the image.
- **Commit:** `f170899`

**Decision.**

The temp image is created `0600` and the rename carries that mode; the startup
repair pass (`hardenStatePaths`) clears group/other bits from images an older
koto created. Measured before that fix: every booted group's `workspace.img` was
`-rw-r--r--` owned by its per-VM subuid — which is the headline of M136.

### L25 / L28. Triaged together (LOW)

- **L25. Guest control payloads are logged globally without field-aware redaction** (`daemon/ctlpb.go`), already fixed (M115). `ctlDispatchPB` serializes the complete guest-controlled control request and emits it before command-specific validation, truncation, or sanitization. The resulting content is written to stderr, retained in the global log ring, and sent to global log subscribers.
- **L28. Guest-controlled control requests can cause bounded daemon and TUI memory exhaustion through oversized log events** (`daemon/events.go`), already fixed (M115). A guest that can use the authorized control channel can submit near-1 MiB protobuf requests whose decoded contents are serialized and logged before command-specific truncation or rate limiting. The shared log-delivery path sanitizes but does not byte-limit the resulting message before stderr output, daemon ring retention, or subscriber delivery. Because downstream limits are based primarily on event count, repeated requests can consume substantial daemon/TUI memory and CPU and amplify host logging.
- **Commit:** `f170899`

**Decision.**

`ctlDispatchPB` logs the **verb and the envelope's size**, not its body, past
`ctlLogMax` — and it did so before any authorization decision, which is exactly
what M115 addressed. Small requests still log in full, which is what makes the
ctl log useful; the cap is where a payload starts being a payload rather than a
command. Verified rather than assumed.

### L26 / L123. Triaged together (LOW)

- **L26. govulncheck scans the workspace dependency graph instead of each module's build graph** (`.github/workflows/govulncheck.yml`), fixed. The workflow changes into each module but does not disable the repository's automatically discovered go.work. As a result, Go package loading and dependency selection can use the workspace-wide MVS graph, allowing a newer dependency required by daemon to mask an older dependency selected by fcguest, protocol, or tui.
- **L123. Release vulnerability scan does not reproduce the CGO-disabled build configuration** (`.github/workflows/govulncheck.yml`), fixed. The release binaries are built with `CGO_ENABLED=0`, but the release vulnerability scan runs without explicitly setting it. On the Ubuntu runner this can analyze the cgo-enabled variant, allowing cgo/!cgo build-tag differences to make the scanned call graph differ from the shipped binaries.
- **Commit:** `f170899`

**Decision.**

`.github/workflows/govulncheck.yml`.

**Confirmed and measured.** `go.work` ties all four modules together, and a go
command run from a module *beneath* it uses workspace mode, where minimal
version selection runs across the union. The manifests genuinely diverge —
`daemon` requires `x/text v0.41.0`; `fcguest`, `protocol` and `tui` require
`v0.40.0`. Measured: `go list -m golang.org/x/text` in `fcguest` returns
**v0.41.0** under the workspace and **v0.40.0** with `GOWORK=off`. So three of
the four modules were scanned at a version they do not ship, and a vulnerability
fixed in v0.41.0 but reachable in v0.40.0 would have passed the release gate.
The workflow comment claiming each module is scanned with its own go.mod was
simply not true.

**Fix:** `GOWORK: "off"` for the scan step, plus `CGO_ENABLED: "0"` to match how
the release binaries are actually built (L123) — cgo changes which files build,
the cgo-guarded variants of `net` and `os/user` among them, so a scan with it
enabled analyses a call graph that is not the one being released. The loop also
prints each module's resolved `x/text` so the log shows which graph was scanned.

### L27 / L32. Triaged together (LOW)

- **L27. Resource and alert caches survive same-name group recreation** (`daemon/resources.go`), fixed. Resource rings, guest-probe results, live-CPU history, and alert hysteresis are keyed only by reusable group name. Destroying and recreating that name can leave prior VM state associated with the replacement, while delayed probes can commit results from the old VM after recreation.
- **L32. Unbounded activity-state retention after group destruction** (`daemon/activity.go`), fixed. `withActivity` allocates a process-global activity entry keyed by group name, but group destruction removes replay and subscriber state without deleting or invalidating that entry. An authorized caller with group lifecycle permissions can repeatedly create, activate, and destroy uniquely named groups, causing retained state to grow for the daemon's lifetime. Reusing a name while an old probe is still active can also let late callbacks update the new group's activity state.
- **Commit:** `f170899`

**Decision.**

`daemon/resources.go`, `daemon/activity.go`, `daemon/groups.go`.

**Confirmed.** `resSweep` prunes against a snapshot taken **before** its
asynchronous guest probes and never takes `groupOpMu`, and `destroy` invalidated
neither the collector's caches nor the activity registry. Group names are
reusable, so a group destroyed and recreated inside one sweep — or a probe
landing after the recreation — handed the replacement the previous group's
samples. The activity map is worse: unbounded, created by any non-empty
transition, never reclaimed by idle completion, so destroyed groups accumulated
forever and a callback from an older incarnation could write into a newer
group's phase.

The alert state is the part with a security consequence rather than a tidiness
one: an alert fires only on a level **increase**, so a replacement inheriting
`critical` gets **no alert** on its own first genuine disk or CPU crossing.

**Fix:** `resForgetGroup` and `activityForget`, called from `destroy` beside
the teardowns already there (`delGoalsFor`, `disarmReport`, `delSchedsFor`,
`dropJobsCache`, `dropGroupTailState`).

Test: `TestDestroyForgetsCollectorAndActivityState` in
`daemon/audit_fixes_test.go`, including that the recreated group's first
crossing fires again.

### L29. Configuration write failures are silently acknowledged, leaving restrictive posture changes unapplied (LOW, already fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/config.go`
- **Finding:** `configCmd` ignores configuration filesystem and serialization errors and unconditionally returns `OK:true`. If an existing permissive configuration cannot be overwritten, restrictive requests such as disabling networking, guest root, or published ports appear successful while runtime readers continue using the prior persisted configuration.
- **Preconditions:** Requires an authorized configuration caller plus an environmental condition such as a read-only state directory, disk-full behavior, interruption, or corruption. The RPC does not appear to let an ordinary remote attacker create that condition, and an authorized caller already has posture-changing privileges. The practical security risk is misleading recovery or incident-response automation while the previous permissive policy remains active.
- **Commit:** `f170899`

**Decision.**

`configCmd` calls `updateGroupConfig`, checks its error and returns
`errResp("write config: …")`; `updateGroupConfig` returns an error from every
step (temp create, write, chmod, close, rename) and commits by **rename**, so
the non-atomic truncate-and-write the finding also names is gone too. Both
halves were closed by the earlier config work; verified against the code.

### L30. Notification timer race allows duplicate autonomous turns (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/notify.go`
- **Finding:** A stale `time.AfterFunc` callback can delete a replacement timer's registration and flush the same pending notification batch concurrently with that replacement callback. Because `flushNotify` snapshots pending results before enqueueing and clearing them, both callbacks can enqueue duplicate autonomous turns.
- **Preconditions:** A guest/agent can trigger the race by generating job completions near the three-second debounce deadline. The stale-callback interleaving requires timing, but repeated completion activity makes attempts practical. The resulting capability is limited to duplicate turns, extra cost, queue/worker pressure, and repeated processing within the originating group/session.
- **Commit:** `f170899`

**Decision.**

`daemon/notify.go`, `recordJobDone`.

**Confirmed.** `Timer.Stop` does not unschedule a callback that is already
runnable, and the old callback carried no identity: it could acquire `notifyMu`
after the replacement was stored, delete the **replacement's** map entry, and
flush. Deleting the entry does not cancel the replacement's own callback, so
both ran, both snapshotted the same pending results before either cleared them,
and one job's completion woke the session twice. A guest that can produce
notify-enabled completions could turn that into extra model turns in its own
group — self-amplification on the one path that exists to wake an agent.

**Fix:** each arming takes a generation, recorded per key; a callback that is no
longer the registered arming returns without flushing, leaving the live one to
do it.

Test: `TestNotifyTimerSupersessionIsOneFlush` in `daemon/audit_fixes_test.go`.

### L31. AttachShell retains handlers and vsock resources after guest termination (LOW, partly fixed, with the limit stated)

- **Where:** `daemon/grpc_server.go`
- **Finding:** The AttachShell handler can leave a server goroutine, gRPC stream state, and the guest connection retained after the guest-to-client forwarding goroutine terminates, because the handler remains blocked in the independent client-side stream.Recv loop until the client closes the RPC.
- **Preconditions:** Requires an authenticated client certificate/token and attach_shell permission for the target group. The caller opens multiple bidirectional streams, keeps their request sides open, and waits for or causes guest-side attachment termination. Each stream can then retain a server handler and guest connection until the caller cancels it or the process is otherwise disrupted.
- **Commit:** `f170899`

**Decision.**

`daemon/grpc_server.go`, `AttachShell`.

**Confirmed.** The guest→client forwarding goroutine returns on guest EOF, a
read failure, an `End` or `Error` frame, or a `Send` the client would not take —
and closed nothing. The handler's deferred `c.Close()` runs only when the
handler returns, and the handler is parked in `stream.Recv()` until the
**client** closes or cancels. An authenticated caller with `attach_shell` could
hold request streams open and accumulate one vsock connection, one guest file
descriptor and one of the group's bounded connection slots per dead attach.

**Fixed:** the forwarder closes the guest connection on the way out, whichever
way it ends. That releases every guest-side resource immediately and also
unwedges the receive loop at the next client message, since `fcWriteFrame` on a
closed connection fails and the handler returns.

**Not fixed, and why:** the handler goroutine and its gRPC stream. A server
cannot cancel a stream context it does not own, and returning with a `Recv` in
flight is outside what grpc-go permits — the same limit recorded under M154.
Their count is bounded by `streamAdmit` (M95): 512 streams per identity, 2048
globally.

### L33. Unvalidated PKI client name enables writes outside the credentials directory (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/pki.go`
- **Finding:** The `pki client` command rejects only an empty client name and then embeds the raw value in output paths. A name containing parent-directory components can escape `credsDir`, allowing the provisioning process to create or truncate writable files outside the intended credentials directory.
- **Preconditions:** Requires local ability to invoke the PKI provisioning command or influence a provisioning wrapper, plus the process's existing filesystem access. No remote unauthenticated path is demonstrated, but a crafted name reliably redirects generated credential writes outside the intended credentials directory.
- **Commit:** `b5f4e1d`

**Decision.**

`daemon/pki.go`, `pkiClient`.

**Confirmed.** The positional name from `koto pki client <name>` reached
`filepath.Join(credsDir, "token-"+name)` and the `client-<name>.{crt,key}`
pattern with no filename-component validation. The fixed prefix eats the first
`..` component, but `../../x` still resolves above `credsDir`, so the writes
landed wherever the caller pointed them — and a name carrying a control
character or a space would have produced material whose allowlist entry and CN
could never be matched again.

**Fix:** `pkiClientNameOK` — letters, digits and `._-`, no `..`, bounded length
— checked inside `pkiClient` so every caller (the CLI and the wizard) is covered
rather than just the one that was noticed.

### L34. Installation copies guest assets with unbounded buffering and destroys sparse-file allocation (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/install.go`
- **Finding:** The first-install asset seeding path uses `os.ReadFile` followed by `os.WriteFile` for checkout-provided guest assets. This can allocate the entire logical source in memory and convert sparse images into fully allocated destination files.
- **Preconditions:** Requires local ability to modify or substitute the checkout used for installation, followed by an operator running `koto install`; no network request or authenticated daemon access is required. A malicious contributor or compromised artifact/build workspace can trigger it, but the prerequisite substantially limits remote exploitability and the impact is primarily installation and host-resource availability.
- **Commit:** `b5f4e1d`

**Decision.**

`daemon/install.go`, `copyFile`.

**Confirmed.** It read the whole source into memory and wrote it back, so
seeding `fcassets/rootfs.img` — ~2 GiB apparent and mostly holes — cost ~2 GiB
of installer memory *and* ~2 GiB of real blocks on the state disk.

**Fix, after a first attempt that did not work.** Streaming with `io.Copy`
between two `*os.File` was measured writing **all 131072 blocks** of a fully
sparse 64 MiB source on this host, so `copy_file_range` is not something to rely
on here — the test caught my own fix. `copySparse` now skips all-zero blocks
explicitly and truncates to the source's size so a trailing hole survives.

The source is also opened `O_NONBLOCK` and judged on the **descriptor**: a plain
`os.Open` on a FIFO blocks at `open(2)`, *before* any mode check can run, which
the same test demonstrated by hanging.

### L35. Delivered attachments are retained indefinitely in each guest workspace (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `fcguest/main.go`
- **Finding:** Each attachment delivery extracts files into the persistent guest `.cs/uploads` directory, while host-side staging limits and cleanup do not limit cumulative guest retention. An authenticated group sender can therefore fill that group's finite workspace with repeated ordinary image uploads.
- **Preconditions:** Requires an authenticated client identity with ACL permission to send to the target group. Each request is a normal bounded image upload; repeated successful deliveries can consume the finite workspace over time. The effect is limited to targeted group(s), but no malformed archive or elevated privilege is required.
- **Commit:** `b5f4e1d`

**Decision.**

`fcguest/main.go`.

**Confirmed.** The host bounds what is *staged and undelivered*
(`maxUploadsPending`, plus a 24-hour orphan sweep), but delivery removes only
the host copy: every image ever sent to a group stayed in its workspace under a
fresh timestamped name. A caller with `send` could fill the group's disk one
valid attachment at a time, and a full guest filesystem remounts read-only and
wedges the agent.

**Fix:** `pruneUploads` trims the guest's uploads directory to
`uploadsKeepBytes` (256 MiB), oldest first, after each delivery. Attachments are
referenced by the turn that delivered them, so what matters is the recent ones;
the budget is far above any conversation's back-reference and far below the
smallest workspace preset.

Test: `TestUploadsArePrunedToABudget` in `fcguest/turn_test.go`.

### L36. Recovery mount drops nosuid/nodev protections on the worker-writable workspace (LOW, already fixed (M138))

- **Where:** `fcguest/main.go`
- **Finding:** When the initial protected workspace mount fails, the mkfs recovery path remounts `/dev/vdb` with flags set to zero. This omits the explicit `MS_NOSUID` and `MS_NODEV` protections applied to a filesystem that receives root-extracted, request-controlled archives and is subsequently used by the worker.
- **Preconditions:** Conditional and low-to-moderate: an attacker needs access to a VM whose initial workspace mount fails or whose image is otherwise forced onto the recovery path. Once active, request-controlled archives can reach the unprotected filesystem and supply device entries, but the demonstrated effect is confined to the guest absent a separate VM escape.
- **Commit:** `b5f4e1d`

**Decision.**

All three mount attempts now carry `wsMountFlags` (`MS_NOSUID|MS_NODEV`) —
fixed as the second half of M138, where the reformat-on-any-error was the first.

### L37. Per-file log ceiling is bypassable by oversized and concurrent appends (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/fc.go`
- **Finding:** `logSinkAppend` checks the existing file size separately from the subsequent append and does not account for the incoming buffer length. It also relies on caller-side locking, while background-tail writers call the sink without that lock. A guest-controlled frame can therefore exceed the nominal 1 GiB per-file ceiling, and concurrent writers can cross it by more than a single frame.
- **Preconditions:** An attacker needs only the normal application capability to submit workload that produces guest turn output or background-task output. No special host access is required. Reaching the 1 GiB boundary requires substantial output because of the configured rate limiter, but once near the boundary a single multi-megabyte frame crosses it, and concurrent sink callers can produce a larger overrun.
- **Commit:** `b5f4e1d`

**Decision.**

`daemon/fc.go` (`logSinkAppend`), `daemon/send.go` (the bg tailer).

**Confirmed, both halves.** `size >= max` let a file one byte under the ceiling
accept a whole chunk and finish over it — by up to the chunk size, every time a
writer tried. And `tailBackgroundTask` reached `logSinkAppend` **without**
`logWriteLock`, so its size check and write were not atomic against the turn
sink or the marker flush: two writers could both see a below-limit size and both
append past it, and a `[[bg]]` line landing between another writer's open and
write could split a marker.

**Fix:** the arithmetic includes the buffer (`size+len(b) > max`), and the bg
tailer takes the same per-path lock every other writer of that stream takes.

### L38. Unnormalized multiline input escapes the framed prompt box (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/view.go`
- **Finding:** Embedded newline characters are retained in logical input rows, while the renderer frames each row as one physical terminal line. A recalled multiline prompt or persisted draft can therefore emit unframed terminal lines and exceed the prompt's row budget.
- **Preconditions:** Low. The attacker needs to influence a prompt event or history item visible to the victim, and the victim must recall it, or the victim must load a multiline persisted draft. The resulting primitive is terminal UI spoofing and layout disruption; VT control-sequence execution is not demonstrated because control characters are scrubbed and are not needed for the newline issue.
- **Commit:** `b5f4e1d`

**Decision.**

`tui/view.go`, `wrapInput`.

**Confirmed.** Rows were broken on width only, so an embedded newline went into
a row verbatim — and `drawBox` writes each row between one pair of borders, so
the terminal produced extra physical lines with no borders and no place in the
layout. `inputRows` and `maxInputRows` size the chat viewport from the
**logical** row count, so those lines were unaccounted for and pushed the frame
out of shape. Persisted drafts and recalled prompt history both reach this path.

**Fix:** a newline ends the row.

Test: `TestMultilineInputStaysInsideTheBox` in `tui/input_test.go` checks the
rows, the cursor's row, and that a three-line draft leaves the rendered frame
exactly as tall as the terminal.

### L39. History RPC performs uncancellable log replay after client cancellation (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/grpc_server.go`
- **Finding:** The History handler ignores the gRPC request context and performs a synchronous, full replay of every history stream before applying the requested page limit. An authorized client can submit many History calls and cancel them after admission, leaving the server to continue reading, parsing, merging, sorting, and converting each result without a history-specific concurrency limit.
- **Preconditions:** Requires a valid client certificate, matching bearer token, and ACL grant for history on the target group. Once authorized, exploitation is straightforward over the gRPC endpoint: submit parallel History calls and cancel them after admission. The per-stream 4 MiB tail cap limits individual work, but there is no observed application-level per-history concurrency or rate limit.
- **Commit:** `b5f4e1d`

**Decision.**

`daemon/grpc_server.go`, `daemon/logtail.go`.

**Confirmed.** The handler discarded the request context, so a client could fire
parallel `History` calls and cancel them the moment they reached the handler
while the daemon read every one of the group's eleven streams, parsed all of it,
allocated the events and sorted the merge — the whole cost, for a reply nobody
would take. A small `limit` does not avoid it: paging is applied to the result,
not to the read.

**Fix:** `readHistoryCtx` checks cancellation between streams, which is where
the work is, and the handler checks before and after. Limits are still applied
to whatever was read, so a cancelled call returns consistent data rather than a
half-built slice.

### L40. Warning-only raw RunScript mode permits terminal escape injection (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/ctl_cli.go`
- **Finding:** `koto ctl runscript -raw` forwards guest-controlled output directly to stdout even when stdout is an interactive terminal. Although the CLI detects this unsafe condition, it only warns, while the daemon disables terminal-output sanitization for raw requests.
- **Preconditions:** Low: exploitation requires a valid administrator client, access to a hostile or compromised guest group, and an explicit `-raw` invocation with stdout attached to a terminal. No unauthenticated or ordinary tenant path is shown, but the warning-only check permits the unsafe operation once those conditions hold.
- **Commit:** `b5f4e1d`

**Decision.**

`daemon/ctl_cli.go`.

**Confirmed.** `-raw` is documented for binary output being redirected to a
file, and on a TTY it was allowed with a warning printed to stderr — *before*
the output that would do the damage, which is no protection at all. The guest
authors those bytes, so it could set the title, rewrite the screen, put data on
the clipboard via OSC 52, or trigger whatever the emulator does with an untested
sequence.

**Fix:** refused, with the redirection spelled out. Redirecting is the whole
documented use case, so the legitimate caller pays a `>`.

### L41. Goal names can shadow goal IDs in control operations (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/goals.go`
- **Finding:** A caller can create a goal whose name equals another goal's 12-character ID. Group-scoped goal operations then resolve that token by first match rather than rejecting the name/ID collision, so an operation presented as targeting the named goal can mutate the older goal.
- **Preconditions:** Low. An attacker must be authorized to create a goal in a group and must cause an operator or coordinator to use the colliding displayed name. An attacker with direct transition permission can already address the existing goal by its ID. The resulting impact is limited to integrity/disruption of goals in that same authorized group, but the collision is deterministic once an ID is observed from GoalList or a response/event.
- **Commit:** `b5f4e1d`

**Decision.**

`daemon/goals.go`.

**Confirmed.** IDs are 12 hex characters; names may be up to 16 and may be
hexadecimal; and `resolveGoalLocked` matches `Name == want || ID == want`,
returning the first record in creation order. A goal named after an existing
goal's id was therefore permanently unreachable by its own name — every
`approve`, `pause`, `interrupt`, `resume` and `cancel` aimed at it landed on the
older goal instead.

**Fix:** a name may not wear an id's shape. The two namespaces share one lookup,
so they cannot share a shape; the refusal says exactly that.

### L42. Denied `/destroy` leaks the existing subscription and opens a replacement stream (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/model.go`
- **Finding:** The TUI deletes `m.subscribed[group]` before the destroy RPC succeeds. When a scoped user is denied destroy, the response still refreshes the group list, causing a new `SubscribeGroup` stream to be opened while the old stream remains active. Repeating the denied command accumulates live subscriptions and duplicate event processing.
- **Preconditions:** Requires an authenticated user with TUI access, list permission, and subscribe_group permission for a target group, but without destroy permission. The attacker can repeat the denied command without victim interaction or secret disclosure. The effect grows with the number of commands and with subsequent group event activity.
- **Commit:** `b5f4e1d`

**Decision.**

`tui/model.go`.

**Confirmed.** The local wipe ran **optimistically**, and it cleared
`m.subscribed[target]` — the guard that stops a second `SubscribeGroup` stream
being opened for the same group. A denied destroy therefore left the group alive
with its guard gone, and the unconditional `listCmd` on the response opened a
replacement stream while the first stayed live with no cancellation handle. Each
denied attempt leaked one more, and every event of that group was then delivered
to all of them.

**Fix:** nothing local until the daemon has actually destroyed it. The success
branch does the teardown — now `forgetGroup` (M155), which is a superset of the
hand-written wipe it replaces — and moves the focus; the failure branch changes
nothing at all.

### L43. OAuth credential path mismatch can make the daemon use an unintended account (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/proxy.go`
- **Finding:** The CLI resolves its default OAuth credential file beneath `KOTO_HOME`, while the daemon resolves its default beneath the process `HOME`. A manual or development launch with different values can therefore make login/status succeed against one credential file while proxy requests authenticate with another. Relative `CRED_PATH` values create a similar mismatch when the CLI and daemon have different working directories.
- **Preconditions:** Low: the mismatch requires an operator-controlled deployment or development invocation with inconsistent environment variables or working directories. An attacker cannot select the credential path through the demonstrated behavior alone, but a party already able to submit agent traffic can cause requests to use the unintended credential and upstream identity in an affected deployment.
- **Commit:** `ae05d91`

**Decision.**

`daemon/proxy.go`, `proxyInitPaths`.

**Confirmed.** `authOAuthPath` (what `koto claude-login` writes and verifies)
derives from `KOTO_HOME`; `proxyInitPaths` derived from `os.UserHomeDir()`. The
unit and the Make targets keep those consistent, but nothing enforced it for a
daemon started by hand — and the divergence is silent and expensive: an operator
could log in, watch it verify, and have the live proxy go on using a **different
account's** token from their personal `~/.claude`. Other identity, other quota,
other bill, with nothing on either side saying so.

**Fix:** prefer `<state>/.claude/.credentials.json` when it exists, so the daemon
agrees with the command that writes it whatever `HOME` happens to be. The `$HOME`
fallback stays for a state dir with no credential, and `CRED_PATH` still
overrides both. Checked against the live install, where `<state>/.claude` is the
installer's symlink to `creds/` and the file is there.

### L44. Authenticated SubscribeLogs clients can retain unbounded subscribers and exhaust daemon resources (LOW, already fixed (M95, L156))

- **Where:** `daemon/grpc_server.go`
- **Finding:** SubscribeLogs allocates and globally registers a buffered subscriber for every authorized stream without per-client, per-connection, or global admission limits. Cleanup occurs only after the stream handler returns, while log delivery traverses every retained subscriber. A client with subscribe_logs permission can therefore retain many streams and consume daemon memory, goroutines, sockets, and per-log fan-out CPU.
- **Preconditions:** Low to moderate: exploitation requires a valid allowlisted client certificate and an identity authorized for subscribe_logs, but one client can create many server-streaming RPCs and retain them without further victim interaction.
- **Commit:** `ae05d91`

**Decision.**

Every streaming RPC goes through `authStream`, whose `streamAdmit` caps
concurrent streams at 512 per identity and 2048 globally — SubscribeLogs
included, and named as such in that code's own comment. A subscriber that stops
reading is no longer silent either: L156 added the per-subscriber drop counter
and the notice. The residual — a handler blocked inside `stream.Send` cannot be
reclaimed from this side — is the grpc-go limit recorded under M154.

### L45. Unbounded cron representation enables authenticated schedule-store resource exhaustion (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/schedules.go`
- **Finding:** The schedule-add path validates a trimmed/tokenized cron expression but persists the original unbounded string. An authorized caller can submit a valid five-field expression surrounded by large whitespace, causing each schedule entry to consume excessive persistent storage and making every subsequent addition perform increasingly expensive full-file JSON serialization and rewriting.
- **Preconditions:** Requires a valid mTLS client/token identity with `sched_add` permission for a group, or control of a guest possessing that permission. The transport and per-group limits make the impact finite, but repeated accepted requests can still create substantial persistent data and increasingly costly full-store rewrites.
- **Commit:** `ae05d91`

**Decision.**

`daemon/schedules.go`, `addSched`.

**Confirmed.** `parseCron` `TrimSpace`/`Fields` its way past arbitrary
whitespace, so a megabyte of blanks around `* * * * *` is a *valid* expression —
and `addSched` stored it verbatim, in a file `saveSched` rewrites in **full** on
every addition, so the cost is paid again on each one.

**Fix:** `schedCronMax` (256 bytes) in `addSched`, which is where both the RPC
and the ctl plane's `sched_add` arrive.

### L46. Pending job notification can restart a group after `/stop` (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/notify.go`
- **Finding:** A debounce timer created by `recordJobDone` survives `stopGroup`. When it fires after the stop has drained queues and powered off the VM, `flushNotify` creates a new queue entry; the worker then calls `sendNow`, whose first lifecycle operation is `ensure`, restarting the group.
- **Preconditions:** Low: the attacker must control or induce a job completion in the affected guest and race or precede an operator stop. The effect is confined to that group and requires no host credentials, but it can reliably occur with a completion already pending when stop is invoked.
- **Commit:** `ae05d91`

**Decision.**

`daemon/notify.go`, `daemon/groups.go`.

**Confirmed.** `stopGroup` discards the queued messages and cancels the in-flight
turns — the whole point being that the VM stays down — but a job completion that
arrived just before the stop sat in `notifyPending` with its debounce timer
ticking, and the flush that followed is an `enqueueSend`, whose first act is
`ensure()`. So the group booted again seconds after the operator stopped it,
from a send the operator never made. The documented "a future send boots a
stopped group" rule is about work someone *chooses* to do.

**Fix:** `dropPendingJobNotifications` in `stopGroupPrepare`, beside the
autostart revocation that is there for the same reason — results discarded,
timers stopped, and their generations dropped so a callback `Stop` could not
unschedule finds itself superseded (L30).

### L47. Sequence cursor is reused across daemon/group lifecycles, suppressing the required gap (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/events.go`
- **Finding:** `replayFrom` treats a retained client cursor as current when it numerically equals the newly reset per-group counter. Because the counter and replay ring are keyed only by group name and kept in memory, daemon restart or group destroy/recreate can make new-lifecycle events collide with the old cursor and prevent the TUI's history-recovery path from running.
- **Preconditions:** Low. The condition is reachable when a daemon or group lifecycle resets the in-memory counter and the new lifecycle emits the same or a greater number of events as the retained client cursor. It is primarily an operational/reliability integrity issue rather than an attacker-controlled privilege or data-exfiltration primitive.
- **Commit:** `ae05d91`

**Decision.**

`daemon/events.go`.

**Confirmed.** The sequence space is process-local: destroy deletes the counter,
a daemon restart recreates the map empty, and group names are reusable. And
`replayFrom` answered `since == cur` with "you are up to date" **before** proving
the cursor belonged to this sequence space at all. A client holding N from a
previous incarnation, against a new one that had emitted exactly N events, got
neither replay nor a `gap` — and went on believing its transcript was current.

**Fix, without a protocol change:** each incarnation's sequence space starts at a
random base, and `ringFloor` starts with it, so a cursor from an earlier
incarnation lands **below the floor** and is answered with the `gap` the client
already knows how to handle. The continuity check now runs before the
up-to-date check, which is what makes the floor decisive.

The ring tests pin the base to 1 through `seqBaseFn` so their explicit
arithmetic survives; one existing test of mine that assumed sequences start at 1
was corrected to take its cursor from the floor.

Test: `TestStaleCursorAcrossIncarnationsGetsAGap` in
`daemon/audit_fixes_test.go` stages exactly the coincidence — ten events, a
destroy-and-recreate, ten more — and asserts a gap, while a live cursor still
replays and a head cursor is still up to date.

### L48. Scheduled occurrences are committed before queue admission and are permanently lost on overflow (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/schedules.go`
- **Finding:** The cron loop advances and persists `LastFiredAt`/`NextDueAt` before `fireSchedule` attempts the bounded queue. If the queue is full, enqueue fails synchronously, only an error is logged, and the occurrence is never retried.
- **Preconditions:** Low-complexity authenticated attack. A user must have permission to target the group and either submit enough queued work or configure multiple same-minute schedules. The effect is normally scoped to that group and causes missed scheduled actions rather than code execution or data disclosure.
- **Commit:** `ae05d91`

**Decision.**

`daemon/schedules.go`.

**Confirmed.** `cronLoop` recorded the occurrence as fired and advanced
`NextDueAt` **before** delivery, and `fireSchedule` discarded the enqueue error.
So an occurrence the live queue refused was skipped until the next cron
recurrence — and a `@daily` or one-shot schedule could simply never run. The
documented no-catch-up rule covers daemon **downtime**, not work the delivery
queue rejected.

**Fix:** `rearmSchedule` puts it back due for the next tick, and only when the
schedule still exists, is still enabled, and still carries the due time this
fire advanced it to — a concurrent `sched_del` or re-time owns its own state.

### L49. Goal handoff capture replays and sorts every active log stream (LOW, accepted)

- **Where:** `daemon/logtail.go`
- **Finding:** Each goal turn invokes `goalCaptureHandoff`, which calls `readHistory(g, 0, 0)` and scans every existing group and slot log before the requested session is filtered. With all 11 streams populated, the 4 MiB per-stream tail permits roughly 44 MiB of parsing input per capture, and multiple active goal drivers can repeat that work.
- **Preconditions:** A guest-level attacker needs execution in a group VM and must cause or control a goal worker. They can start multiple goals within the existing eight-goal limit and emit output through the supported turn/log path. The daemon's per-group output-rate, active-goal, slot, and per-stream-tail limits constrain the attack, but do not prevent repeated aggregate replay work.
- **Commit:** `ae05d91`

**Decision.**

`daemon/goals.go`, `goalCaptureHandoff`.

The facts are right: it calls `readHistory(g, 0, 0)`, which reads, parses, merges
and sorts **every** stream of the group, and then keeps one session's events.

**Accepted, because the cheap fixes do not work and the real one is a design
change.** A session's turns run in whichever slot was free, so there is no
subset of streams that can be read instead; `limit` is applied after the merge,
so a small page does not reduce the read; and the events are only attributable
to a session *after* they are parsed. Making this O(1 stream) means indexing the
transcript by session — a change to how koto stores conversations, not an audit
fix.

What bounds it is already in place and is what keeps this low: `historyTailCap`
caps each stream at 4 MiB, `groupSlots` caps the streams at eleven, the goal cap
is eight per group, and `fcLogSinkWait` rate-limits what a guest can put in them
in the first place. The cost is one bounded read at the end of a goal turn,
which is the slowest thing in the loop by orders of magnitude already.

### L50. Per-command Bash timeout does not terminate descendant processes (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `sidecar/venice_stream.js`
- **Finding:** The timeout in `execBash` signals only the directly spawned Bash process, so ordinary commands can leave descendants running and holding inherited output pipes after the advertised 30-second limit.
- **Preconditions:** A model-generated shell command can do this without special privileges, but the attacker must be able to influence a session or prompt that causes the Bash tool to run. The effect is bounded by the guest/container and worker lifecycle, though descendants can outlive the per-command timeout and affect subsequent work until they exit or the guest is torn down.
- **Commit:** `ae05d91`

**Decision.**

`sidecar/venice_stream.js`.

**Confirmed and demonstrated.** The tool spawned bash without its own process
group and signalled the bash pid alone on timeout, so a command that forked or
backgrounded anything left those children running — free to keep burning the
guest's CPU and writing to `/workspace` after the tool call had reported a
timeout. And a descendant holding the inherited stdout or stderr keeps the pipe
open, so the `close` event the Promise waits on does not arrive until **it**
exits either.

Measured, same command both ways (`(sleep 5; touch marker) & sleep 30`):

| | `close` event | descendant |
|---|---|---|
| signal the pid (old) | **never arrived** | **survived**, wrote its marker |
| signal the group (new) | arrived promptly | died with it |

**Fix:** `detached: true` puts bash in its own process group, and the timeout
signals the negative pid — the whole tree — falling back to the bare pid if the
group is already gone.

### L51. Concurrent clear can resurrect deleted Venice history (LOW, already fixed (M60))

- **Where:** `sidecar/venice_stream.js`
- **Finding:** An in-flight Venice worker can rewrite a transcript after `/clear` deletes its history file.
- **Preconditions:** Requires authenticated access to the affected group or session and timing a clear while a Venice turn is in flight, such as during a slow provider request or tool execution. No special filesystem privilege or cross-tenant access is required beyond the existing workspace access.
- **Commit:** `ae05d91`

**Decision.**

`clearFence` closes admission for the scope, discards its queued messages,
**cancels its in-flight turns and waits for them to retire**, and only then is
the guest state deleted. A Venice worker whose turn is cancelled is signalled
(SIGINT, escalating to SIGKILL) and gone before `clearSessionContext` removes the
history file, so there is no worker left to write a stale array back. The
residual is the one `clearFence` already documents and logs: a turn that will not
die inside `clearFenceWait` is the stall path's problem.

### L52. Untrusted PATH controls `du` execution during uninstall (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/uninstall.go`
- **Finding:** `uninstallStateSummary` invokes `du` by name, allowing an attacker who can influence the uninstalling user's PATH or a writable directory in that PATH to execute a malicious helper with the user's privileges. The invocation occurs before the purge confirmation, so declining the deletion does not prevent execution.
- **Preconditions:** Requires local influence over the uninstalling user's inherited PATH or write access to a directory earlier in that PATH, plus the operator running uninstall. No authentication bypass or root execution is demonstrated; the payload runs with the normal invoking user's privileges.
- **Commit:** `ae05d91`

**Decision.**

`daemon/uninstall.go`.

**Confirmed.** `exec.Command("du", …)` resolves through `PATH`, and this runs
**before** the confirmation prompt and before any sudo, as the operator — so a
`du` planted in a writable `PATH` directory executed with their privileges purely
because they typed `koto uninstall`.

**Fix:** an absolute path (`/usr/bin/du`, then `/bin/du`), checked to be a
regular file. The size is a courtesy line in the prompt; if `du` is not where it
belongs, the uninstall does without it rather than resolving one.

### L53. Unescaped dynamic values allow TUI debug-log injection (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/debuglog.go`
- **Finding:** The TUI writes attacker-influenced text directly into a line-oriented debug log. Embedded newlines are preserved, allowing forged physical records and ambiguity for operators following the documented log file.
- **Preconditions:** Low: exploitation requires influence over text reaching the TUI logger, such as guest/provider/daemon output, plus access to the operator's local debug-log viewing workflow. The TUI's mTLS and bearer-token connection prevent an unauthenticated network caller from directly submitting arbitrary daemon responses; local environment and slash-command sources require local control.
- **Commit:** `a0e9edd`

**Decision.**

`tui/debuglog.go`.

**Confirmed.** `dbgWrite` wrote group names, session names, slash-command
arguments and daemon error strings into `run/tui/tui.log` verbatim. That file is
read with `tail -f` in a real terminal — the one thing the TUI's own renderer
cannot sanitize on the operator's behalf, because it never passes through it.

**Fix:** `flattenLogValue` — `scrubVTStrict` plus folding every line break to a
space, so one logged value is one log line and carries no escape.

### L54. Uninstall can purge a running daemon when its fixed unit file is absent (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/uninstall.go`
- **Finding:** `runUninstall` treats `/etc/systemd/system/koto.service` existence as proof that the daemon needs stopping. A daemon started manually, through another unit path, or whose unit file was removed can remain active while uninstall removes its executable and `--purge` deletes its live state and environment file.
- **Preconditions:** Requires local access to run the uninstall command and, for the destructive state impact, explicit `--purge` (or an existing administrative service setup). No unauthenticated remote exploit is demonstrated; the issue is an unsafe lifecycle boundary and can occur during ordinary manual/alternate-unit operation or after unit-file removal.
- **Commit:** `a0e9edd`

**Decision.**

`daemon/uninstall.go`.

**Confirmed.** The stop step was guarded by `exists(unitPath)` alone — a question
about a *file*, not about whether a daemon is running. With the unit gone
(removed by hand, an interrupted earlier uninstall, systemd still holding it
loaded after a `rm` with no `daemon-reload`, or a daemon started straight from
the binary) the stop was skipped silently and the run went on to delete the
binaries and, under `--purge`, `os.RemoveAll(state)` — out from under a live
daemon and its live microVMs. Every workspace image left dirty, which is exactly
the failure the stop-first ordering documented at the top of that file exists to
prevent.

**Fix:** three signals instead of one.

- The stop runs when the unit file exists **or** `systemctl is-active koto` says
  systemd still has it running.
- A **post-stop verification** scans `/proc` for a live `koto daemon` and refuses
  the whole uninstall if one is serving this state directory — a successful
  `systemctl stop` is not the sentence "no daemon is serving this state dir".
- The scan reads `/proc` directly rather than shelling out to `pgrep`, because
  that is what lets it tell *which* state dir a daemon serves (`KOTO_HOME` from
  its environ, else its cwd — exactly how `kotoHome()` resolves it). A dev-clone
  daemon under `.dev/` must not block the uninstall of an installed one, and the
  two coexist by design.

Under `--dry-run` it warns rather than refusing, since nothing was stopped.
Pinned by `TestUninstallSeesADaemonWithNoUnitFile`, which starts a stand-in with
the real thing's `/proc` shape and checks both the match and the non-match.

### L55. WatchState change detection can suppress changed client-visible job state (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/events.go`
- **Finding:** WatchState uses an incomplete and non-injective state fingerprint as its sole publication gate. It omits client-visible fields such as job command, start time, and global token rate, and concatenates guest-influenced fields with unescaped delimiters. Distinct snapshots can therefore produce the same fingerprint, causing subscribers to retain stale state.
- **Preconditions:** A process running in a guest can modify its own job metadata files and can deterministically create delimiter-containing values. An attached WatchState client is then left with stale state when only omitted fields change or when a collision is created. Exploitation requires guest/job execution access and affects monitoring clients rather than host authorization or code execution.
- **Commit:** `a0e9edd`

**Decision.**

`daemon/events.go`.

**Confirmed.** The hash joined variable-length fields with `':'` and `'|'`, both
of which occur naturally in job metadata — and job metadata is written by the
*guest*, in agent-writable files. `status="running"`,`rc="0:1"` and
`status="running:0"`,`rc="1"` hashed identically, so a transition between two
such states pushed **no** `WatchState` frame: every attached client kept
rendering the stale one until some other field happened to move.

Two fields were also simply missing from the hash while riding in the frame —
`Cmd` and `Started` — so a change a client can see was one the daemon had
decided not to tell it about.

**Fix:** a length-prefixed field writer (`%d:%s|`), so no value can impersonate
a boundary, plus `Cmd`, `Started` and the fleet-wide tok/s rate. Pinned by
`TestStateHashCannotBeCollidedByJobMetadata`.

### L56. History RPC accepts an unbounded positive page size (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/grpc_server.go`
- **Finding:** A history-authorized client can set `HistoryReq.limit` to a very large positive value, bypassing the intended 1000-event default and causing the daemon to parse, materialize, serialize, and return all events found in the bounded tails of the group's history streams.
- **Preconditions:** Requires a valid allowlisted mTLS client certificate, bearer token, and history ACL permission for a target group. A single standard unary RPC with `limit=2147483647` is sufficient; concurrent authenticated requests amplify resource consumption. No malformed protobuf or special log content is required.
- **Commit:** `a0e9edd`

**Decision.**

`daemon/logtail.go`.

**Confirmed.** Only a *non-positive* limit was replaced with the 1000 default; a
client-chosen `2147483647` therefore disabled tail trimming entirely, so a single
request parsed, copied into protobuf objects and serialised everything eleven
streams' `historyTailCap` could yield — and repeated calls multiplied it.

**Fix:** `historyLimitMax = 5000`, generous next to anything a client renders.
Pinned by `TestHistoryLimitIsBoundedAtBothEnds`.

### L57. Self pause/resume resets a goal's MaxIterations budget (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/goals.go`
- **Finding:** A non-main group can pause and resume its own goal, and resuming resets the persisted iteration counter. Since the goal driver enforces MaxIterations only against that counter, a compromised or prompt-injected group agent can repeatedly reset its own budget and keep the goal running beyond its configured execution limit.
- **Preconditions:** Moderate: exploitation requires code execution in, or successful prompt injection of, a running non-main group agent and access to that group's control FIFO. Once those prerequisites exist, the cycle is simple and repeatable without operator approval or a reset limit.
- **Commit:** `a0e9edd`

**Decision.**

`daemon/goals.go`.

**Confirmed.** `goalResume` set `it.Iteration = 0`. The driver stops when
`Iteration` reaches `MaxIterations`, so zeroing it made the configured budget
unreachable — and this is *not* only an operator's own foot: a non-main group may
`goal_pause` and `goal_resume` its **own** goal over the ctl plane (the
self-scoped `goal_*` verbs), so alternating the two keeps a goal iterating
forever, burning worker slots, provider spend and the group's own capacity with
the limit never arriving.

**Fix:** the count carries across the pause. Resume means "carry on", and
carrying on includes the count. `TestGoalResumeKeepsTheIterationBudget` pins it
(negative control: the old line runs 6 work turns where the fix runs the 1 left
in the budget); `TestCtlGoalSetAndStatusFromMain` asserted the old reset and was
corrected.

### L58. Unbounded completed-job metadata can exhaust daemon and TUI resources (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/jobs.go`
- **Finding:** Completed `/workspace/.cs/jobs/*` directories are retained indefinitely, and refresh, hashing, protobuf conversion, and TUI ingestion process every retained record without count, byte, age, or message-size limits.
- **Preconditions:** An authenticated user with access to a running group can invoke `cs-job run` repeatedly, directly or through the guest agent. The attacker does not need to exceed the concurrent limit; short-lived jobs complete and remain on disk. Impact grows with the number of retained directories and affects connected daemon/TUI observers.
- **Commit:** `a0e9edd`

**Decision.**

`daemon/jobs.go`.

**Confirmed.** `CS_MAX_JOBS` caps jobs **running**, not jobs that have ever run,
and a completed job directory stays listable until `cs-job clean` — so an agent
minting short-lived jobs grows a list that is parsed, cached, hashed on every
state tick, serialised into every `List` and changed `WatchState` frame, and
copied by every attached TUI.

**Fix:** `jobsMaxPerGroup = 256`, trimmed from the oldest end after the sort, so
the mirror keeps the jobs the tree actually shows. Pinned by
`TestParsedJobMirrorIsBounded`.

### L59. Concurrent schedule additions bypass the per-group quota (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/schedules.go`
- **Finding:** `addSched` checks the per-group schedule count under one lock acquisition, releases the lock while building the item, and later appends under a separate lock acquisition. Concurrent authorized callers can therefore all pass the quota check and persist more than `schedMaxPerGroup` schedules.
- **Preconditions:** Requires an authenticated principal or guest sidecar already authorized to invoke `sched_add` for the target group. The attacker sends multiple valid schedule-add requests concurrently while the group is below the cap; no secret or transport bypass is needed. A single burst can exceed the cap, and the persisted schedules continue generating work according to their cron expressions.
- **Commit:** `a0e9edd`

**Decision.**

`daemon/schedules.go`. The per-group and daemon-wide caps were read in one
`schedLock` acquisition and the append made in another, so concurrent `sched_add`
calls could each observe `n < cap` and all commit. Both caps are now re-checked
inside the same acquisition as the append.

### L60. Network-enabled guest traffic lacks a per-guest daemon resource budget (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/fcnet.go`
- **Finding:** A process running in a network-enabled guest can continuously send size-bounded Ethernet frames to the host gateway. The daemon allocates and classifies each frame, records flow information even for policy-blocked traffic, and forwards permitted traffic into the daemon-resident gVisor network stack. VM cgroup limits do not directly budget this host-side work, and no per-guest packet, byte, work, or excessive-drop limit is enforced at the gateway boundary.
- **Preconditions:** A process with code execution inside a guest configured with network access can run the proof of concept without additional host authentication. Socket and VM scheduling backpressure may limit the absolute rate, but every accepted frame still causes daemon-side work and no per-guest aggregate budget is shown at this boundary.
- **Commit:** `a0e9edd`

**Decision.**

`daemon/fcnet.go`.

**Confirmed.** Every frame a networked guest emits costs the daemon an
allocation, a policy classification, a flow-table lookup and a netstack parse —
all in the daemon process, beside the gRPC server and the proxy every *other*
group depends on. vsock backpressure bounds what is **queued**, not the work rate
a guest may demand, so a guest with code execution could take a share of the
host's scheduling capacity simply by sending. Denied frames are not cheaper:
rejecting costs the classification too.

**Fix:** a per-connection token bucket on both frames and bytes
(`fcTokenBucket`), charged before the frame is judged. Two deliberate choices:

- It **throttles rather than drops** — the reader stops reading until its tokens
  refill and the pressure propagates back down vsock to the guest's own pump.
  Dropping would be indistinguishable from the egress filter's DROP and would
  turn a permitted TCP stream into a retransmit storm: more host work, not less.
- The numbers are **loose on purpose** — 64 MiB/s and 100k frames/s per guest,
  an OOM/CPU backstop rather than a shaper, the same call the proxy's
  concurrency caps made (M2). A networked group exists to do real work (a clone,
  an `npm install`, a container pull) and a limit tight enough to shape that
  traffic would make ordinary turns mysteriously slow. The frame cap is the one
  that binds, because the cheapest flood is the smallest frame.

Pinned by `TestGuestFramePumpHasAWorkBudget`, including that an oversized charge
cannot wedge the link forever.

### L61. Global security prompt failure is fail-open (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/prompt.go`
- **Finding:** The daemon treats a missing or unreadable harness-controlled global prompt as a valid partial prompt and still delivers the turn to a Claude worker launched with permission checks disabled.
- **Preconditions:** Requires an installation, upgrade, or filesystem condition that makes the harness prompt unavailable, followed by a user authorized to submit a turn. A remote user generally cannot create the missing-file condition through this code path alone; exploitation is therefore low-likelihood, but all subsequent turns are affected until the prompt is restored or the daemon is fixed or restarted.
- **Commit:** `a0e9edd`

**Decision.**

`daemon/prompt.go`, `daemon/install.go`.

**Confirmed, and it is the worst of this batch.** `composeSystemPrompt`
discarded the `os.ReadFile` error for `prompts/global.md`; the empty string was
then simply not appended, `sendNow` called `fcSendMsg` unconditionally, and the
guest launched `claude --dangerously-skip-permissions` with **no harness
policy** — indistinguishable, from every observable, from a healthy turn. The
installer's prompt copy discarded every error it could produce (`ReadDir`,
`ReadFile`, both `WriteFile`s), so an install that never delivered the file
reported success.

**Fix, both halves:**

- `composeSystemPrompt` logs at **error** — which reaches the operator as a
  banner through `logalert`, and is rate-limited there — naming the path, for an
  unreadable file *and* for an empty one, since an empty policy is the same
  failure wearing a readable disguise. It cannot refuse the turn from where it
  sits (it returns a string, and its callers treat it as one), so saying so
  loudly on every turn until it is fixed is the available answer.
- `koto install` treats `global.md` as **required**: a read or write failure is
  returned as an error, the other prompt files warn, and the step ends by
  asserting the file exists rather than reporting a successful install that left
  every group unpoliced.

`TestMissingHarnessPolicyIsLoud` pins all three cases (missing, empty, healthy).

### L62. Persisted group and session names permit terminal escape injection (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/view.go`
- **Finding:** The TUI sanitizes persisted draft text but restores the persisted current group and session names verbatim. These values are concatenated into status and banner strings and passed through lipgloss, which styles text without escaping embedded terminal control sequences.
- **Preconditions:** Requires write access to the TUI runtime directory, normally equivalent local-user access or a trusted/compromised runtime peer, followed by TUI startup or state reload. No remote unauthenticated path is demonstrated, but the values are accepted without validation and emitted to the operator's terminal once loaded.
- **Commit:** `a0e9edd`

**Decision.**

`tui/persist.go`.

**Confirmed.** `loadState` scrubbed only `Draft`; `newModel` copied `Cur` and
every `Sessions` entry straight into the live model, and `renderStatusLeft` and
the empty-conversation banner emitted them. Mono mode is not a defense — it
removes color parameters, not OSC or CSI.

**Fix:** `loadState` scrubs `Cur` and every `Sessions` key and value with the
same function `Draft` already went through. Pinned by
`TestPersistedNamesAndDebugLogAreScrubbed`, which covers L53 as well.

### L63. Malformed extended-background SGR bypasses theme background reassertion (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/theme.go`
- **Finding:** Malformed extended-color SGR attributes such as `ESC[0;48m` and `ESC[0;48;3m` retain code `48` while being marked invalid. Because `sgrClearsBg` ignores that invalid flag, it treats them as valid background setters and fails to reassert the configured TUI background after the reset.
- **Preconditions:** An attacker must be able to influence text rendered by a sidecar/event or another untrusted output source, and an operator must be viewing the themed TUI. The sanitizer preserves the required SGR form, so no additional terminal-control bypass is needed. The resulting effect is reliable visual-state manipulation with no demonstrated code execution or arbitrary terminal input injection.
- **Commit:** `13c150a`

**Decision.**

`tui/theme.go`.

**Confirmed.** `sgrExtended` marks an incomplete or unknown `38`/`48`/`58`
introducer *bad* without changing its code, and `sgrClearsBg` treated every `48`
as a background setter. So in `ESC[0;48m` the reset cleared the tracked
background and the malformed `48` then re-marked one as set; `reassertBg` omitted
the theme ground and the rest of the line rendered on the terminal's default
background. Guest output reaches this — the daemon's sanitizer deliberately
preserves digit-and-semicolon SGR sequences, malformed ones included.

**Fix:** a bad attribute sets nothing. A sequence that touches no background at
all still reads as "not a clear", since there is nothing to reassert after it.
Pinned by `TestMalformedBackgroundSGRStillReassertsTheGround`.

### L64. Destroy reports success even when workspace deletion fails (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/groups.go`
- **Finding:** The destroy path ignores registry-persistence and filesystem-cleanup errors, then always returns success. If workspace removal fails, the prior workspace image can remain and be reused when the same group name is later created, exposing retained workspace data to the newly authorized group.
- **Preconditions:** Requires an authenticated client with permission to destroy the group and a cleanup failure such as a protected, immutable, mounted, or otherwise undeletable workspace. Once cleanup fails, same-name recreation reaches the stale-image reuse path without further exploit sophistication; the resulting exposure depends on a different authorized client later reusing that name.
- **Commit:** `13c150a`

**Decision.**

`daemon/groups.go`.

**Confirmed.** `_ = os.RemoveAll(vol(g))`. `fcEnsureWorkspaceImg` treats an
existing `groups/<name>/workspace.img` as authoritative and reuses or grows it,
and **group names are reusable** — so a permission, EIO, immutable-flag or busy-
mount failure left the destroyed group's entire workspace in place for the *next*
group of that name to mount, while the operator had been told the data was gone.

**Fix:** the error is checked, and the *name* is what carries the hazard, so a
surviving directory is renamed to `<g>.undeleted-<unix>`. A later spawn then
starts clean even though the bytes are still on disk, and the response says so
rather than answering OK — `group destroyed, but its data was not deleted: …`,
naming the quarantine path, with an error-level log line behind it. Everything
else has genuinely been torn down by that point, so this is not a failed destroy;
it is a destroy the caller must not read as a deletion. Pinned by
`TestDestroyReportsAFailedWorkspaceDeletion`.

### L65. Existing permissive TUI state files retain broad permissions during overwrite (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/persist.go`
- **Finding:** `saveState` passes `0600` to `os.WriteFile`, but that mode is only used when creating a new file. If `tui-state.json` already exists with broader permissions, persistence overwrites it without repairing its mode, leaving saved operator drafts readable to local users or processes allowed by the existing permissions.
- **Preconditions:** Requires local access to the state directory and a pre-existing state file with permissive permissions. It is not remotely exploitable and normally affects shared-user or otherwise misconfigured deployments.
- **Commit:** `13c150a`

**Decision.**

`tui/persist.go`.

**Confirmed.** `O_CREATE` with `0600` sets the mode only when the call *creates*
the file. A `tui-state.json` already at `0644` — copied, restored from a backup,
migrated, or left by an older build — keeps that mode through every later write,
and what is written includes the operator's unsubmitted input bar.

**Fix:** the mode is repaired through the **descriptor**, not the path, so it
cannot be redirected between the open and the chmod, and only when it is actually
too broad. Same reasoning as the daemon's `hardenStatePaths()`: `MkdirAll` and
`OpenFile` never fix an existing mode. Pinned by
`TestStateFilePermissionsAreRepairedOnWrite`.

### L66. Unbounded schedule listing can exhaust daemon resources (LOW, already fixed (M87), residual accepted)

- **Where:** `daemon/schedules.go`
- **Finding:** Authorized callers can request an unfiltered schedule list that copies, sorts, converts, and serializes the entire in-memory schedule store without pagination or an output-size limit.
- **Preconditions:** Requires an authenticated caller with ACL permission to add and list schedules, or compromise of the trusted main sidecar. After authorization, exploitation consists of ordinary schedule-add requests followed by repeated unfiltered list requests and does not require a race or victim interaction.
- **Commit:** `13c150a`

**Decision.**

The two premises the finding rests on are both closed already:

- *"Schedule creation is capped only per group"* — `schedMaxTotal = 2000` bounds
  the store across all groups (audit M87), on exactly this reasoning: the
  per-group cap is evaded by using more group names.
- *"does not impose the inbound control-frame maximum on outbound data"* —
  `fcWriteFrame` refuses a frame over `fcFrameMaxWrite`, so an oversized ctl
  response errors rather than being written.

The residual is a full unfiltered `SchedList`: at most 2000 records of a bounded
cron (`schedCronMax`) and message (`schedMaxMsg`). **Accepted.** The caller must
already hold `sched_add` *and* `sched_list` on the `"*"` target, which is an
operator grant, and the alternative — silently truncating a schedule listing —
trades a bounded allocation for an operator not seeing a schedule that will fire.
That is the worse failure.

### L67. Queue-full recovery can resurrect a stale report window (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/report.go`
- **Finding:** `deliverReport` restores a previously consumed `pendingReport` based only on the absence of a current map entry. If a newer delegation arms and consumes its report window before the older failed delivery recovers, the older window can be resurrected and a later retry can be routed to the superseded main session.
- **Preconditions:** Low.
