and the write truncated. A timestamp is not an identity — UnixNano repeats on a
coarse clock, and the pending-size check is not a uniqueness check — so two
handlers landing on the same value both resolved to the same relative path and
the later write silently replaced the **first** message's attachment. The image
is what the operator is asking the agent about, so delivering the wrong one is
worse than failing. Now 64 bits of `crypto/rand` in the name **and**
`O_CREATE|O_EXCL|O_NOFOLLOW`, which makes it an invariant rather than a
probability. Pinned by `TestAttachmentNamesCannotCollide`.

### L113. Unthrottled main-only tail polling consumes daemon host resources (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/ctl.go`
- **Finding:** A compromised main guest can submit an unlimited sequence of valid `tail` control requests. Each request synchronously opens, stats, seeks, reads up to 256 KiB from a host log, splits the data into lines, and serializes a response, with no request-rate, concurrency, or aggregate-work budget.
- **Preconditions:** Requires code execution or equivalent control inside the main guest, but no additional daemon authentication or operator interaction. The standard guest control forwarder serializes requests, so sustained polling is the primary demonstrated path; direct additional vsock connections may increase concurrency depending on guest restrictions.
- **Commit:** `4cb2837`

**Decision.**

`daemon/ctl.go`. The `tail` verb was gated on main-only and a valid group name,
and on nothing else. `main` is a guest running an agent on
attacker-influenceable input, and every call stats and reads up to 256 KiB,
parses it and builds a response — synchronously on the ctl goroutine, so a poll
loop is free host CPU and IO *and* starves that group's own control plane. Now a
per-group token bucket (`ctlPollAllow`: burst 30, then 2/s) — far above any sane
polling interval for a log tail, far below a loop — and the bucket joins
destroy's name-keyed teardown. Pinned by `TestCtlTailIsRateLimited`.

### L114. Count-only Markdown caching can exhaust operator TUI memory (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/model.go`
- **Finding:** The TUI retains complete response text in Markdown-cache keys and complete rendered output in values, while limiting only the number of entries. An attacker who can generate many distinct, large outputs that an operator views can therefore cause substantial aggregate memory use, CPU work, GC pressure, UI stalls, or termination of the operator's TUI.
- **Preconditions:** An attacker must be able to cause large, distinct output in content visible to an operator's TUI, and the operator must view or hover the affected job or response. Existing output and history limits constrain individual inputs and slow exploitation, but they do not prevent aggregate byte growth across cache entries, transcript records, or parsed peek blocks. No authentication bypass is involved, and the primary effect is confined to the victim's TUI process.
- **Commit:** `4cb2837`

**Decision.**

`tui/model.go`. `mdCache` was evicted on entry count alone, which is not a
budget: the key is a block's source text and the value its rendered form, both
chosen by a guest, a job or a model, and the daemon's per-event cap is 1 MiB —
so 1024 entries could be a gigabyte. `mdCachePut` now bounds both, with a
per-entry cap (`mdEntryMax`, 256 KiB) that is the more useful of the two: a
block nobody can read on one screen is not worth keeping to save one re-render,
and refusing to cache it costs only that. Pinned by
`TestMarkdownCacheIsBoundedByBytes`.

### L115. Concurrent turn completion can hide another in-flight turn from shared activity state (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/activity.go`
- **Finding:** The daemon permits concurrent turns across sessions, but activity lifecycle state is stored once per group. A completing turn clears the shared active flag, session, and phase without verifying ownership or accounting for other active turns, so the activity stream and TUI can report idle or attribute activity to the wrong session while another turn remains in progress.
- **Preconditions:** Low-complexity for an authenticated caller already authorized to send turns in the same group. The caller needs two supported concurrent sessions and suitable completion timing. The resulting effect is limited to transient activity visibility and interrupt fallback behavior; normal turn completion and timeout behavior remain in place.
- **Commit:** `4cb2837`

**Decision.**

`daemon/activity.go`.

**Confirmed.** `activityState` held ONE group-wide `turnActive`/`session`/`base`
triple while `sendNow` runs turns concurrently across sessions (up to
`groupSlots` of them). A later `activityTurnBegin` overwrote the earlier turn's
state and ANY `activityTurnEnd` cleared it — so session B finishing while
session A was still running left the group reading **idle** with A in flight.
The TUI uses activity as its fallback signal for whether a live-only attached
turn is interruptible, so a turn hidden this way loses that fallback.

**Fix:** `turns` is a map of session → guest-side phase. `turnActive` is "the
map is non-empty"; the phase is the most informative across the turns in flight
(the same spirit as the existing counters); and the frame's session is the
single turn's when there is one and **empty** when there are several — the phase
is then a property of the group, and naming one conversation would attribute it
to a turn that may not be causing it. The proxy still cannot attribute an
upstream call to a session (one listener per group), so `end()`'s `work` applies
to every turn in flight, which is what it meant before. Pinned by
`TestConcurrentTurnsKeepTheGroupActive`.

### L116. Provider-controlled streaming response can exhaust sidecar memory (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `sidecar/venice_stream.js`
- **Finding:** The Venice SSE parser has no aggregate limit on provider-controlled response data, assistant text, tool-call arguments, or non-success response bodies. A configured provider that emits a large or unending sequence of otherwise valid SSE events can therefore drive the sidecar's memory and garbage-collection usage until the affected worker is killed or encounters an out-of-memory failure; completed large messages may also be retained in replay history.
- **Preconditions:** Requires the configured provider or an upstream component it trusts to return an unusually large or unending response. A normal authenticated user may reach the path indirectly through a model request, but cannot generally choose the provider response. The resulting denial of service is expected to be confined to the affected group's sidecar VM, making this a low-severity availability issue.
- **Commit:** `4cb2837`

**Decision.**

`sidecar/venice_stream.js`. The worker watchdog bounds elapsed **time**, not
bytes, and the proxy's scanner bounds one relayed **line**, not a request's
total — so a compromised or malfunctioning provider could grow the SSE line
buffer, the retained reply and the accumulated tool-call arguments without limit
inside one turn, and the non-200 path buffered the whole body before slicing 500
bytes out of it. Four bounds added: `SSE_BUF_CAP`, `REPLY_CAP_BYTES`,
`TOOLARG_CAP_BYTES`, and a 4 KiB ceiling while the error body accumulates. The
reply cap is on the **retained** copy (it goes into the history file replayed on
every later turn); streaming to the operator has its own budget and is
unaffected, and the truncation is reported through `[[err]]`.

### L117. Unvalidated guest filesystem counters can corrupt disk telemetry (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/resources.go`
- **Finding:** The guest filesystem parser validates only parse success, positive block size/total, and `freeBlocks <= blocks`. It accepts negative or inconsistent counters and performs unchecked signed `int64` multiplication, allowing malformed guest output to produce wrapped or misleading disk metrics used by alerting and the TUI.
- **Preconditions:** Requires control of a running guest with root-level ability to influence the probe output, or another trusted metric source. There is no demonstrated network-only exploit or host escape; the practical result is corruption, suppression, or distortion of monitoring for that guest. A malicious guest can also submit plausible false values, which limits the security value of validation to rejecting malformed or overflow-inducing inputs.
- **Commit:** `4cb2837`

**Decision.**

`daemon/resources.go`. `resParseGuestFS` turned guest-authored counters into
byte values checking only `freeBlocks > blocks`. Negative counts, available
above the filesystem size, and products that **wrap to a negative byte count**
all reached the cache, the per-group disk alert and the TUI — whose own validity
test asks only for a positive total. Every relationship is checked now and the
multiplication goes through `mulNoOverflow`; `resParseMemInfo` likewise rejects
a shifted value that would wrap and an available above total. This is the
parse-site half of L79's envelope check — a guest that controls the probe can
still submit *plausible* false values, and the ledger for L79 says so. Pinned by
`TestGuestFilesystemCountersAreValidated`.

### L118. Concurrent config materialization can corrupt the shared group configuration (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `fcguest/main.go`
- **Finding:** Concurrent turns can race in `writeWorkerFile` because every write to the group-wide `config.json` uses the same `config.json.tmp` pathname, while write, rename, and cleanup errors are ignored.
- **Preconditions:** A normal user who can start turns in multiple sessions of the same group can trigger the race by submitting concurrent messages. Exploitation is timing-dependent and affects availability or configuration selection within that group; no elevated privileges or external network access are required.
- **Commit:** `4cb2837`

**Decision.**

`fcguest/main.go`.

**Confirmed.** The daemon delivers turns concurrently across sessions and every
delivery materialises the same `config.json`, while `writeWorkerFile` used ONE
shared `<path>.tmp`: the second writer's `os.Remove` unlinked the first's inode
mid-write, its `O_EXCL` open then succeeded on a name the first still had open,
and the two renames raced — so a rename could put a **partially written** file
into place and a concurrent turn read malformed, incomplete or missing
configuration and fell back to defaults with nothing said.

**Fix:** `os.CreateTemp` gives each write its own name (and the mode is restored
to 0644, since CreateTemp makes 0600). The rename onto the final path was always
atomic; what was not atomic was getting there. Pinned by
`TestWriteWorkerFileIsConcurrencySafe` — the timing race itself is narrow, so
the test pins the invariants plus the deterministic consequence: another
writer's temporary file is never destroyed (negative control: the old code
deletes it).

### L119. Unbounded concurrent TUI prewarming can exhaust client resources (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/model.go`
- **Finding:** TUI prewarming snapshots and fully scans the transcript for each job, while resize, history, and navigation paths can launch overlapping jobs without cancellation, coalescing, or a concurrency limit. Large permitted response bodies and repeated resize generations can therefore consume excessive client CPU and heap and make the affected TUI freeze or terminate.
- **Preconditions:** Low-to-moderate. The attacker must be able to cause large permitted event bodies to be displayed by an authorized TUI user, and normally needs that user to attach to, resize, or navigate the client. The practical effect is denial of service of that user's TUI process; no daemon compromise or server-wide outage is demonstrated.
- **Commit:** `4cb2837`

**Decision.**

`tui/model.go`. `startPrewarm` only **counted** jobs — it rejected nothing,
queued nothing and cancelled nothing — so a settled resize fanned out one
renderer per loaded group, each scanning the whole transcript and running
glamour over every response body in it, with history and navigation able to
stack more on top. On a fleet of twenty groups that is twenty concurrent
renderers competing with the update loop they exist to keep free. Bounded now at
`prewarmMaxInFlight` (4) and `prewarmMaxPerGroup` (2), with the CURRENT group
exempt because it is the one on screen and `refreshLog`'s plain-build fallback
is waiting for exactly its prewarm. Over the bound the prewarm is **skipped**,
not queued: that group keeps its stale-width cache and pays a synchronous render
when the operator actually visits it — which is what a group that was never
prewarmed already does. Pinned by `TestPrewarmConcurrencyIsBounded`.

### L120. Repeated same-kind transcript merging causes quadratic TUI work (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/model.go`
- **Finding:** Consecutive ordinary transcript lines are merged with immutable string concatenation. Each append copies the accumulated block, and every appended line invalidates the cached transcript so a sustained stream can repeatedly rebuild the growing block, causing disproportionate CPU and allocation pressure in the operator's TUI.
- **Preconditions:** Low-to-moderate exploitability: the attacker must be able to cause a sustained stream of ordinary output in a group that the victim's TUI is viewing. No unauthenticated network-wide trigger is established, and the effect is confined to the affected local client, but the input shape is straightforward once that prerequisite is met.
- **Commit:** `4cb2837`

**Decision.**

`tui/model.go`. `allBlocks` merged consecutive same-kind lines with `text +=
"\n" + l.text`, which copies the whole accumulated block on **every** line —
quadratic in the block's length. `addLine` bumps the group version per line, so
the viewport cache misses and this rebuild runs again: a sustained stream in the
group on screen re-paid a cost that grows with what it had already sent, and the
16 ms debounce coalesces repaints, not the merge. Collected into a slice and
joined once, which is linear. Pinned by `TestBlockMergeIsLinear` — 8× the lines
must not cost 25× the time; the old code measured **51×**.

### L121. Full send queue can permanently suppress a job completion notification (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/notify.go`
- **Finding:** When the debounced completion notification encounters a full per-session send queue, the timer is deleted and no replacement retry is scheduled. The buffered completion is retried only if another job_done event arrives for the same group/session.
- **Preconditions:** Low. An attacker needs access to a group/session capable of generating turns or otherwise causing its bounded send queue to fill, and must time a background-job completion during that pressure. The effect is scoped to that group/session's completion notification and does not execute code or expose data by itself.
- **Commit:** `4cb2837`

**Decision.**

`daemon/notify.go`. The debounce callback deletes its own timer entry before
calling `flushNotify`, so a queue-full `enqueueSend` left the results buffered
with **nothing scheduled** to retry them — the comment said "will retry on next
job_done", and that was the whole mechanism: the queue draining on its own
caused no second attempt, so a completion could stay unreported indefinitely and
was lost outright on a daemon restart. The arming is now a shared helper and the
failure path re-arms it at `notifyRetryDelay` (30s), under the same generation
fence, so a later `job_done` still supersedes it. Pinned by
`TestNotifyRearmsItselfWhenTheQueueIsFull`.

### L122. Unbounded leading-zero SGR parameters permit TUI render resource exhaustion (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/sgr.go`
- **Finding:** The SGR parser bounds the numeric value but not the length of a numeric field. Arbitrarily long fields containing only zeroes therefore pass validation and are retained as raw SGR text, causing repeated TUI scanning and copying in theme and monochrome rendering paths.
- **Preconditions:** An attacker must be able to cause guest, job, shell, or model output to be displayed by an operator using the TUI. No special terminal capability is required once output influence exists. A single payload is bounded by existing output paths, while repeated output can sustain the rendering overhead and degrade the affected operator interface.
- **Commit:** `4cb2837`

**Decision.**

`tui/sgr.go`, `daemon/sanitize.go`. Both parsers bounded a parameter's **value**
and not its length — and `strconv.Atoi` accepts any number of leading zeros — so
`ESC[0000…0m` was a valid SGR 0 of unlimited size. Both sanitizers keep pure
SGR, so the raw field was copied verbatim into the transcript, the replay ring
and every client, re-scanned and re-emitted at each hop, and finally parsed again
by the operator's terminal: a zero-width payload that costs CPU, allocations and
frame bandwidth all the way along. Four digits is the cap (the largest attribute
is 107; truecolor components reach 255); the **empty** field stays valid, since
ECMA-48's default parameter is 0 and `ESC[m` is the reset every styled span ends
with. Fixed in both, because the two sanitizers are deliberate mirrors. Pinned
by `TestOversizedSGRParametersAreRejected`.

### L124. Non-streaming LLM responses are buffered without a response-size limit (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/proxy.go`
- **Finding:** The Anthropic and Venice relay paths fully read non-event-stream upstream responses into memory before forwarding them, without enforcing a daemon-side response-size budget. A compromised guest can issue concurrent non-streaming inference requests and create bounded-but-uncontrolled response allocations in the host daemon.
- **Preconditions:** A compromised guest can reach its own group's proxy over the guest-to-host relay without needing a separate host credential. The attack requires concurrent permitted inference requests and depends on the provider accepting a large output request, but it is straightforward to repeat within the existing per-group semaphore.
- **Commit:** `8f67b38`

**Decision.**

`daemon/proxy.go`. `proxyMaxBody` caps the request and the inflight semaphores
cap how many, but nothing capped **one response** — and both provider branches
`io.ReadAll` it whole before a byte reaches the guest, with the Venice branch
unmarshalling the same buffer on top. A guest that can use its group's relay
picks non-streaming inference and asks for a large output; 128 global slots
times an unbounded response is the arithmetic M15 already wrote about for
request bodies. `proxyReadResponse` bounds it at the same 64 MiB, relays a
truncated body as-is (the guest sees a malformed reply from its provider, which
is what happened) and logs the reason. Pinned by
`TestNonStreamingResponseIsBounded`.

### L125. Queued guest probes can delay resource threshold evaluation (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/resources.go`
- **Finding:** `resSweepGuestMem` waits for every semaphore-queued guest probe, so the five-second timeout applies per concurrency batch rather than to the complete sweep. With 100 registered groups and concurrency eight, unresponsive guests can block `wg.Wait()` for about 65 seconds before threshold evaluation resumes.
- **Preconditions:** Limited. An ordinary group can influence only its own guest probe; reaching the worst case requires many compromised groups or access to the privileged main control plane. The result is delayed monitoring and alerts rather than direct service takeover or data loss.
- **Commit:** `8f67b38`

**Decision.**

`daemon/resources.go`. The semaphore bounded how many probes run at once and
said nothing about how long the queue behind it takes, while `resourcesLoop`
calls `resCheckThresholds` only after `resSweep` returns. With enough
unresponsive guests — ceil(N / `resGuestExecPar`) × `resGuestExecTimeout` — the
threshold check that exists to catch a filling disk simply was not running,
which is the 2026-08-03 outage's exact shape: the metrics were fine, nobody was
reading them. `resSweepDeadline` (20s) bounds the whole guest leg; a group that
misses its slot is recorded as a **missed reading**, not a stopped VM —
`resGuestRetain` already distinguishes them, and a missed reading is handled
honestly while a delayed alert is not handled anywhere.

### L126. Malformed guest frames can poison egress flow audit records (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/fcnet.go`
- **Finding:** fcParseFlow accepts incomplete or internally inconsistent IPv4 TCP/UDP frames as flow starts. Because fcEgressConn records frames before forwarding them to gVisor, guest-controlled malformed packets can create misleading flow events and occupy the logger's per-destination deduplication entry.
- **Preconditions:** Low-complexity exploitation by code inside a networked guest that can inject raw Ethernet frames. No host authentication or network-policy bypass is needed, but the effect is limited to poisoning egress audit records and their deduplication state.
- **Commit:** `8f67b38`

**Decision.**

`daemon/fcnet.go`. The flow log is an audit record, and it recorded flows from
frames the netstack would reject: `fcParseFlow` checked a 14-byte TCP payload
and a 4-byte UDP payload and nothing about the IP version, the IPv4 total
length, the IPv6 payload length, TCP's 20-byte minimum and data offset, or
UDP's declared length. A false record is bad on its own, and its tuple then
**suppresses a later real flow** to the same destination for the whole dedup
TTL. Every one of those fields is checked now. Pinned by
`TestMalformedFramesDoNotProduceFlowRecords`; the existing `fcnet_test.go`
frame builders were made well-formed, which is what they always meant to be.

### L127. AttachShell permits unbounded persistent tmux-session creation (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `fcguest/main.go`
- **Finding:** An authenticated caller with `attach_shell` permission can create a new persistent guest tmux session for every unique `ShellOpen.session` value. Disconnecting the AttachShell stream removes only the per-connection client; the tmux session and shell remain, allowing resource accumulation in the affected group VM.
- **Preconditions:** Low-to-moderate: exploitation requires a valid mTLS client certificate, bearer token, and an ACL grant for `attach_shell` on the target group, but each authorized stream can submit a distinct name and disconnect after creation. No race or special timing is required.
- **Commit:** `8f67b38`

**Decision.**

`fcguest/main.go`. The daemon pins the session NAME's shape (an earlier audit
fix), but a name is not a quota: `tmux new-session -A -s <name>` is
create-or-attach, and detaching deliberately leaves the session and everything
in it alive — that persistence is the feature — so every previously unused name
is a new shell, a new pty and permanent tmux state. `tmuxSessionAdmit` caps
CREATION at `shellSessionMax` (24 — one per chat session with room to spare)
while always allowing an attach to a session that already exists, which is what
does not accumulate. The refusal names the fix (`tmux kill-session`).

### L128. Destroyed groups leave log tailers and file descriptors running (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/groups.go`
- **Finding:** `destroy` removes a bare group name from the tail registry, while group and slot tailers are registered under full log-path keys. As a result, destroying a group does not remove those registrations or cancel the associated `tailFile` goroutines.
- **Preconditions:** An authenticated principal with permission to subscribe to, activate, and destroy groups can repeat the lifecycle sequence using distinct group names or repeated slot activation. No special payload is required, but sufficient churn can accumulate workers and descriptors. The issue is therefore practical for an authorized client, but not remotely unauthenticated.
- **Commit:** `8f67b38`

**Decision.**

`daemon/logtail.go`.

**The key mismatch the finding describes was already fixed** (M104's
`dropGroupTailState` deletes by PATH). The residual is the real one: `tailFile`
had **no exit at all**. Its claim was dropped and the workspace deleted, and the
goroutine went on `os.Stat`ing a path that no longer exists, forever, holding
its open descriptor — so group churn accumulated a goroutine and a descriptor
per group, and a reused name could not start a replacement tailer, because since
M104 the claim is exactly what gates one.

**Fix:** the claim IS the lifetime. `tailFile` checks `tailClaimed(p)` at the top
of each iteration and returns, closing its file, when the claim is gone — so
`dropGroupTailState` is how a tailer is told to stop rather than merely how it is
forgotten. Pinned by `TestTailerStopsWhenItsClaimIsDropped`.

### L129. Unbounded published-port fan-out enables authorized resource exhaustion (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/config.go`
- **Finding:** An authorized group configurator can persist all 64,512 valid published TCP ports. Group startup or restart then attempts to create one host listener and accept-loop goroutine per port and one guest bridge per port, with no cardinality or resource-budget limit.
- **Preconditions:** Requires an authenticated client identity with permission to configure and activate a group. No race or guest code execution is required: one oversized configuration followed by group startup or restart is sufficient. The practical impact depends on process file-descriptor, listener, goroutine, and guest-vsock limits; those limits may cause partial allocation failure while still exhausting available resources. An oversized persisted configuration can also reproduce the condition during autostart or daemon restart until it is repaired.
- **Commit:** `8f67b38`

**Decision.**

`daemon/config.go`. The list was range-checked and deduped but unbounded in
**count**, and each entry becomes a host listener with its own accept loop plus a
guest-side bridge, serialised into the guest's init request. One valid config
write could hand a group 64,000 of them — spending the daemon's descriptors and
goroutines, and quite possibly stopping that VM from booting. `configMaxPorts =
32`; publishing a few services is what this is for. Pinned by
`TestPublishedPortListIsBounded`.

### L130. History lacks an aggregate byte budget and can burden the operator TUI (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/logtail.go`
- **Finding:** `readHistory` applies the history limit after merging the tails of all group streams, so it has no aggregate response or per-event byte budget. The TUI then creates additional JSON, transcript, formatting, and markdown-cache representations of those bodies. A permitted client or compromised guest can therefore cause a group history view to process tens of megabytes and stall or exhaust the affected operator TUI, subject to existing stream, slot, and rate limits.
- **Preconditions:** Low-to-moderate: requires an authenticated client or compromised guest with access to a permitted group, plus an operator opening or paging that group in the TUI. Existing 4 MiB per-stream, 10-slot, and log-rate limits constrain the maximum and make this a low-severity client-side availability issue.
- **Commit:** `8f67b38`

**Decision.**

`daemon/logtail.go`. The page limit bounds how MANY events; nothing bounded how
big they are, and each can carry up to `eventTextMax` of guest-authored body.
Eleven streams times `historyTailCap` is ~44 MiB that one request marshals, one
client unmarshals, and that client then keeps in its transcript — whose own
limits are a line count and a markdown-cache size, neither of which is this.
`historyBytesMax` (8 MiB) trims from the **oldest** end, like the count, so the
page stays the recent one the reader asked for, and sets `more`, which is exactly
what "there is older content" means.

### L131. Per-slot log ceilings allow aggregate host-storage exhaustion (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/fc.go`
- **Finding:** The guest-output sink enforces a 1 GiB limit independently for each slot log, while a group has ten slots and the shared limiter only controls write rate. An attacker able to generate authorized concurrent turns can therefore accumulate up to roughly 10 GiB per group, with additional growth possible across groups.
- **Preconditions:** Requires authenticated access to a writable group or control of a guest capable of producing valid turn-stream output, plus sustained activity. The per-group limiter makes accumulation gradual-roughly 10 GiB requires several hours at the sustained rate-but there is no lifetime aggregate quota and the pattern can be repeated across groups.
- **Commit:** `8f67b38`

**Decision.**

`daemon/fc.go`. `logSinkAppend` checked only the path it was handed, while a
group's turn output goes to eleven independent streams (`.cs/log` plus
`log.0`…`log.9`) — so the real per-group ceiling was **eleven times** the
per-file one, reachable by ordinary turn output with no file ever exceeding its
own limit. The token bucket delays a burst; it is not a cumulative budget. A
group-wide ceiling (`fcLogGroupMaxBytes`, 2 GiB) is checked alongside the
per-file one, from a per-directory total cached for 5s — a cache miss costs
eleven stats, a hit a map lookup, and the error the cache can make is bounded by
one TTL's writes. The cached total joins destroy's teardown. Pinned by
`TestGroupLogsHaveAnAggregateCeiling`.

### L132. Per-group proxy semaphore channels are never reclaimed (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/proxy.go`
- **Finding:** Destroying a group removes its listener and persistent group data but leaves its entry in the process-global `proxyInflightGroup` map. Every distinct group that reaches the LLM proxy therefore permanently retains a buffered channel.
- **Preconditions:** Low to moderate: exploitation requires an authenticated client whose ACL permits group creation, proxy use, and destruction. Each iteration incurs group and VM/proxy lifecycle overhead, but no application-side bound reclaims the retained entries once created.
- **Commit:** `8f67b38`

**Decision.**

`daemon/proxy.go`. `proxyInflightGroup` was keyed by group name and pruned by
nothing — neither destroy nor `proxyUnlisten` reached it — so churning uniquely
named groups retained a channel and its key per group for the daemon's lifetime,
reachable from the map and therefore not collectable. `proxyForgetGroup` joins
the name-keyed teardown beside L80's and L98's. Safe at destroy time because the
listener is already closed and the VM is down, so nothing can hold a slot.

### L133. Concurrent client provisioning can lose token authorization entries (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/pki.go`
- **Finding:** `pkiClient` performs an unsynchronized read-modify-write of `tokens.json` through the shared `tokens.json.tmp` pathname. Concurrent provisioning processes can therefore overwrite each other's registry updates, leaving a newly minted client token unusable and causing an existing client identity to fail token authentication.
- **Preconditions:** Low. The vulnerable operation is a local CLI/admin provisioning path with no identified remote trigger. A user or process that can run it concurrently can cause lost authorization entries, but equivalent local access also permits direct tampering with the credentials directory.
- **Commit:** `8f67b38`

**Decision.**

`daemon/pki.go`.

**Confirmed.** Each `koto pki client` run read `tokens.json`, added its own entry
and renamed a **fixed** temporary name into place, with no locking. Two runs with
overlapping snapshots left the loser's token hash out of the file authentication
reads — and its `token-<name>` file still existed, so the failure presented as a
working credential the daemon inexplicably rejects. `make pki-client` in a loop,
or two terminals, is all it takes.

**Fix:** the read-modify-write is under an `flock` on `<creds>/.tokens.lock`, and
the temporary file is unique. flock rather than a lockfile-by-rename because the
kernel releases it when the process exits, so an interrupted provisioning run
cannot wedge every later one. Pinned by
`TestConcurrentClientProvisioningKeepsEveryToken` (negative control: the old code
fails with a rename onto a name another run already consumed).

### L134. Authenticated Resources RPC permits unbounded concurrent fleet-wide scans (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/grpc_server.go`
- **Finding:** An authenticated identity granted the global read-only `resources` verb can trigger an expensive synchronous fleet snapshot for every request, without endpoint-specific rate limiting, concurrency bounds, caching/coalescing, or effective cancellation.
- **Preconditions:** Low: exploitation requires a valid allowlisted client certificate/token and an ACL grant for `resources`, although that verb is explicitly available to non-admin monitoring identities. Once authorized, no group-specific target or request parameter is needed; concurrent empty requests independently trigger the fleet-wide path, and client cancellation does not stop the synchronous work after dispatch.
- **Commit:** `9f4aceb`

**Decision.**

`daemon/grpc_server.go`, `daemon/resources.go`. The handler ignored its context
and ran a full scan per call — stat every image, read every config, read `/proc`
for every running VM, consult the sample ring, touch the shared CPU trail — with
no budget of any kind. An authorized monitoring identity could spend the
daemon's CPU and the host's IO just by asking repeatedly, contending with the
collector itself. `resourcesSnapshot` now coalesces within `resSnapTTL` (1s,
shorter than the TUI's own 5s poll and far shorter than the 30s sweep, so no
consumer sees staler data than it already tolerates) with the lock held across
the computation, which makes concurrent callers share one scan rather than each
starting another; the RPC checks `ctx.Err()` first. The cache is keyed on the
state root as well as the clock, because the whole snapshot is derived from it.
Pinned by `TestFleetSnapshotIsCoalesced`.

### L135. Unbounded prompt history can exhaust TUI resources when the picker is opened or filtered (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/view.go`
- **Finding:** Prompt history is capped only by entry count, not by entry size or aggregate bytes. An authorized sender can submit large prompts that are retained by subscribed TUIs; opening or filtering Ctrl+R then performs synchronous full-string copying, normalization, width measurement, lowercasing, and fuzzy matching before display truncation.
- **Preconditions:** Low to moderate: the attacker needs authenticated permission to send prompts to a group and the victim must open or filter prompt history. A single request is transport-bounded, but repeated permitted submissions can fill the 200-entry client history with large strings and trigger synchronous processing.
- **Commit:** `9f4aceb`

**Decision.**

`tui/model.go`. `promptHistoryMax` caps the ring at 200 entries; nothing capped
one prompt, which may be up to the daemon's `sendMsgMax` (1 MiB) — pasted, or
echoed back from a scheduler or ctl fire. Opening the ctrl+R picker snapshots
every entry and renders the visible ones through `strings.ReplaceAll` and
`lipgloss.Width` on the WHOLE string before any display clipping, and a
non-empty filter lowercases and scans all of them, once per keystroke, on the
update loop. `promptEntryMax` (8 KiB) bounds one entry — far longer than any
picker row or inline ghost can render. Pinned by
`TestPromptHistoryEntriesAreBounded`.

### L136. VM exit and startup-failure paths leave per-VM cgroups behind (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/fc.go`
- **Finding:** Per-VM cgroups are created before Firecracker startup, but the startup-failure cleanup and asynchronous process-exit handler do not remove them. An authorized client can repeatedly create distinct groups and terminate their VMs, accumulating stale cgroup objects and directories.
- **Preconditions:** Requires an authenticated client identity with permission to invoke Spawn and access to a functioning Firecracker host. The attacker repeatedly creates distinct valid group names and causes each VM to exit; no path traversal or unauthenticated access is required. The effect is gradual kernel-resource consumption and possible degradation of later cgroup creation or enforcement, not host takeover.
- **Commit:** `9f4aceb`

**Decision.**

`daemon/fc.go`. `fcCgroupCreate` runs before `cmd.Start`, and neither the `fail`
closure nor the `cmd.Wait` reaper removed it — only an explicit
stop/destroy/restart, or a reuse of the same NAME, ever did. So repeated spawn
attempts and crashed VMs accumulated a kernel cgroup and its directory per group
name. Both paths call `fcCgroupRemove` now; the superseded-generation branch
deliberately does not, because the replacement VM is in that cgroup.

### L137. Partial CA state silently replaces the trust anchor and causes an mTLS outage (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/pki.go`
- **Finding:** If `ca.crt` remains but `ca.key` is missing, PKI initialization treats the directory as a fresh installation, writes a new CA over the existing certificate, and leaves existing server and client certificates in place. The daemon then rejects identities issued by the old CA, causing a service-wide mTLS outage.
- **Preconditions:** Low. Triggering requires local write access to the daemon's protected credential directory or an equivalent operational failure that removes only `ca.key`. A remote unauthenticated attacker cannot directly reach this initialization path, but the resulting trust-anchor replacement can deny service to all existing mTLS clients.
- **Commit:** `9f4aceb`

**Decision.**

`daemon/pki.go`, `daemon/setup_steps.go`.

**Confirmed, and this is the worst outcome in the batch.** `pkiEnsureCA` decided
whether to reuse by testing for `ca.key` alone. A `ca.crt` with no key therefore
made it mint a fresh CA and write **both** paths — replacing the trust anchor.
Every client certificate ever issued then fails verification against the new
`ca.crt` on the daemon's next start, and any client still holding the old CA
rejects the new server certificate: a total mTLS outage, from a state the setup
detector (which checked `ca.crt` and not `ca.key`) read as "PKI missing,
initialise it".

**Fix:** refuse. This code cannot tell the two causes apart — a key lost by
accident (the certificate is still the fleet's anchor and the key must be
restored from backup) or a stray certificate on a genuinely new install (move it
aside) — and the operator can, so the message spells out both. The setup
detector lists `ca.key` too, so a half CA is reported as the step that needs
attention rather than passing as complete. Pinned by
`TestHalfCAIsRefusedRatherThanReplaced`.

### L138. Reserved goal-session LLM calls contaminate group activity state (LOW, mostly fixed by L115, residual accepted)

- **Where:** `daemon/proxy.go`
- **Finding:** The send path intentionally excludes reserved goal sessions from interactive activity, but both LLM proxy handlers unconditionally create a group-scoped probe. Goal requests therefore increment and clear the same counters used by interactive turns and can overwrite the phase shown to group subscribers.
- **Preconditions:** Requires access to the daemon's group-goal facility and an active group. A goal-capable caller can generate overlapping or repeated inference requests, but the effect is confined to misleading that group's operator-facing activity stream and does not provide unauthorized data access.
- **Commit:** `9f4aceb`

**Decision.**

The specific harm the finding names — *"can leave the UI reporting work after
the goal request finishes"* — is closed by L115: `llmProbe.end()` sets `work` on
the turns in flight, and a group whose only activity was a goal session has
none, so it resolves to idle rather than parking in `work`.

The residual is that `llmWait`/`llmRecv`/`retryText` are **group-wide** and a
goal's upstream call raises them, so a group with an interactive turn in `work`
and a goal call in flight reports `llm`. **Accepted**: the proxy has one
listener per group and receives no session (the guest reaches it through
`ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`; nothing in the request identifies the conversation), so
attribution is not available at that layer, and the counters have been
group-wide by design since activity.go was written — "several upstream calls in
flight at once, show the most informative" is the documented contract. The
reported phase is also *true of the group*: it really is waiting on the model.
Fixing it properly means carrying a session identifier from the guest into every
proxied request, which is a protocol change well past this finding's weight.

### L139. Host disconnect can permanently stall guest network redial (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `fcguest/net.go`
- **Finding:** The TAP-to-vsock sender loop is not cancelled when the receive goroutine detects EOF, a read error, or an invalid inbound frame. If the guest is idle, the sender remains blocked in tapFile.Read, so netPump never closes the dead connection or reaches dialRetry. After a transient host gateway or daemon disconnect, that VM can remain without L3 networking until outbound TAP traffic wakes the sender or the VM is restarted.
- **Preconditions:** Low. The failure is reliably reproducible after a host-side vsock/gateway teardown while the guest has no outbound TAP traffic, but causing that teardown generally requires host/daemon access or an operational restart rather than an unauthenticated network request.
- **Commit:** `9f4aceb`

**Decision.**

`fcguest/net.go`.

**Confirmed.** The receive goroutine returned on failure without closing the
shared connection or telling the TAP loop, and `tapFile.Read` blocks until the
guest transmits — so after a host-side gateway or daemon restart, a VM waiting
on INBOUND traffic (a published port's server, a TCP retransmit, anything the
agent is waiting to receive) sat with a dead connection and no path to the
redial, indefinitely.

**Fix:** the receive goroutine closes the connection and signals `dead`, and the
TAP read carries a one-second deadline so the pump can notice it. A TAP fd is
pollable, so the deadline is real; an idle link pays one timed-out read per
second. The outer loop then redials, and waits for the receive goroutine to
retire first so the two never overlap on one connection.

### L140. Malformed history is treated as empty and then overwrites the recoverable transcript (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `sidecar/venice_stream.js`
- **Finding:** `loadHistory()` catches all history read and JSON parsing errors and returns an empty array. The next turn then persists that fallback plus the current turn, replacing the previously recoverable transcript after corruption, truncation, or a transient read failure.
- **Preconditions:** Requires an existing workspace/session writer or an interrupted history write. The effect occurs automatically on the next turn without confirmation or a recovery copy, but it is limited to the affected session's durable transcript and grants no additional privileges.
- **Commit:** `9f4aceb`

**Decision.**

`sidecar/venice_stream.js`. The transcript lives in `/workspace`, which the
agent's own bash and file tools can write, and a direct `writeFileSync` is a
plausible source of a half-written file on its own. Treating unreadable as
**empty** and then letting `saveHistory` overwrite it destroyed the only copy of
the conversation — including whatever operational or safety context the turn was
meant to carry — silently, as a side effect of the next turn. Now a malformed
file is renamed to `<history>.corrupt-<ts>` before the turn proceeds and the
failure is said out loud; the turn still starts fresh, because there is nothing
usable to replay. `saveHistory` also writes through a pid-suffixed temporary and
renames, so an interruption leaves the previous transcript intact rather than a
truncated one.

### L141. Tool payloads can grow the TUI job transcript without an aggregate byte limit (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/model.go`
- **Finding:** The TUI bounds parsed job history by entry count, but not by the total size of tool entries. A subagent can emit repeated tool-use records that are retained as large strings, causing excessive local TUI memory use and rendering work.
- **Preconditions:** Requires the attacker to influence a subagent run, for example through prompt-injected repository content or another compromised model/tool stream, and have an operator view the resulting job in the TUI. No server compromise is required, but the effect is confined to the operator's client process and is therefore low severity.
- **Commit:** `9f4aceb`

**Decision.**

`tui/model.go`. `formatTool`'s unknown-tool fallback returned the **complete raw
JSON** — the one branch that hands back arbitrary model-chosen bytes, and
unscrubbed at that — while transcript lines and parsed job-peek entries are
bounded by count rather than size. Scrubbed and capped at `toolSummaryMax`
(2 KiB); this line's job is to say which tool ran with roughly what, and the full
arguments are in the transcript file and the job's own output. Pinned by
`TestUnknownToolSummaryIsBoundedAndScrubbed`.

### L142. Live and queued rows allow raw tabs to corrupt terminal layout (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/view.go`
- **Finding:** The live-stream and queued-prompt renderers pass tab characters directly to wrapping and frame composition, while width calculations treat tabs as zero-width. The terminal expands tabs to tab stops, allowing rows to exceed their declared pane width and disturb the local TUI layout.
- **Preconditions:** Low. The attacker must influence text displayed by a running local TUI, typically through a compromised or prompt-influenced sidecar/model response. A tab-containing local paste also reproduces the behavior but is not a meaningful remote attack. No authentication bypass, data access, code execution, or cursor-control escape injection is demonstrated.
- **Commit:** `9f4aceb`

**Decision.**

`tui/view.go`. `renderLiveLines` and `renderPendingLines` wrapped raw text
without `expandTabs`. A tab measures **zero cells** to both `cellWidth` and
`ansi.StringWidth` while the terminal advances it to the next stop, so
`padCells` pads a row that is already wider than it looks, the physical line
crosses its pane, the terminal wraps it, and the frame scrolls — the exact
failure of 2026-08-29 (commit `5f68d03`), in the two renderers that never got
the fix. The sanitizer keeps tabs deliberately, so this is where they have to be
normalised. Pinned by `TestLiveAndPendingRowsExpandTabs`.

### L143. `clean` can delete jobs during initialization or before completion notification (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `sidecar/cs-job`
- **Finding:** `cs-job clean` does not use the lock held by `run`, and it treats a missing or unreadable status file as non-running. A concurrent cleanup can therefore remove a newly created job directory before `run` finishes initialization and launches the detached command. It can also remove a completed job after the status changes to `done` but before `_notify` reads the result files.
- **Preconditions:** Low to moderate: the attacker needs the ability to execute sidecar commands and must win a narrow scheduling window, but repeated concurrent `clean` and `run` calls make the race practical. The resulting capability is disruption or loss of background-job results, not code execution or data access.
- **Commit:** `9f4aceb`

**Decision.**

`sidecar/cs-job`. Two races, both real:

- The mint takes `$LOCK`; the **clean scan did not**. A directory `mktemp`'d but
  not yet carrying its `status` file read as "not running" and was deleted out
  from under the caller, who then got back a job id whose directory was already
  gone and whose output redirection failed.
- Completion writes `status=done` **before** `_notify` reads `rc` and `out`, so
  a clean in that gap lost the very result the agent asked to be woken with.

**Fix:** `clean` takes the same lock, skips a directory with no status yet (a
mint that has released the lock but is still between files), and skips one
carrying a `notifying` marker — raised by the job before the status flips and
cleared after the callback has read its result, so the window is one `clean` can
see rather than one it can only lose.

### L144. PKI initialization fails to renew expired server certificates (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/pki.go`
- **Finding:** pkiInit treats the mere existence of server.crt as proof that the daemon certificate is usable. It does not parse or validate the certificate, check its expiration or CA relationship, or ensure it matches server.key. Because generated certificates expire after 825 days, rerunning the otherwise idempotent initialization does not recover a normally expired deployment.
- **Preconditions:** Low for a remote attacker because the natural trigger is certificate expiration and the credentials directory is local. It is nevertheless reproducible without malicious filesystem tampering by allowing the generated certificate to expire, after which idempotent initialization fails to restore authenticated daemon connectivity. An operator must use a separate reissue path or manually replace the credentials.
- **Commit:** `643016e`

**Decision.**

`daemon/pki.go`. Generation was skipped whenever `os.Stat(server.crt)`
succeeded, and the setup step's detector agreed — so an **expired** certificate
(they are minted for a year), one that no longer chains to the current CA, or an
unparseable pair survived every `koto setup` and every `koto pki init`
untouched. What the operator gets is a daemon every client refuses, from a setup
run that reported success, with `koto pki server` as a fix nothing told them to
run. `pkiServerCertNeedsReissue` now decides: absent, missing key, unreadable,
expired, inside a 30-day renewal window, not yet valid, or not chaining to this
CA. SANs are deliberately **not** checked — changing them is the operator's
explicit `koto pki server -san …`, and silently changing what the daemon answers
to is a different decision from renewal. Pinned by
`TestExpiredServerCertIsReissued`.

### L145. Container fallback can scan the wrong Git index (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tools/hooks/pre-commit`
- **Finding:** The containerized Betterleaks fallback does not inherit Git's alternate-index or Git-directory environment, so it can inspect the repository's default index instead of the index that `git commit` will actually use.
- **Preconditions:** Low effort for a local contributor or build automation that uses an alternate Git index; no remote attack is needed, but the attacker must already be able to create a commit in the private repository and the local hook can also be bypassed with `--no-verify`. The issue primarily enables accidental or policy-level secret-scanning bypass in the container fallback.
- **Commit:** `643016e`

**Decision.**

`tools/hooks/pre-commit`. The containerized scanner sees only the repo it is
handed: no `GIT_INDEX_FILE`, no `GIT_DIR`, no mount for metadata outside the
worktree. Pointed at an alternate index, git commits from that one while the
scanner inside reads the default — empty or stale — and reports clean. That is
the hook's entire guarantee, lost silently. The container route now **refuses** when
git's git-dir, index or object store resolves outside the directory being
mounted, naming the two ways forward (install betterleaks natively — it inherits
the environment and sees what git sees — or bypass deliberately). Refusing rather
than guessing, because a scan that cannot see what is being committed is worse
than no scan: it reports success.

The test is **where**, not whether — git sets `GIT_INDEX_FILE` for every hook it
runs and the ordinary value is this repo's own index, which the mount does
contain. The first version of this fix refused on the variable being set at all
and blocked an ordinary commit immediately, which is how the distinction got
found. What it catches now: an alternate index, a separate git dir, and a linked
worktree whose real git dir lives in another checkout (where the containerized
scan cannot work at all).

### L146. SIGTERM/SIGINT can bypass VM cleanup during daemon startup (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/daemon.go`
- **Finding:** The daemon begins synchronous and asynchronous VM startup before registering its termination signal handler. A SIGTERM or SIGINT received in that interval follows Go's default termination behavior, bypassing the coordinated shutdown path and potentially leaving guest filesystems uncleanly unmounted or startup-created Firecracker processes untracked.
- **Preconditions:** Requires local ability to start, stop, or signal the daemon, or a service supervisor restart during the short startup window. It is not remotely triggerable through the authenticated control plane. Exploitation can cause lifecycle disruption, filesystem recovery on restart, and possible stale VM/resource state, but does not grant code execution or access to another tenant.
- **Commit:** `643016e`

**Decision.**

`daemon/daemon.go`. `signal.Notify` ran *after* `ensure("main")` and the
autostart sweep, so a termination signal arriving while `fcSpawn` was booting a
VM took the **default disposition**: the process died without setting
`shuttingDown` and without `fcStopAll`, leaving a Firecracker child with no
parent (FC is started with no parent-death signal) and a guest that never got
its sync-and-unmount window — a workspace image needing journal replay. Under
systemd the cgroup teardown kills the child but does not give the guest the
protocol either. The channel is registered before the first boot now; it is
buffered, so a signal arriving early is queued and the handler — which still
starts once the daemon is up — reads it.

### L147. Independent error buckets can exhaust the shared main notification queue (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/logalert.go`
- **Finding:** Error alerts are rate-limited independently by `(subsystem, group)`, but all accepted alerts are queued in the single bounded `main` notification queue. A broad error burst can fill the 32-entry queue and cause later log, resource, or goal notifications to be rejected. Forwarded log alerts ignore the rejection, and their rate-limit token is consumed before delivery succeeds.
- **Preconditions:** Low-to-moderate: an attacker needs an authorized way to induce repeated daemon errors across multiple groups/subsystems, or control a malfunctioning guest fleet. No unauthenticated direct alert-injection path is established, and the effect is limited to suppressing transient operator notifications rather than stopping the daemon itself.
- **Commit:** `643016e`

**Decision.**

`daemon/logalert.go`. Each (subsystem, group) pair gets its own token bucket and
every one of them empties into main's single notification queue, which
`notifyDeliver` drops from once full **without keeping** what did not fit. A
fleet in trouble is exactly when there are many distinct pairs — and what gets
lost behind the crowd is the resource alert, on the same queue, from the subject
that is silent by design until it is catastrophic. A fleet-wide budget (burst 20,
then 1 per 10s) now sits above the per-pair ones: a forwarded log line is a
convenience mirror of something already in the daemon log, so it yields. Pinned
by `TestForwardedAlertsHaveAFleetWideBudget`.

### L148. Deadline-less streaming RPC setup can stall TUI reconnects (LOW, partly already covered, watchdog added)

- **Where:** `tui/daemon.go`
- **Finding:** The TUI opens its long-lived gRPC streams with cancellation-only contexts. If the configured endpoint or an intermediary accepts the connection but stalls TLS, HTTP/2, or RPC establishment, the stream-opening calls can remain blocked indefinitely and prevent the stream-closed messages that reset reconnect bookkeeping.
- **Preconditions:** Low to moderate. The attacker must control the configured endpoint or occupy a network position capable of accepting and stalling the connection. The effect is limited to the affected TUI's availability, but a stalled stream setup can suppress that stream's reconnect path until the process or transport is restarted.
- **Commit:** `643016e`

**Decision.**

Two of the three legs the finding describes are already closed: `grpc.NewClient`
is lazy and non-blocking, transport keepalive (30s ping / 10s timeout,
`PermitWithoutStream`) detects a dead link under a long-lived stream, and both
`streamClosedMsg` and `watchClosedMsg` clear the active flag so a replacement is
not suppressed.

What was not covered: a stream that OPENS and then says nothing. `WatchState`
sends its first frame immediately by contract, so silence there is a stream that
opened and is not working — and the model marks state active when the goroutine
starts. A first-frame watchdog (`streamFirstFrameWait`, 10s) cancels the
context, which makes `Recv` return and takes the ordinary closed-and-reconnect
path. `SubscribeGroup` deliberately gets none: a live-only group stream is
legitimately silent for hours.

### L149. Null tool arguments terminate the isolated Venice worker (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `sidecar/venice_stream.js`
- **Finding:** A valid JSON `null` in a streamed tool-call argument bypasses the syntax-error handler, then `executeToolCall` dereferences `args.command` before `execBash` can validate it. The resulting rejected promise is not caught by the tool loop or top-level worker, so the per-turn Node worker exits instead of returning a tool error.
- **Preconditions:** Low. The triggering value must reach the worker through a model/provider tool-call response; a crafted prompt may influence that response, and a faulty or compromised provider can emit it directly. The resulting availability impact is confined to the isolated worker and current turn, although it can be repeated for later turns.
- **Commit:** `643016e`

**Decision.**

`sidecar/venice_stream.js`. `JSON.parse("null")` **succeeds** and yields `null`
— as do `"42"`, `"\"x\""` and `"[]"` — and `args.command` on null throws a
TypeError. The tool loop awaits `executeToolCall` without catching and the
worker has no top-level rejection handler, so node's default behaviour killed
the process: the whole response lost, for a turn the model can reproduce at
will. The parsed value is now required to be a plain object, and the dispatch is
wrapped — a tool that throws is a failed tool call fed back to the model, not a
dead worker.

### L150. Monochrome post-layout folding expands ZWJ grapheme clusters (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/mono.go`
- **Finding:** In monochrome mode, the TUI wraps text using grapheme-cluster width and then folds each rune independently. A ZWJ emoji sequence can therefore expand from roughly two terminal cells to multiple ASCII replacement cells after wrapping, allowing attacker-controlled output to overflow rows and corrupt the victim's terminal UI.
- **Preconditions:** Low to moderate. The attacker must influence text displayed by a victim TUI running in monochrome mode, such as job, agent-response, or job-output text. The crafted sequence is trivial to generate and can be repeated in otherwise normal output, but exploitation is limited to the affected client-side terminal UI and requires no authentication bypass or host compromise.
- **Commit:** `643016e`

**Decision.**

`tui/mono.go`.

**Confirmed, and it is a frame-integrity bug of the class this project has been
bitten by.** `monoFrame` runs AFTER layout and wrapping, so whatever `foldASCII`
produces must occupy exactly the width the layout already measured — and
`foldRune` measured each rune in isolation. A family emoji (`👨‍👩‍👧`) measures
**two** cells as a cluster and folded to **six** (`??` per component, joiners
dropped), so the row overran its pane and wrapped the terminal. The sanitizer
preserves printable emoji and U+200D deliberately, so this input arrives.

**Fix:** fold by grapheme cluster — the span of a rune plus the joiners,
variation selectors, skin-tone modifiers and combining marks that attach to it —
and size the replacement with `ansi.StringWidth` over the whole span, which is
the same authority the layout used. Pinned by
`TestMonoFoldPreservesClusterWidth`, which asserts folded width equals original
width across ZWJ sequences, variation selectors, skin tones, combining marks and
CJK.

### L151. Unbounded subagent buffers and detached-job output allow tenant-scoped resource exhaustion (LOW, fixed (persisted job output accepted))

- **Where:** `sidecar/stream_filter.js`
- **Finding:** Authenticated users can cause the sidecar to buffer arbitrarily large stream records, thinking text, and partial tool JSON, while detached jobs append unlimited stdout and stderr to persistent workspace files. Read-side limits do not constrain allocation or production, allowing exhaustion of the user's group-scoped memory, CPU, or workspace storage.
- **Preconditions:** An authenticated user with access to a running group can request a subagent/tool operation or detached command that emits oversized or non-terminating output. No special network primitive beyond the normal authenticated capability is required. The fixed concurrent-job cap limits parallelism but not per-job output, retained buffers, or completed-job storage.
- **Commit:** `643016e`

**Decision.**

`sidecar/stream_filter.js`. Neither accumulating buffer had a budget: readline
hands over a whole line before the handler sees it, and `inputBuf` and
`wordsBuf` then grew for the life of a block whose length the model chooses.
`inputBuf` additionally becomes a single `[[tool]]` LINE that the daemon parses,
the transcript stores and every client carries. Bounded at `TOOL_INPUT_MAX`
(64 KiB, with the marker saying the JSON is clipped) and `WORDS_BUF_MAX`
(256 KiB — that buffer exists only to count words at the end, so past the cap
the count is reported as approximate with a `+`).

**The persisted half is accepted.** A detached job's `out` file has no byte quota
of its own, but it is bounded by the workspace image (the `size` preset) and
that filesystem's fullness is exactly what the per-group disk alert watches — the
subject this audit has spent L79 and L117 keeping truthful. The available
alternative, `ulimit -f` in the job's subshell, applies to every file the job
writes and would break a legitimate job producing a large artifact in
`/workspace`, which is a worse trade than the one being closed.

### L152. TUI library loader permits blocking special-file reads and unbounded local allocation (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/scripts.go`
- **Finding:** The shared prompt/script loader passes validated library names to `os.ReadFile` without requiring a regular file or enforcing a size limit. A FIFO, device, or symlink to a streaming source can block the client, while a large or unbounded source can consume excessive client memory.
- **Preconditions:** Requires local or deployment-level ability to place or modify an entry in the configured prompt/script directory, plus an operator invoking that entry. No network access or daemon authentication bypass is demonstrated; the result is a blocked or memory-exhausted TUI client.
- **Commit:** `643016e`

**Decision.**

`tui/scripts.go`. The name policy stops traversal and says nothing about what
the filesystem object IS, and `os.ReadFile` on a FIFO blocks inside `open(2)`
until a writer appears, before any check can run. Both callers are interactive:
`/runscript` reads synchronously while handling the command, `/prompt` reads the
whole file before the RPC goes out. One FIFO under a name an operator is likely
to type freezes the client with no timeout. `readLibraryFile` uses the same
shape as L102 and L3 — `O_NONBLOCK` so the open returns, `O_NOFOLLOW`,
`fstat` on the descriptor, and a 1 MiB ceiling. Pinned by
`TestLibraryLoaderRefusesSpecialFilesAndHugeOnes`.

### L153. Goal enqueue retry ignores cancellation and sleeps after the final failure (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/goals.go`
- **Finding:** When a goal session's send queue is full, `goalEnqueue` performs three unconditional 30-second sleeps, including after the last failed enqueue. During this period the goal driver does not observe updated pause, cancellation, interrupt, or shutdown state, delaying goal automation and operator controls for up to 90 seconds.
- **Preconditions:** Requires control of an agent or sidecar belonging to the affected group; unauthenticated network access is not demonstrated. The attacker can forge in-group `job_done` notifications targeting a reserved session, fill that session's bounded queue, and cause the goal driver to enter the uninterruptible retry path. Impact is limited to goal availability and delayed control actions in that group.
- **Commit:** `643016e`

**Decision.**

`daemon/goals.go`. The retry loop slept unconditionally between attempts — and
after the LAST one, which nobody waits through for a reason — and looked at
nothing in between. An agent in the group can forge `job_done` notifications
into a reserved goal session and keep the queue full, and while that held,
pause, cancel, interrupt and stop could not make the goal driver return: the
operator's `/goals interrupt` did nothing visible until the retry sequence ran
out on its own. `goalStillRunnable` is checked between attempts, and the final
sleep is gone. Pinned by `TestGoalEnqueueGivesUpWhenTheGoalStops`.

### L154. SubscribeLogs can reorder live events across concurrent producers (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/events.go`
- **Finding:** `logDeliver` appends events and snapshots subscribers under `logSubsLock`, but publishes to subscriber channels after releasing the lock. Concurrent producers can therefore append events in one order while their channel sends complete in another order, causing an authorized log consumer to observe a live stream that differs from ring order.
- **Preconditions:** Requires an authenticated client with the `subscribe_logs` ACL grant and favorable scheduling between concurrent daemon log producers. The result is a low-impact integrity and monitoring problem: live event chronology can be wrong, without unauthorized access or direct state modification.
- **Commit:** `ea6e113`

**Decision.**

`daemon/events.go`. The daemon-log path already fans out under its own lock
(M156), so `SubscribeLogs` — the finding's subject — was closed. The **group**
stream was not: `recordEvent` assigned the sequence and copied the subscriber
list under `subsLock`, and the fan-out ran after releasing it, so producer A
could take seq 1, pause, let producer B take seq 2 and deliver it, and only then
deliver its own. The ring then held A,B while the live stream carried B,A — and
a client renders in arrival order. `emitOrderMu` now spans sequencing and
delivery (a separate mutex, because `dropSub` takes `subsLock`); the critical
section is the same non-blocking sends it always was.

### L155. Notification parser decodes oversized fields before enforcing caps (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/logparse.go`
- **Finding:** An authenticated caller who can control output from an ACL-authorized job can make the parsed JobTail path base64-decode notification fields up to the Scanner's per-line limit before the 200/2000-byte decoded caps are applied.
- **Preconditions:** Requires an authenticated client with permission to use JobTail for a running group and the ability to make a job emit attacker-controlled output. The payload is straightforward and can be repeated through one or more parsed-tail streams; no credential bypass is involved.
- **Commit:** `ea6e113`

**Decision.**

`daemon/logparse.go`. `notifyField` base64-decoded and converted to a string
before `notifyTitleMax`/`notifyMsgMax` were applied — so the advertised limits
bounded what was **kept**, not what was **done**, and `JobTail` hands
guest-controlled job output to this parser directly, without the live tailer's
authenticity filtering. `notifyFieldMax` (64 KiB) bounds the ENCODED field
first. Pinned by `TestNotificationFieldsAreBoundedBeforeDecoding`.

### L156. Time-dependent tree hit-test uses a stale row index (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/mouse.go`
- **Finding:** `handleLeftClick` validates a row index against one `treeRows()` snapshot, then dereferences that index in a second snapshot whose contents can change when the wall-clock job linger timeout expires.
- **Preconditions:** Low: no separate attacker capability is required beyond operating the TUI, but exploitation requires a row to expire during the narrow interval around a victim click. The result is a client-side TUI crash or unintended row selection, not server-side code execution or privilege escalation.
- **Commit:** `ea6e113`

**Decision.**

`tui/mouse.go`. `treeRowAt` builds its own rows and `handleLeftClick` builds a
second set, and the tree is time-dependent — a finished job row blinks for ~10s
and then vanishes. If it was the last visible row, `rows[i]` **panics and takes
the TUI down**; if it was an earlier one, the same index names a different
group, session or job and the click selects or attaches to the wrong thing.
`syncPeekToHover` already rechecked fresh bounds for exactly this; the mouse
path now does too. Pinned by `TestStaleTreeIndexDoesNotPanic`.

### L157. Guest job metadata can impose repeated daemon allocation and hashing work (LOW, already fixed (M160, L58))

- **Where:** `daemon/events.go`
- **Finding:** A process running inside a guest group can place a large value in a job's status or return-code file. The guest job-listing script reads these files without bounds, the parser retains the sanitized values, and the daemon copies them into a newly built state-hash buffer on every WatchState tick.
- **Preconditions:** Requires code execution inside a running guest group and a connected WatchState subscriber. No daemon authentication bypass is needed after those conditions hold. A single large status or RC field must fit within the existing guest-agent response limit, which bounds the per-refresh workload but still permits sustained multi-megabyte processing.
- **Commit:** `ea6e113`

**Decision.**

Every field the mirror carries is bounded at the producer: `jobsListScript`
reads status, rc, session and started through `head -c 64` and the command
through `head -c 200` (M160), the record count is capped at `jobsMaxPerGroup`
(L58), and forged directory names are rejected outright (L72). A group's whole
job set is therefore on the order of 100 KB, which is what the 1 Hz state hash
walks. The finding describes the pre-M160 code.

### L158. KOTO_FC_NOJAIL does not bypass the daemon's unconditional user-namespace bootstrap (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/daemon.go`
- **Finding:** Setting `KOTO_FC_NOJAIL=1` disables only the per-VM Firecracker jail, while `daemonMain` still unconditionally calls `usernsEnsure()` before VM launch. On hosts where AppArmor blocks unprivileged user namespaces, the daemon exits during startup before the no-jail setting can take effect.
- **Preconditions:** Requires an operator to deploy or restart the daemon on a host where unprivileged user namespaces are restricted and follow the documented no-jail workaround. It is not directly triggerable by an unauthenticated remote attacker; the deterministic result is that the daemon and its microVM control plane fail to start on that host.
- **Commit:** `ea6e113`

**Decision.**

`daemon/daemon.go`. `usernsEnsure()` ran unconditionally and consulted neither
`fcJailEnabled()` nor the variable — while the failure message it prints, and
`koto setup`'s diagnostics for a restrictive AppArmor policy, both offer
`KOTO_FC_NOJAIL=1` as the way out. It was not one: an operator following the
advice got the identical failure and no microVMs at all. Both users of the
namespace (`fcJailCommand`'s two-entry uid_map, `fcJailFixupPerms`' chown to the
per-VM id) are on the jailed path, so the bootstrap is gated on the same switch,
and the unjailed path now says plainly what it costs. Pinned by
`TestNoJailSkipsTheUsernsBootstrap`.

### L159. Unbounded prompt and suggestion text can exhaust the TUI renderer (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/view.go`
- **Finding:** The TUI disables input length limits and fully materializes and wraps prompt values and suggestion suffixes before applying visible row and width caps. A sufficiently large persisted draft or oversized prompt from an authorized participant in a shared group can therefore cause repeated allocations and CPU work on the Bubble Tea event loop.
- **Preconditions:** Low to moderate: the remote path requires valid authenticated credentials with send access to a group viewed by the target and generally a matching-prefix interaction. The local draft/state-file path requires local write access. The issue can make the affected TUI unresponsive or consume substantial memory and CPU, but no daemon-wide outage or authorization bypass is demonstrated.
- **Commit:** `ea6e113`

**Decision.**

`tui/view.go`. `wrapInput` converted the whole value to rows and
`renderInputLines` built every one of them before windowing to `maxInputRows` —
which is a handful — and both run on **every keystroke**, on the update loop.
The value can be a paste, or a prompt an authorized peer sent into a shared
group that the operator's history then offers as a ghost. `inputWindow` narrows
the layout to `inputRenderMax` (8 KiB) of runes around the cursor, which is the
only part that can be drawn; an ordinary value passes through untouched. The
ghost's source is bounded by L135. Pinned by `TestInputLayoutIsWindowed`.

### L160. Prewarm command races on the shared session map (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/model.go`
- **Finding:** The asynchronous prewarm closure reads `m.session[group]` after `prewarmGroupCmd` returns, while the Bubble Tea update loop can mutate the same map in `setActiveSession`. Since copying `Model` preserves the map reference, this is an unsynchronized concurrent map read/write that can crash the TUI.
- **Preconditions:** Low. The issue is reachable during ordinary local TUI use, but exploitation requires the victim's client to be running and the session-switch/prewarm operations to overlap. It does not expose server-side resources or enable access beyond terminating the local client.
- **Commit:** `ea6e113`

**Decision.**

`tui/model.go`. `prewarmGroupCmd` snapshots every other input but read
`m.session[group]` inside the returned `tea.Cmd`, which bubbletea runs on its
own goroutine — while `Update`, on the main one, may be writing or deleting that
key for a `/session` switch or a destroy. A Go map is not safe for a concurrent
read and write: a race-detector failure at best, a fatal `concurrent map read
and map write` — which kills the TUI — at worst. Snapshotted with the rest.

### L161. Destroyed groups leave unbounded jobsPrimed entries in the TUI (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/model.go`
- **Finding:** Each non-empty job snapshot permanently adds its group name to jobsPrimed, while group-destruction cleanup removes other job-tracking state but not this entry. Repeatedly creating, priming, and destroying uniquely named groups can therefore grow the long-lived TUI's map without bound.
- **Preconditions:** Requires an authenticated principal with sufficient group/job lifecycle permissions and a connected TUI instance. Each cycle requires normal group/job creation and destruction work, so exploitation is not unauthenticated or cheap; however, uniquely named groups allow the retained state to accumulate without a bound over time.
- **Commit:** `ea6e113`

**Decision.**

`tui/model.go`. It was the one per-group entry the job sweep did not drop:
`forgetGroup` removes it, but a group that simply stops being listed (destroyed
by another client, gone from a `WatchState` frame) never goes through
`forgetGroup`. The sweep prunes it now, beside `jobStatusSeen`, `jobDoneAt`,
`peekCache` and `jobsOpen`. Pinned by `TestJobsPrimedIsDroppedWithTheGroup`.

### L162. Guest-writable job metadata can terminate `cs-job peek -new` (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `sidecar/cs-job`
- **Finding:** A same-UID job process can write an invalid arithmetic expression to the shared job's `started` metadata. When `cs-job peek -new` evaluates that value under `set -e`, the arithmetic error terminates the peek operation instead of returning progress or output.
- **Preconditions:** Low effort for a process already executing in the guest with the shared job UID; it needs a known job ID and write access to that job's metadata. The result is a narrowly scoped, low-impact denial of progress inspection, not privilege escalation or data exfiltration.
- **Commit:** `ea6e113`

**Decision.**

`sidecar/cs-job`. `started` is job metadata the worker can write and it went
straight into **shell arithmetic** — a value like `1/0` reaches the evaluator,
the division fails, and under `set -e` the whole peek exits non-zero, so a job
that already holds the guest identity can break progress inspection for every
job it can name. Both `started` and the peek cursor are now read bounded
(`head -c 20`) and validated as plain decimals **before** any arithmetic.

### L163. Unbounded stdin ingestion allows ctl client memory exhaustion (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/ctl_cli.go`
- **Finding:** `koto ctl` materializes an entire stdin stream with `io.ReadAll` before any RPC is made, with no size limit or streaming behavior. A caller that can cause an invocation such as `koto ctl send GROUP -` to consume an unbounded or very large stream can exhaust the ctl process's memory.
- **Preconditions:** Requires local ability to invoke or influence a `koto ctl` process and provide its stdin; no daemon authentication bypass or remote network access is needed. Exploitation is straightforward with a large file or a producer that does not close its pipe, but the primary effect is exhaustion or termination of that client process and host memory pressure, warranting low severity rather than medium or high severity.
- **Commit:** `ea6e113`

**Decision.**

`daemon/ctl_cli.go`. `-` read all of stdin, converted it to a string and trimmed
it — three large copies — before any RPC started, so the daemon's own
message-size limit could not protect this process. Bounded at `ctlStdinMax`
(1 MiB, matching `sendMsgMax`), with a usage-style refusal past it: anything
larger was never going to be sent. Pinned by `TestCtlStdinIsBounded`.

### L164. Unbounded persisted TUI state can exhaust resources before startup (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/persist.go`
- **Finding:** `loadState` reads and JSON-decodes `tui-state.json` without size or structural limits. A local process with write access to the daemon/TUI runtime directory can provide an oversized draft or sessions map, causing excessive allocation and parsing before the TUI becomes usable.
- **Preconditions:** Requires a local process or compromised daemon with write access to the operator's runtime state directory. No authentication bypass or remote network reachability is demonstrated; once the file is written, the next TUI startup or reload deterministically processes it without a size check.
- **Commit:** `ea6e113`

**Decision.**

`tui/persist.go`. The 1 MiB read already bounded the FILE (an earlier audit);
nothing bounded what a megabyte of JSON can describe — unrestricted strings and
a map with no entry limit — and `loadState` runs twice at startup, once for the
theme and once building the model, with every value then copied into the live
model and rendered. Names, the draft and the session-map size are bounded now.
Trimmed rather than refused: this file is a convenience (which group you were
on, a half-typed draft), and a startup that fails because of it would be worse
than one that starts with less of it. Pinned by
`TestPersistedStateContentsAreBounded`.
