### L165. Strict mtime watermark can permanently strand pending attachments (LOW, already fixed (M36))

- **Where:** `daemon/fc.go`
- **Finding:** `fcNewUploads` uses a persisted filesystem modification time as the sole upload cursor and selects only files whose mtime is strictly newer than that cursor. An upload created during a clock rollback or with an mtime tied to the cursor remains on disk but is excluded from every later synchronization scan.
- **Preconditions:** Low. A normal upload request supplies the file contents but does not directly control its mtime; exploitation relies on host clock rollback or a filesystem timestamp tie. When that condition occurs, an authenticated user can cause permanent omission of that attachment from guest synchronization.
- **Commit:** `ea6e113`

**Decision.**

The group-wide mtime watermark is gone: `fcTurnUploads` reads ownership out of
the message text (`[image: .cs/uploads/<name>]`), which is exact and needs no
timestamps. M36 replaced it for three reasons the finding's fourth joins.

### L166. `ctlShell` can terminate while leaving the operator's terminal in raw mode (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/ctl_cli.go`
- **Finding:** After putting the terminal into raw mode, `ctlShell` invokes `ctlClient`, whose missing-token and client-construction failure paths call `os.Exit`. Because `os.Exit` skips deferred functions, the saved termios settings are not restored and the operator's terminal can remain without echo, canonical input, or local signal processing. Default-terminating signals can cause the same cleanup failure.
- **Preconditions:** Requires a local user/operator to run the interactive shell, or a local process/supervisor to terminate that shell. No remote attacker-controlled request is needed; the resulting disruption is confined to that operator's terminal and is readily recoverable with `reset` or a new terminal.
- **Commit:** `ea6e113`

**Decision.**

`daemon/ctl_cli.go`. `ctlClient()` was called AFTER raw mode was entered, and it
calls `ctlFatal` — `os.Exit`, which skips the deferred restore — on a missing
credential or a TLS setup error. The operator is then left with no echo, no line
discipline and Enter arriving as a bare `\r`, recoverable only with a blind
`reset`. Everything that can fail fatally now happens before raw mode. The other
half: only `SIGWINCH` was handled, so a `SIGTERM` or `SIGHUP` — a closed
terminal emulator, a logout, a supervisor stopping the session — took the
default disposition and left the terminal just as raw; both now restore and
exit. `SIGINT` is deliberately not in that set, because raw mode clears `ISIG`
and ctrl-C is a byte for the guest, which is the point of attaching.

### L167. Stream closure leaves stale activity state in the TUI (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/model.go`
- **Finding:** `streamClosedMsg` removes the subscription marker but does not remove the group's live activity entry. If the stream cannot be re-established or no subsequent idle frame reaches the client, the TUI continues treating the group as active indefinitely.
- **Preconditions:** Requires the affected TUI to receive a non-idle activity frame and then lose its group stream without obtaining a later idle frame. This can occur on a denied resubscription or failed replay; ordinary reconnects often self-heal, limiting exploit reliability and impact.
- **Commit:** `ea6e113`

**Decision.**

`tui/model.go`. `applyActivity` clears a phase only on an explicit empty-phase
event — which arrives over the stream that just died. A reconnect usually
delivers one, but a `PermissionDenied` resubscribe stops permanently and a gap
need never produce one, and the stale entry keeps `anyActivity()` and
`isAnimating()` true: the TUI spins a tree dot and repaints the whole frame on
every tick, forever, for a turn that is not running. `streamClosedMsg` clears it.
Pinned by `TestStreamCloseClearsActivity`.

### L168. Fixed `.disabled` destination can be silently overwritten during credential cleanup (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/claude_login.go`
- **Finding:** When the cleanup prompt is accepted on Unix, the active API-key file is renamed to a fixed `.disabled` path without checking whether that destination already exists. `os.Rename` replaces an existing regular-file destination, silently discarding the previously disabled credential.
- **Preconditions:** Low. The caller must already be a local operator able to invoke the login flow and write the credential directory. The issue is most plausibly triggered accidentally by accepting the default prompt after a prior disabled credential exists; it is not a remote or privilege-escalation primitive.
- **Commit:** `ea6e113`

**Decision.**

`daemon/claude_login.go`. The prompt's whole point is that the shadowing key is
**preserved** rather than deleted — it is a secret the operator may hold nowhere
else — and a fixed `.disabled` broke exactly that: disable key A, later activate
key B, accept the default again, and `os.Rename` silently replaces A. That is an
ordinary sequence, and the loss is unrecoverable and unannounced. `authAsidePath`
keeps `.disabled` as the first name (the one the operator has been told about)
and dates the rest. Pinned by `TestSetAsideNeverOverwritesAnEarlierKey`.

### L169. Guest-controlled JobLogs metadata can corrupt framing and output integrity (LOW, already fixed (M160))

- **Where:** `daemon/jobs.go`
- **Finding:** fcJobLogs places guest-writable metadata in a tab/newline-delimited record, then treats the first newline as the metadata boundary and decodes all remaining text as base64. A guest job can inject a newline to make authorized JobLogs requests fail, or inject a syntactically valid first record and base64-compatible continuation to prepend attacker-controlled bytes to the decoded log output.
- **Preconditions:** A process running in a guest job can write its own job metadata files or otherwise arrange attacker-controlled metadata. No host authentication bypass is required, but the attacker must be able to run a job in a group whose logs are read by an authorized client. Exploitation is deterministic and affects the selected job's JobLogs response only.
- **Commit:** `ea6e113`

**Decision.**

`fcJobLogs` reads status, rc, session and started through the same `fld()`
helper the list script uses (`head -c 64 | tr -d '\t\n'`) and squashes the
command's tabs and newlines, so no metadata field can break the record's
framing; the id is validated by `jobIDRE` before it is interpolated, and the
output half is base64 the daemon's own `tail` produced. The finding describes
the pre-M160 code.

### L170. Interactive OAuth subprocess cannot reliably be cancelled with SIGINT (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `daemon/claude_login.go`
- **Finding:** The OAuth login and setup interactive subprocess paths globally ignore SIGINT before launching child processes, run those children without a cancellation context, and reset SIGINT handling afterward instead of restoring the setup context's notification state. A blocked child can therefore survive Ctrl-C, and setup can lose its original SIGINT cancellation behavior after the child exits.
- **Preconditions:** Requires local access to run the setup or login command and a stuck or unresponsive interactive child. No remote attacker, privilege escalation, or cross-user impact is involved. The realistic effect is wedging the operator's command and disrupting setup cancellation, so this is low severity.
- **Commit:** `ea6e113`

**Decision.**

`daemon/setup.go`, `daemon/claude_login.go`. Two problems, one cause:
`signal.Reset(os.Interrupt)` after an interactive child removes **every**
registration for the signal — including `koto setup`'s own context canceller —
so the second Ctrl-C of a session behaved differently from the first: it took
the default disposition and killed the process mid-run instead of cancelling.
And `sc.interactive` used `exec.Command`, so a cancel by any route left the
child running and attached to the operator's terminal.

`withChildInterrupt` now hands the signal to the child and **restores** the
wizard's registration afterwards (`newSetupSignals` makes that registration
re-armable, which `signal.NotifyContext` does not), and interactive children are
bound to `sc.ctx`. Ignoring the signal while the child owns the tty is still
right — a signal that killed this process mid-login would leave a half-written
credentials file — it just has to be undone rather than reset.

### L171. Valid zero MemAvailable readings are discarded and rendered as non-alerting RSS (LOW, fixed)

- **Where:** `tui/wire.go`
- **Finding:** A guest reporting MemTotal > 0 and MemAvailable: 0 is a valid severe memory-pressure state, but both the daemon parser and TUI helper treat zero availability as unknown. The TUI consequently displays a gray RSS high-water fallback instead of a 100% guest-pressure metric.
- **Preconditions:** An attacker needs the ability to run a workload inside an existing VM and consume most of that VM's memory. No network exploit or privilege escalation is required, but the result is confined by the VM's resource boundary and primarily suppresses operator visibility for that VM.
- **Commit:** `ea6e113`

**Decision.**

`daemon/resources.go`, `tui/wire.go`.

**Confirmed, and it is the one that hides the thing the mirror exists for.**
`resParseMemInfo` returned `(0, 0)` when MemAvailable was zero and
`guestMemUsage` independently rejected `GuestMemAvail == 0` — so a guest that had
**actually run out of memory** was treated as having no reading at all, the
pressure path was bypassed, and the display fell back to RSS: which the code
itself calls a high-water mark, renders gray, and never alert-colours. The
operator misses the signal until the guest stalls or is OOM-killed.

**Fix:** the sentinel moves off the value. The parser tracks whether the
MemAvailable LINE was present, so a missing one still yields no reading while a
zero is carried through; `MemTotal` (never legitimately 0 for a running guest)
stays the "is there a reading" test everywhere downstream, and the TUI's check
becomes `GuestMemAvail < 0`. Pinned by `TestZeroMemAvailableIsAReading`,
including that a missing line is still nothing.
